**Monetary Policy after the Great Recession** 

Walter Bagehot noticed once that “John Bull can stand many things, but he cannot stand two per cent.” Well, for several years, he has had to stand interest rates well below that, in some countries even below zero. However, despite this sacrifice, the economic recovery from the Great Recession has been disappointingly weak. This book’s aim is to answer this question. 

The central thesis of the book is that the standard understanding of the monetary transmission mechanism is flawed. That understanding adopts e rroneous assumptions—s uch as, that low interest rates always stimulate economic growth by boosting the credit supply, investment, and consumption— and does not fully take into account several unintended channels of monetary policy, such as risk-t aking, high level of debt, or zombification of the economy. In other words, the effectiveness of monetary policy is limited during economic downturns accompanied by the debt overhang and the balance sheet recession, and generates negative effects, which can make the policy counterproductive. 

The author provides a thorough analysis of the issues related to the interest rates in the conduct of monetary policy, such as the risk-t aking channel of monetary policy, the portfolio- balance channel and the wealth effect, zombie firms in the economy, the misallocation of resources, as well as the neutral interest rate targeting and the difference between the neutral and natural interest rate and the negative interest rate policy. 

The book is written in an accessible and engaging manner and will be a valuable resource for scholars of monetary economics as well as readers i nterested in (unconventional) monetary policy. 

**Arkadiusz Sieroń** received the title of Doctor of Economic Sciences in 2016; and has been an assistant professor at the University of Wrocław since 2017. He is a board member of the Polish Mises Institute of Economic Education and author of several dozen scientific publications (including in such periodicals as the “Prague Economic Papers,” “Quarterly Journal of Austrian Economics,” and “Research in Economics”). He received scholarships at The Ludwig von Mises Institute in America (2014 and 2018 Summer Fellowships), won the 2018 Lawrence W. Fertig Prize for the best paper advancing economic science in the Austrian tradition in 2017, and placed third in the sixth International Vernon Smith Prize for the Advancement of Austrian Economics.

My thanks go also to the participants of the Polish Ludwig von Mises Institute seminars, in which I presented a few chapters of this book, for their critical approach and extraordinarily valuable comments.

## **Glossary** 

- **asset lock- in** — a situation in which assets are locked in to their current uses, which blocks competitors from entering 

- **asymmetric monetary policy** — central banks’ tendency to ease aggressively but tighten gradually 

- **balance sheet recession** — a type of recession in which the private sector is deleveraging, which reduces its spending 

- **Cantillon effect** — a distribution and price effect resulting from uneven changes in the money supply 

- **debt overhang** — a condition in which the debt burden is so large that a debtor cannot take on additional debt to finance future projects 

- **forbearance lending** — a situation in which banks increase or refinance some or all of their loans to borrower firms, even though they regard those firms as unlikely to repay the outstanding loans 

- **inflation premium** — the component of a market interest rate that compensates investors for expected inflation 

- **interest rate channel** — the channel of the monetary transmission mechanism that operates through changes in market interest rates 

- **market interest rate** — the interest rate determined on the market according to the demand for and supply of loanable funds; on the unhampered market, it would be in line with the natural interest rate (but it would also include the risk and inflation premia) 

- **monetary transmission mechanism** — the process through which monetary policy decisions affect the economy 

- **natural interest rate** — an interest rate that reflects the time preference of market participants and allocates resources among the temporally defined stages of production; an equilibrating interest rate consistent with intertemporal consumption preferences and production plans 

- **negative interest rate policy (NIRP)** —a monetary policy maintaining the main policy interest rate below zero 

- **neutral interest rate** — an interest rate consistent with actual output equaling potential output and inflation being stable; an interest rate that is supposed to ensure macroeconomic equilibrium as defined in terms of 

xvi _Glossary_ 

   - full employment and price stability; a benchmark for the policy interest rate at which monetary policy is neither expansionary nor contractionary 

- **neutrality of money** — a lack of any impact of monetary phenomena on real variables 

- **nominal GDP targeting (NGDP-targeting)** —a type of monetary policy which targets the nominal GDP (instead of the inflation rate) 

- **non- neutrality of money** — a notion that money is not neutral, that is, that changes in the money supply have lasting effects on real variables 

- **originary interest rate** — a ratio of the value assigned to want satisfaction in the immediate future to the value assigned to want satisfaction in the remote future; it expresses a value differential that results from time preference 

- **policy interest rates** — interest rates set by the central bank 

- **portfolio-balance channel** — the channel of the monetary transmission mechanism that operates through changing the quantity and mix of financial assets held by the public 

- **quantitative easing** — an unconventional monetary policy of buying financial assets in the market, which increases central bank reserves beyond the level needed to keep short- term interest rates at zero 

- **risk premium (entrepreneurial component)** —the component of a market interest rate that compensates investors for the risk of an investment project 

- **risk- taking channel** — the channel of the monetary transmission mechanism that operates through affecting agents’ perception and pricing of risk 

- **searching for yield** — investing in higher- yielding but riskier assets to boost profits 

- **secular stagnation** — a prolonged period of economic growth that is chronically too weak to realize full employment because of the secular changes in the economy resulting in a persisting shortfall of aggregate demand relative to aggregate supply 

- **signaling channel** — a channel of the monetary transmission mechanism that operates through signaling the central bank’s future actions 

- **time preference** — a preference for present satisfaction over future satisfaction (or a present good over a future good), provided the same satisfaction (or good) is compared across time 

- **total factor productivity (TFP)** —the growth of output that cannot be explained by the growth of the inputs used in production; it measures the efficiency of the inputs that results mainly from technological improvements 

- **ultralow interest rates** — interest rates close to or even below zero 

- **unconventional monetary policy** — a monetary policy used when policy interest rates have reached near zero; a nonstandard policy that includes measures different from lowering policy interest rates within positive territory 

_Glossary_ xvii 

- **wealth effect** — the change in spending caused by the change in perceived wealth due to changes in the prices of owned assets 

- **zero interest rate policy (ZIRP)** —a monetary policy maintaining the main policy interest rates at near zero 

- **zero lower bound (effective lower bound)** —the lower limit that policy interest rates can be cut to; also a macroeconomic problem resulting from the fact that policy interest rates cannot be cut further at that level 

- **zombie firms** — the nonviable companies, including banks, that would typically exit or be forced to restructure in a competitive market but are kept alive by ultralow interest rates, easy monetary policy, and help from the state or creditors 

- **zombification** — the process in which the share of zombie firms is rising across the economy

## **Introduction** 

A long time ago in an economy far, far away, the central problem of the business cycle seemed to be solved. Monetary policy was effective, and macroeconomic volatility was largely reduced. It was marvelous. Everything was so perfect that even economists appeared to be likable. The next depression was never going to come. 

But it came, of course. The outbreak of the crisis took most people by surprise. Goodbye Great Moderation, hello Great Recession. It turned out that preventing downturns was not that easy after all. 

The size of the bust jolted central banks and prompted them to respond aggressively. They slashed interest rates to near zero. It did not help the economy return to its previous growth path. They launched quantitative- easing programs, which did not succor the global economy. The central banks’ response to the crisis could prevent the crisis from deepening, but it could not significantly stimulate the economy. As King (2016, 251–52) notes, “Whatever can be said about the world recovery since the crisis, it has been neither strong, nor sustainable, nor balanced.” Actually, the US postcrisis economic expansion has been not only anemic relative to previous economic expansions but the slowest postwar recovery, as one can see in Figure 0.1. 

This means the weakest recovery took place when monetary (and fiscal) policy was the most aggressive in the history of American peacetime. Why? I wrote the book you are now reading to answer this question. Just as Demandt (1984) listed 210 different theories on why Rome fell, so there are many explanations for the slow recovery from the Great Recession. I argue that we should blame wrong economic theories and monetary policies based on them. 

My book adds to the literature about the limits of monetary policy in general and the effectiveness of unconventional monetary policy in particular. The central thesis is that the standard understanding of the monetary transmission mechanism is flawed. That understanding adopts erroneous assumptions— such as the one that low interest rates always stimulate economic growth by boosting the credit supply, investment, and consumption— and does not fully take into account several unintended channels of monetary policy, such as risk- taking, high level of debt, or zombification of the economy. In other 

2 _Introduction_ 

**==> picture [323 x 125] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 0.1_ US real GDP growth (as percentage change from year ago) from Q1 1948 to Q2 2019. Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019a), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved April 6, 2020. 

words, the effectiveness of monetary policy depends on the economic environment and is limited during economic downturns accompanied by the debt overhang and the balance sheet recession. But it generates negative effects, which can make the policy counterproductive. Easy monetary policy does not supply a free lunch. 

Another key message of my work is that the interest rate is not just a hurdle, the cost of borrowing money, or “the reward for parting with liquidity for a specified period,” as Keynes (1936, 146) believed; rather, it is a crucial variable in the economy that fulfills important signaling and allocation functions. As Brandt (1959, 4) notes, “A freely functioning interest rate is an essential element of an economic system, such as ours, whose purpose is to provide freedom of choice in free markets.” However, when central banks lower interest rates to ultralow level, these functions are weakened, which leads market participants to make costly pricing and allocative errors. 

In a sense, this publication is a continuation of my previous book, _Money, Inflation and Business Cycles_ , which was about the Cantillon effect and the non- neutrality of money. Central banks target neutral interest rates, but in the process they intervene in the market, creating a divergence between market and natural interest rates, which distorts the structure of prices and production, leading to macroeconomic instability. The ultralow interest rates set by central banks in the aftermath of the Great Recession zombified the economy and lengthened the economic recovery, aggravating the problem of slow productivity growth. This means that the neutral rate of interest is not independent of monetary policy. So there is no such thing as a neutral interest rate policy. If central banks are unable to align market interest rates with natural interest rates, they should stop setting interest rates, or they should normalize their level, at least. 

Unlike my previous publication, this book is not focused on a single effect of the increase in the money supply. It adopts a broader scope by analyzing 

_Introduction_ 3 

a few channels of the monetary transmission mechanism and assessing the effects of a few unconventional monetary policies. Although this work deals with monetary economics, it is more devoted to monetary policy. It is less concerned with policy- induced increases in the money supply and more with policy- induced suppression of interest rates. 

I believe that this focus makes this book a useful complement to the previous publication. In times of loose and nonstandard monetary policy, examining the effectiveness of monetary policy and the negative effects of ultralow, including negative, interest rates is of particular importance. This book makes a statement in the critical debate on the working of the monetary transmission mechanism: it shows significant shortcomings in the traditional Keynesian interest rate channel of monetary policy and the related literature. 

I am not the first who points out the negative effects of the monetary policy in general and the ultralow interest rates in particular. The Austrian economists have long emphasized the negative effects of interest rates being set below the natural level by the central banks (Mises [1949] 1998; Rothbard [1969] 2009, Huerta de Soto 2009). However, I have greatly advanced the achievements of the Austrian school by applying its insights to the topics of zombie firms, negative interest rates, neutral interest rates, risk structure, and many others. 

White (2012) lists several unintended consequences of ultraeasy monetary policy, but he discusses them briefly. Taylor (2017) argues that the Great Recession and the weak recovery have been partly due to poor monetary policy, but his article is very short and far from being comprehensive. 

King (2016) and the economists working for the BIS in several papers (e.g., Borio and Zhu 2011; Borio and Zabai 2016; Borio and Hofmann 2017) point out that the easy monetary policy has diminishing positive effects, and that it encourages risk- taking. However, my work is not a mere article that either focuses on a small part of the phenomenon of ultralow interest rates or discuss a few aspects, and only briefly, but a comprehensive study of the ultralow interest rates and their several channels of operation. I am not aware of another book that would so thoroughly and completely analyze the issues related to the interest rates in the conduct of monetary policy (the interest- rate channel of monetary policy, the negative interest rates, the decline in the neutral rate of interest as an explanation of ultralow policy rates, and the links between the ultralow interest rates and the risk- taking, debt, and the zombification of the economy). 

Moreover, King (2016) focuses more on the financial crisis of 2007–8, the radical uncertainty and the credit action of the commercial banks, while I concentrate on the monetary policy after the Great Recession and its negative effects on the signal and allocation functions of the interest rates. 

I would say that my work is most similar to Rzońca (2014), who also thoroughly analyzes the effects of ultralow interest rates. However, he does not explicitly study many issues that my book cover, such as the risk- taking channel of monetary policy, the portfolio- balance channel and the wealth effect, 

## 4 _Introduction_ 

the zombie companies, the neutral interest rate targeting and the difference between the neutral and natural interest rates, and the negative interest rate policy. 

My work is theoretical in nature; however, I review thoroughly the available data and empirical papers. The book should inspire readers interested in the channels of the monetary transmission mechanism, (unconventional) monetary policy, or the role of interest rates, the wealth effect, risk- taking, misallocation of resources, and zombie firms in the economy. 

I start the book with a chapter on the traditional Keynesian interest rate channel. I discuss in it the assumptions behind the postulated impact of interest rates on the real economy and show that they are not necessarily met. Borrowing costs are an important driver of economic activity, but not the only one. Although reducing the discount factor increases net present value (NPV), positive NPV is not a sufficient condition (though it is a necessary one) for undertaking an investment project. This is why the traditional Keynesian interest rate channel does not have the same potency in certain economic environments, such as a balance sheet recession, and why it did not revive the economy after the Great Recession. 

When policy interest rates reached almost zero but the economy did not boom, the Federal Reserve (and other major central banks) implemented quantitative easing. In the second chapter, I treat this particular program as a starting point for analyzing the theoretical justification for quantitative easing in general. If the Keynesian approach, which focuses on the interest rate solely, is not adequate, then maybe the monetarist view helps us to better understand monetary impulses. Indeed, I find that monetarists are right: monetary policy does not become totally powerless when interest rates reach the zero lower bound, as it affects the economy through the relative prices of assets, goods, and services. However, the mainstream presentation of the portfolio- balance channel is not free of defects, as it wrongly relies on the substitutability of assets and the wealth effect, instead of the Cantillon effect, while the wealth effect is dubious: the unconventional monetary actions do affect wealth but only that of some agents, so they boil down to redistributive policy. 

After analyzing the portfolio- balance channel, I go on in the third chapter to examine its basis, the risk- taking channel. Despite its significance and the rising acknowledgment of its existence, risk- taking remains a relatively poorly studied channel of monetary policy. I try to fill this gap, showing how monetary policy affects risk- taking. I show that ultralow interest rates make it much more difficult, if not impossible, to properly assess risk, which distorts the risk structure of the economy. Hence, although risk- taking may help stimulate the economy in the short term, excessive risk- taking can aggravate the business cycle and fuel asset- price booms that endanger financial stability in the long run. 

The fourth chapter shows that easy monetary policy not only can fail to revive economic growth but can actually hamper it because monetary policy 

_Introduction_ 5 

not only affects aggregate demand but also affects resource allocation. The misallocation of resources is emerging as a key explanation of the recent productivity slowdown in some countries. I argue that although zombification of the economy was on the rise before the financial crisis of 2008–9, the continued increase in the share of zombie firms in its aftermath, an important side effect of easy monetary policy, could significantly inhibit creative destruction and slow economic growth. It’s ironic since the super- easy monetary policy was justified by the slow growth that was caused partially by it in the first place. What is important is that ultralow interest rates may cause excessive risk- taking and zombification with sluggish recovery at the same time, as more speculative ventures can crowd out productive real investments. 

The fifth chapter develops further the considerations from the previous chapter. I show that, contrary to common belief, monetary policy is not neutral: it affects real factors, including the neutral rate of interest. Thus, the decline of long- term interest rates might be not a symptom of secular stagnation, as some economists claim, but the unintended effect of the easy monetary policy. The chapter argues that ever- looser monetary policy was not only the consequence of a fall in long- term rates but also its cause. Policy rates do not just passively reflect exogenous forces; they also shape economic conditions. 

It may seem contradictory that in the first chapter I characterize monetary policy as an impotent force that failed to revive the economy, while in Chapter 5 and other chapters I show the counterproductive effects of monetary policy, which suggests it was a powerful but perverted tool. However, the contradiction is only apparent, as the perversion leads to impotence and ineffectiveness. Despite Bastiat (1850 [2007]), economists often neglect the longer- term effects of monetary policy. In other words, the monetary policy of ultralow interest rates cannot spur sustainable economic growth, but it can definitely hamper it; so in this sense it is very potent. 

In the sixth chapter, I analyze negative interest rate policy (NIRP)— perhaps the most striking and controversial of all the unconventional monetary policies introduced in response to the Great Recession. And it might be even more widely implemented when the next crisis hits, so understanding it should be high on the research agenda. This chapter adds its two cents by strengthening the arguments presented in previous chapters. Much as ultralow but positive interest rates spur risk- taking, negative interest rates spur it even more. Much as ultralow but positive interest rates reduce the debtservice burden, negative interest rates reduce it even more, delaying the exit of zombie firms and the efficient reallocation of resources. Much as targeting neutral interest rates, which requires low policy rates and lowers market interest rates below the natural level, may lead to macroeconomic imbalances, negative policy rates may lead to even- larger macroeconomic imbalances. I also analyze the rationale behind NIRP, showing that it is based on a misunderstanding of the interest rate as a purely monetary phenomenon, on an erroneous view of the nature of money, and on the mistaken premise of the 

## 6 _Introduction_ 

zero- bound problem, resulting from focusing on the interest rate channel as the main monetary transmission channel. The chapter is thus a voice against proposals to reduce further the effective lower bound and the level of policy interest rates below zero. 

The last chapter analyzes the link between ultralow interest rates and both the private and the public debt. I show that, contrary to mainstream macroeconomic models, debt matters, as excessive indebtedness decreases financial stability and economic growth. Easy monetary policy does not merely passively react to an increase in indebtedness and easing of fiscal policy but encourages them through ultralow interest rates. I argue that there is a negative feedback loop between easy monetary policy and the debt level, leading to the hysteresis problem of low interest rates and high debt. The easy monetary policy thus promotes the debt- driven model of growth, which is not sustainable in the long run. 

The book concludes by summarizing my analysis, offering theoretical and policy implications, and suggesting directions for further research on monetary theory and monetary policy. 

I finished writing the main content of this book in late 2019. Because of the inversion of the yield curve in the United States, I was anxious about the state of the global economy and decided to write an appendix about central banks’ possible reaction to the next economic crisis (Appendix 1). However, I did not expect another crisis to break out before I handed the book over to the publisher (in April 2020). I decided to not alter the content of either the body of the book or the appendix. Instead, I have inserted an additional appendix that analyzes the lowering of policy rates in response to the COVID-19 crisis (Appendix 2). 

## **1 The interest rate channel; or can one make a horse drink water?** 

Few areas of monetary economics have been studied as extensively as the monetary transmission mechanism and its effectiveness. The way it works and its ability to effect intended changes in national income are the most important questions that can be asked about monetary policy.[1] 

It’s no wonder that these questions are the subject of heated debate among economists and that faith in monetary policy changes over time. In the early days of the Federal Reserve, economists generally believed in the effectiveness of monetary policy. The Great Depression triggered a sharp shift in opinion. People started to believe monetary policy is weaker than fiscal policy. This conviction lasted until the stagflation of the 1970s. The following Great Moderation confirmed economists’ newfound belief in the  effectiveness of monetary policy. 

However, the global financial crisis of 2007–8 and the zero- lowerbound problem that emerged in its aftermath (the Fed lowered its target from 5.25 percent in August 2007 to a range of 0 to 0.25 percent in December 2008) reignited doubts about the potency of monetary policy in general and the so-c alled interest rate channel in particular. As slashing interest rates to ultralow levels did not help induce recovery, the Fed started quantitative easing (QE) in December 2008. The purpose was to increase the supply of broad money by directly increasing the monetary base rather than by influencing the price of bank reserves, as the price was already at the zero lower bound. To put it in other words, central banks wanted, through their asset- purchase programs, to bypass the impaired banking sector and directly affect asset prices and long- term interest rates. 

New research then emerged on the effectiveness and limitations of the interest rate channel and other channels of monetary policy, such as the portfolio- balance channel and risk- taking channel. I analyze the latter two channels in later chapters. Here I argue that the problems with the interest rate channel go beyond the zero lower bound problem. I claim that the channel is based on a few fallacious assumptions that, as the Great Recession clearly demonstrates, do not always hold.[2] 

8 _The interest rate channel_ 

## **The transmission mechanism of monetary policy: the standard view** 

From a purely theoretical point of view, in a fully flexible economy without the Cantillon effect it’s pointless to distinguish separate channels of the monetary transmission mechanism (and to measure their relative importance), because a monetary shock leads to proportional and instantaneous increases in all nominal aggregates. However, in real world, focusing separately on the channels of the transmission mechanism is “a useful way to understand monetary policy” (Loayza and Schmidt- Hebbel 2002, 1). 

Mishkin (1996), who presents an overview of the transmission mechanism, distinguishes the traditional interest rate channel, the credit channel, and the channels operating through asset prices. He includes the following channels in the last category: the exchange rate channel (an increase in the money supply lowers the interest rate and the exchange rate, which spurs net exports and thus output), the Tobin’s- _q_ effect (an increase in the money supply raises the prices of equities and houses, which leads to a higher Tobin’s- _q_ ratio and thus greater investment spending and output), and the wealth effect (an increase in the money supply raises the prices of equities and houses, which leads to more wealth and consequently to a rise in consumption and output). The wealth effect gained importance after the financial crisis of 2007–8. With policy rates at almost zero (or, in some places, even below), central banks directly aimed at influencing the composition of private sector portfolios and the prices of assets to induce— through the portfolio- balance channel— the wealth effect.[3] 

Mishkin (1996) also describes the credit view of the monetary transmission mechanism that emphasizes asymmetric information and two channels related to it: the bank- lending channel and the balance sheet channel. The former operates as follows: expansionary monetary policy increases bank reserves and bank deposits, which leads to a rise in bank loans, causing investment (and consumer) spending to rise. In the latter, expansionary monetary policy results in higher asset prices and increases firms’ net worth, which improves their balance sheet position and reduces the adverse- selection and moral- hazard problems. With firms’ net worth improved and their collaterals worth more, banks are more likely to lend money to borrowers, who can then increase their investment spending and thus their output. 

George et al. (1999), who were, at the time of their writing, members of the Monetary Policy Committee of the Bank of England, present an interesting summary of the monetary transmission mechanism.[4] They note that the Bank of England sets the short- term interest rate at which it engages in money markets. Changes in that interest rate then affect economic activity through market rates, asset prices, exchange rates, and expectations. 

The European Central Bank (n.d.) describes the monetary transmission mechanism similarly: changes in official interest rates affect economic activity through expectations and money market rates, which then affect the supply of money and credit, asset prices, bank rates, and exchange rates. 

_The interest rate channel_ 9 

**==> picture [322 x 237] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 1.1_ Main transmission channels of monetary- policy decisions, according to the European Central Bank. Source: European Central Bank (n. d.). 

The Figure 1.1 provides a schematic illustration of the main channels of monetary-policy decisions. 

Although economists disagree about which channels of monetary transmission are the most important, the consensus view has been that monetary policy is effective because central banks are able to affect aggregate demand either via the interest rate channel or via other channels. 

However, the Great Recession undermined this view: central banks lowered interest rates to ultralow levels and conducted quantitative- easing programs, but this did not significantly stimulate the economy.[5] The US postcrisis economic expansion has been the slowest recovery since World War II. Why? I argue that the standard monetary- transmission- mechanism theory— which has been the basis of monetary policy— is not adequate, as it is based on flawed assumptions. I analyze these flaws in the next two sections. 

## **The interest rate channel** 

The interest rate channel concerns how policy- induced changes in the nominal money stock or changes in the policy interest rate affect real variables such as aggregate output and employment through changes in real interest rates. 

## 10 _The interest rate channel_ 

Supposedly, an increase in the money supply or a cut in short- term nominal policy interest rates pushes real interest rates down,[6] which lowers the cost of capital and stimulates investment spending and thus output. Because the channel lies at the heart of the Keynesian IS- LM model,[7] it is seen by scholars as the traditional, or even main, channel of monetary- policy transmission,[8] and was only later extended by monetarists, who argued that monetary policy affects the economy through not only interest rates but other asset prices (Taylor 1995; Loayza and Schmidt- Hebbel 2002). 

The interest rate channel is based mainly on the following three assumptions[9] : 

- 1  Central banks control long- term real interest rates. 

- 2 Monetary policy directly affects the bank credit. 

- 3  Interest rates determine the demand for loans and aggregate spending. 

Let’s analyze these assumptions. 

## _**“Central banks control long- term interest rates”**_ 

First, it is true that the changes in central banks’ policy interest rates affect other interest rates, including long- term yields and rates banks set.[10] However, other factors influence market rates besides official interest rates— especially in the case of long- term rates— such as the risk premium and inflation expectations. As Boivin et al. (2010, 6) note, “The entire expected path of interest rates, not solely the current value, influences asset prices and spending.” 

Indeed, as one can see in Figure 1.2, after the bankruptcy of Lehman Brothers, US bank lending rates and long- term rates increased significantly, which tightened financial conditions and worked against Fed policy (Papadia and Välimäki 2018). White (2012, 7) explains it as follows: 

Due to the potentially interacting effects of rising term and credit spreads, long rates might fall less than normally (or indeed might even rise) in response to lower policy rates. This phenomenon has already been witnessed in a number of peripheral countries in the eurozone area. 

The fact that long-t erm yields in such countries in the aftermath of the global financial crisis rose even though the European Central Bank (ECB) eased its monetary policy at that time is one example of the loose link between shortterm policy rates and long-t erm yields. Another is the famous Greenspan conundrum: as one can see in the Figure 1.2, US long- term yields declined by about sixty basis points between June 2004 and February 2005 while the Fed increased its policy interest rates by 150 basis points (Backus and Wright 2007). 

The relationship between the federal funds rate and long- term real yields is generally far from rigid. As the Figure 1.3 shows, the spread between t en- year inflation- indexed Treasuries and the effective federal funds rate can be quite 

_The interest rate channel_ 11

_Figure 1.3_ Spread between ten- year inflation- indexed Treasuries and effective federal funds rate (percentage) from January 2003 to January 2019. Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019b) and Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019c), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved September 15, 2019. 

volatile, which implies that changes in the policy rate do not always cause changes in the same direction and proportion in long- term real rates and that other factors must be affecting the latter. 

In other words, longer- term rates seem to play a more important role than shorter- term rates in affecting households’ and companies’ spending decisions, but central banks directly control only the short end of the yield curve. Hence, the impact of central banks’ actions on long- term interest rates is far from clear. Some researchers argue that there is no conclusive evidence on the role of the Fed versus market forces on the long- term path of market interest rates (McCloskey 2000; Fama 2013; Hummel 2017). 

## 12 _The interest rate channel_ 

The Great Recession is a perfect example of the breakdown of the interest rate channel. According to Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 3), monetary control before the Great Recession relied on three assumptions: 

first, the ability of the central bank to closely control a short- term market rate; second, a fairly stable relationship between that short- term rate and longer/riskier interest rates that are more important for the real economy; third, the possibility of reducing, in all cases, interest rates as much as needed. 

However, the Great Recession invalidated all of the assumptions. As Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 110) noted, 

Control over the short- term interest rate became imprecise in the first phase of the Great Recession, as the banks’ demand for liquidity could no longer be properly estimated and offset by central bank supply. In addition, the relationship between the short- term risk- free rate controlled by the central bank and those rates directly relevant for the economy underwent two episodes of extreme uncertainty and volatility. The first episode took place in the American phase of the crisis, after the bankruptcy of Lehman Brothers and the massive bailout of AIG, in the autumn of 2008. The second episode came in the European phase of the crisis. Finally, the ability to reduce interest rates was severely impaired during the most advanced phase of the crisis, when the interest rate had already reached its lower bound, or moved close to it. 

The breakdown of the traditional interest rate channel pushed central banks to implement quantitative easing. Although Fama (2013) and Hummel (2017) dissent, the consensus is that the Federal Reserve’s asset purchases have been effective at lowering longer- term interest rates (Gagnon et al. 2011; Krishnamurthy and Vissing- Jorgensen 2011). However, while studies have found that the first round of asset purchases had large effects, the subsequent rounds generated somewhat smaller effects. 

Moreover, there is still no consensus about _why_ the QE programs were effective at reducing long- term interest rates (if they were, in fact, effective). As Bernanke (2014) famously said, “The problem with QE is it works in practice, but it doesn’t work in theory.” The literature focuses on two main possible channels: the supply- induced portfolio- balance channel and the signaling channel (Bean et al. 2015). I elaborate on the former in Chapter 2, but I note here that the working of that channel relies on the assumption of imperfect substitutability across asset maturities, which is not without problems. Meanwhile, the signaling channel relies on the expectational hypothesis of the term structure of interest rates, according to which long- term rates are driven by the expected future path of short- term interest rates. However, there is little evidence for this hypothesis (Thornton 2005). 

_The interest rate channel_ 13 

When it comes to banks’ interest rates, banks do not necessarily have to pass on cost savings or higher costs to their customers. During monetary tightening, commercial banks can be reluctant to increase borrowing costs out of fear that borrowers will go to their competitors. Conversely, during monetary easing, commercial banks may be reluctant to decrease rates on loans, out of fear of attracting too risky borrowers, and on deposits, out of fear of losing their depositors. An example might be European banks’ reluctance to pass on to their retail depositors the negative monetary- policy rates the ECB introduced in 2014 (Madaschi and Nuevo 2017). 

## _**“Monetary policy directly affects bank credit”**_ 

As for the second assumption, it’s true that a change in the interest rates set by commercial banks (or set in the money or capital market) affects the supply of bank reserves and loans. But although changes in policy rates can affect banks’ marginal costs for obtaining external finance (bank reserves), many other factors influence banks’ profitability and behavior. As Genay and Podjasek (2014, para. 11) note, “Interest rate changes generally have small effects on bank profits, but changes in economic conditions do matter relatively much more.” 

Indeed, banks are constrained by the value of their assets and capital, the risk they take, and the profitability of their actions. They have to take into account, for example, the creditworthiness of borrowers or the expected value of collaterals. Indeed, if the value of the collaterals and the banks’ equity fall, while the risk and uncertainty increase, and the wholesale lending market freezes, banks will not grant loans despite cuts in the policy interest rate. Banks are interested not only in the quantity of their assets but also in their quality.[11] They also depend on their customers’ demand for loans, which explains why commercial banks often tend to replace loans to the private sector with Treasuries during downturns, when the demand for loans falls. All these factors explain why the unconventional monetary policy implemented in a response to the Great Recession has not boosted significantly banks’ credit action. 

Some scholars express the erroneous opinion that changes in the _quantity_ (as distinct from the price) of bank reserves influence banks’ credit creation. For example, Beivin et al. (2010, 18) write that “expansionary monetary policy, which increases bank reserves and bank deposits, increases the quantity of bank loans available.” However, commercial banks cannot lend out bank reserves, so the increase in supply of bank reserves does not necessarily translate into boosted credit creation. According to Disyatat (2008, 15), 

Rather than being directly amenable to central bank action, the demand for deposits are driven more by the level of economic activity and the public’s relative preference for bank deposits versus other forms of assets. A striking example of the inability of central banks to affect deposits 

## 14 _The interest rate channel_ 

directly is the Bank of Japan’s ‘quantitative easing’ experience during 2001–6. Despite significant expansions in reserve balances, deposits in the Japanese banking system did not increase absent a pickup in economic activity and the money multiplier fell steadily. 

Indeed, the link between bank reserves and loans is the reverse of that stipulated by the money- multiplier model still described in many economic textbooks (McLeay et al. 2014).[12] Therefore, the standard view of the monetary transmission mechanism should be modified as central banks’ control over credit and the money supply is much weaker than in the standard view. Central banks do not determine the money supply through the money multiplier; they provide reserves on demand, responding to impulses from the economy and the banking sector. This means that the money supply is determined mainly by commercial banks and that monetary policy is not as exogenous and active as the textbooks often suggest. 

Thus, the bank- lending channel works differently than the textbooks present it. The standard view assumes that binding reserve requirements limit banks’ issuance of demand deposits to their access to reserves. But, as Disyatat (2008, 15) explains, 

This is premised on the notion that central banks set the level of reserves as the operational target of policy and that banks’ deposit base, and thus their supply of loanable funds, is linked directly to variations in reserves through the money multiplier mechanism. In fact, the true causal relationship actually runs in exactly the opposite direction. The banking system creates deposits as they are demanded by the private sector, and the central bank’s main liquidity management task is to ensure a sufficient supply of balances for the system as a whole to maintain reserve requirements, if any, associated with those deposits. It is the amount of deposits that the banking sector can attract that determines the level of reserves not the other way around. 

To put it differently, the mainstream monetary transmission mechanism misunderstands the role of commercial banks. They are often seen as mere financial intermediaries, but they actually create credit and money; they generate deposits in the act of lending. The erroneous view of the nature of banks underestimates the effects of commercial banks’ actions on the real economy (Jakab and Kumhof 2015). The standard macroeconomic models should include a role for the financial sector in general and commercial banks in particular. It’s striking that despite all the evidence that credit booms lead to financial crises (Borio and Lowe 2002; Jordà et al. 2012), until the financial crisis the models used by the central banks in conducting monetary policy rarely included financial institutions, as they were considered mere intermediaries (Adrian and Shin 2010). 

_The interest rate channel_ 15 

The problem is that banks’ portfolio behavior plays a small role in mainstream monetary theory. As Chick (1973, 87) notes, “Banks are caught between exogenous cash and fixed reserve ratios and public preferences.” In the real world, commercial banks react differently to changes in policy interest rates in different economic conditions. 

## _**“Interest rates determine the demand for loans and aggregate spending”**_ 

The third assumption is that changes in interest rates set by commercial banks (or set in the money or capital market) affect the demand for loans and aggregate spending by changing borrowing costs. Surely, the cost of capital is an important determinant of investment. 

However, there is no simple link from changes in the interest rates to the cost of capital, especially for large and multinational firms with access to international capital markets (George et al. 1999).[13] Or, in other words, there are many other factors affecting individuals’ spending decisions and companies’ investment decisions— and they could be more important relative to the interest rate. 

Indeed, scholars have had great difficulty in finding a negative link between the interest rates and the GDP growth (Lee and Werner 2018) and in finding a quantitatively important effect of the cost- of- capital channel (Bernanke and Gertler 1995).[14] It should not be surprising, because retained earnings are an important source of funding. It is a well- known fact that many firms rely largely on internal funds to finance capital expenditures (Samuel 1996; Roche 2016). 

Thus, according to some economists, the impact of interest rates on spending is weaker than often thought.[15] This is because companies, when making investment decisions, are mainly guided by their expectations about future demand and the course of the economy, the expected rate of return,[16] and perceived uncertainty (Beivin et al. 2010; Sharpe and Suarez 2015).[17] As Baddeley (2003, 115) notes, “real- world firms use hurdle rates of return generally three to four times the cost of capital; even allowing for differences in risk, hurdle rates are generally high relative to the unadjusted cost of capital.” Similarly, Lane and Rosewall (2015) note that Australian firms set the hurdle rate significantly above the cost of capital to account for uncertainty about their cash flow projections, making investments not very sensitive to the cost of capital.[18] Meier and Tarhan (2007) and Jagannathan et al. (2011) reach similar results for companies in the United States, and Brunzell et al. (2013) for Nordic firms. Also Aastveit et al. (2013) find that the effectiveness of monetary policy is lower when uncertainty is high. Actually, several decades ago Shackle (1946) showed that “allowance for doubt” reduces the power of changes in interest rates to affect investment. 

As Chick (1973) notes, firms undertake investment either to reduce the cost of producing the current volume of output or to expand capacity to meet 

## 16 _The interest rate channel_ 

rising demand. Therefore, monetary policy and lower interest rates will not stimulate investment if firms don’t want to invest in the first place because, for example, they do not expect demand for their output to increase. Moreover, the unconventional monetary policies undertaken by central banks may depress confidence because they may be interpreted as indicating that central banks have little confidence in the health of the economy. 

In other words, if private investment is not elastic, the decline in interest rates will not stimulate much investment.[19] As Blanchard and Fischer (1989, 478) note, 

a recurrent theme in the literature and among market participants is that the interest rate alone does not adequately reflect the links between financial markets and the rest of the economy. Rather, it is argued, the availability of credit and the quality of balance sheets are important determinants of the rate of investment. 

Moreover, the standard view of the interest rate channel of the monetary transmission mechanism does not take into account changes in credit expansion over time. Currently, most loans are not commercial or industrial loans to nonfinancial companies, but mortgages for households. Hence, in many developed economies the majority of credit supports either consumption smoothing across the life cycle or leveraged asset play investments in alreadyexisting assets (Turner 2010), which weakens the link between interest rates and investment.[20] 

Actually, lower interest rates may discourage investment in real assets because at very low interest rates (when associated with greater uncertainty), it may make more sense to invest in existing assets such as property or financial instruments (or to conduct stock buybacks[21] ) than to invest in uncertain new projects. 

The key is that many factors besides interest rates determine demand for loans. This applies not only to companies but to households as well. One factor is households’ balance sheet position. After the onset of the financial crisis in 2007, house prices plunged, which pushed many households underwater. Hence, households lacked the appropriate collateral and were unable to get new loans (Koo 2013).[22] The private debt overhang explains why the ultralow interest rates could not spur the demand for loans, reducing the effectiveness of monetary policy (Jordà et al. 2012; Alpanda and Zubairy 2019).[23] Households with balance sheets underwater are simply not interested in increasing their borrowings at any interest rate, as they are trying to repair their balance sheets and deleverage. 

Hence, the weak domestic investment during periods of low interest rates may be the result of previous malinvestment and necessary subsequent adjustments. This suggests that monetary policy in general and low interest rates in particular work differently during normal times than during crises.[24] Indeed, active monetary policy fails during a balance sheet recession, in which 

_The interest rate channel_ 17
