_Figure 1.4_ The St. Louis adjusted US monetary base and commercial banks’ loans and leases from December 2007 to December 2019 (July 2008 = 100). Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019d, 2019z), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved December 31, 2019. 

the private sector deleverages and refuses to take more credit (Machaj 2017). Companies’ and households’ minimization of debt thus nullifies the effectiveness of the monetary policy. Hence, it is not true that greater liquidity always boosts the economy (Koo 2013). The Great Depression and Japan’s Lost Decade are perfect examples of central banks’ limited potency during periods of over-indebtedness. And, as Figure 1.4 shows, the massive increase in the US monetary base failed to increase credit to the private sector in the aftermath of the Great Recession as well. 

Moreover, it turns out that reducing interest rates in certain circumstances can actually boost savings, not consumer spending, through the income effect. As long ago as in 1970, Weber (1970) found that the income effect of a change in the rate of interest is greater than the substitution effect. The increase in the rate of interest enable consumers to save less and consume more today, maintaining the same level of consumption in the future. 

Similarly, the decreases in the interest rates could decrease the aggregate consumption. Since the rate of return on financial instruments is so low in times of ultralow interest rates, people may try to increase their savings to compensate for the low returns, especially when they prepare for retirement or if they interpret the reduction of interest rates as a sign of a gloomy and more volatile economic outlook. In such cases, low– interest rate policies may lead to a contractionary effect in consumer spending by encouraging saving (Aizenman et al. 2016). 

As Borio and Zabai (2016, 25n22) note, 

The macroeconomic models used nowadays simply assume that lower (real) interest rates always raise consumption by making consumption today more attractive relative to consumption tomorrow (the ‘intertemporal 

18 _The interest rate channel_ 

substitution effect’). They rule out the possibility of depressing consumption as agents need to save more in order to get a given income in the future (the ‘income effect’). 

## **Critique of the interest rate channel** 

There are important overlooked problems with the interest rate channel. The first and main problem with it is not the zero lower bound, but the fact that the channel breaks down when the banking system is impaired and homeowners are underwater (Sellon 2003). 

Second, the interest rate channel is based on intertemporal allocation— that is, on shifting consumption over time. Lower interest rates encourage higher consumption today, but that necessarily means lower consumption tomorrow. As the future unlimited, one might think that’s not a problem. Tomorrow we will borrow from the day after tomorrow, and so on ad infinitum. But, as Mervyn King said in an interview with Gaetano (2016), 

When you transfer spending from the future to the present, you dig a hole— time passes, and the future becomes today. So, now you cut interest rates again to bring even more spending forward, and that digs an even deeper hole. As time passes, that too becomes the present, and if you haven’t tackled the underlying problem, you create more and more of an incentive for central banks to cut rates further.[25] 

Third, even though the interest rate is a crucial price in the economy, changes in it are not enough to increase investment.[26] An increase in savings is needed; entrepreneurs need more funds. Mere cuts in interest rates do not necessarily increase funds, and (as noted above), they may actually lower them, if the income effect outweighs the substitution effect. Allocating resources across time is different from deciding whether to undertake a new investment project.[27] Although the interest rate channel considers the process of granting loans as a crucial link connecting the monetary side of the economy with the expenses of enterprises and households, it misunderstands how credit creation really works. The popular assumption is that when the central bank reduces the policy rate, it lowers the cost of reserves, which spurs credit creation and increases investment. In reality, however, commercial banks create money via granting loans (or buying assets), which reduces interest rates, and they seek reserves only later. Moreover, given central banks’ reaction function, changes in the policy rate are, at least in part, endogenous. 

The fourth problem is that the interest rate channel lacks microeconomic foundations. The increase in the money supply shifts the LM curve, which lowers the interest rate, but Cantillon ([1755] 1959) shows that the impact of the increase in the money supply on the interest rate depends on how the new money enters the economy. The decrease in the interest rate in turn implies that we move along the IS curve to the right, but the channel doesn’t 

_The interest rate channel_ 19 

show how this exactly happens. Put differently, the interest rate channel is not grounded in price theory. It narrows the range of asset prices through which the central banks can affect the economy to just one (Meltzer 1995). But changes in that single interest rate cannot represent all relative price adjustments resulting from monetary policy (Meltzer 2001). 

In a monetarist approach, monetary impulses to the real sector work through relative prices or relative yields, which makes the whole analysis better grounded in economic theory and closer to reality. That approach is based on the portfolio- balance channel, which emphasizes price adjustment and economic entities’ rational pursuit of an optimal asset structure. As Brunner (1968, 15) notes, contrary to the Keynesian view, 

the transmission of monetary impulses is not dominated by the relative importance of borrowing costs. In the process, marginal costs of liability extension interact with marginal returns from acquisitions of financial and real assets. But interest rates on financial assets not only affect the marginal cost of liability extension, but also influence the substitution between financial and real assets. This substitution modifies prices of real assets relative to their supply prices and forms a crucial linkage of the monetary mechanisms; this linkage is usually omitted in standard income-expenditure analysis. 

Fifth, the interest rate channel places too much emphasis on the interest rate as a cost of investment and too little on it as an inducement to invest. It adopts the viewpoint of borrowers. However, as Williams (1942, 241) notes, “there is also the viewpoint of the lender. When the interest rate falls very low there may be inadequate inducement to invest out of income, or even to keep capital invested.” According to him, when interest rates are very low, the interest return from investment may not be high enough to overcome high liquidity preference. From that perspective, all the discussion about how to overcome the zero lower bound to stimulate the economy or about the introduction of negative interest rates is misplaced. 

Moreover, central banks fail to recognize the negative effects of too much debt. It is true that lower interest rates make debt cheaper, which can spur some investment dependent on debt financing. However, that channel has limits. As Reinhart and Rogoff (2010) show, economic growth slows when the debt level is high. Keynesian economists focus on the liquidity trap, but perhaps we should worry more about the debt trap (as I argue in Chapter 7). 

The sixth problem is that there is not one interest rate but many.  Economists do not always specify which interest rate they consider crucial for affecting aggregate spending. One implication of the existence of multiple interest rates is that monetary policy may affect the structure of interest rates, and thus the allocation of capital, which the literature about the interest rate channel does not properly acknowledge. 

## 20 _The interest rate channel_ 

Seventh, the interest rate channel assumes rigidities in the economy. Without sticky prices, a policy change in nominal interest rates would leave the real interest rate unaffected, and the policy would be ineffective. However, prices are not inherently sticky. Rather, the diffusion of money in the economy takes time. In other words, because new money spreads through the economy sequentially, prices rise only gradually. 

Last but not least, like the Austrian school’s transmission mechanism, the mainstream interest rate channel correctly sees the interest rate as the crucial variable that determines the intertemporal allocation of resources.[28] But it focuses on large aggregates without noticing the role of the capital structure and distortions in it caused by the lowered interest rate. In other words, the interest rate channel wrongly considers the interest rate a mere hurdle that prevents investors from increasing investment. Consequently, by focusing exclusively on the volume of investment, it fails to take the intertemporal allocation of resources fully into account. It regards the interest rate as a contract rate on loans, while for Austrian economists the interest rate is the price spread between stages of production and regulates the time dimension of investment (Rothbard [1962] 2009). It is not just an obstacle; it tells investors whether consumers prefer short- term or long- term projects. 

These distinct conceptions of interest rates lead to different opinions on monetary policy. According to the mainstream interest rate channel, the interest rate is just a hurdle[29] ; so, to stimulate the economy, the central bank should (artificially) lower the interest rate.[30] But for the Austrian school, the artificially low interest rate leads to intertemporal misallocation of resources. So even when the mainstream interest rate channel does not function, the Austrian interest rate channel still does. Even when the lower interest rate does not stimulate the investment and the economy in aggregate, it still affects the composition of investment, distorting the time dimension of investment; hence, we need to stop considering interest rates as merely the cost of capital and start to consider the indirect effects of changes in their structure on the economy. 

Mainstream economists also erroneously treat the interest rate as the price of money. They treat the demand for money and the price level as functions of the interest rate. In such an approach, given adequate interest elasticity, monetary policy has a significant effect on interest rates. However, the interest rate is the price of credit, not money. Hence, monetary policy can affect the interest rate only when it alters the supply of credit, not money— or, when the newly injected money enters the economy via the credit channel, in line with Cantillon’s ([1755] 1959) pioneering description. 

Given all these problems, one might ask why the interest rate channel remains the pillar of modern monetary economics. I have two answers. First, it simplifies the role of central banks. If monetary policy affects the real economy mainly through the interest rate channel, then the so- called art of central banking is to pick the “right” federal funds rate— the rate that wards 

_The interest rate channel_ 21 

off both unemployment and inflation (Garrison 2006, 1). As Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 189) note, 

Standard monetary policy influences economic activity and eventually prices by altering the financing costs in the economy. Under normal conditions, the central bank steers the risk- free short- term market rate of interest to a level consistent with its objective. The monetary policy stance is more or less completely described by the current and expected future levels of the policy rate. 

Second, even if low interest rates do not stimulate the economy (contrary to what is often assumed), they do support the government by reducing borrowing costs. Sayers (1951) suggests that the policy of cheap money was introduced in Britain in the 1930s primarily because it meant money was cheap to the Treasury. 

## **Conclusions** 

Despite being heavily studied, there are still widespread misconceptions regarding monetary- policy transmission. The conventional thinking is that easy monetary policy with lower interest rates encourages households to consume more and companies to invest more, boosting the demand for loans, aggregate demand, and economic growth. 

However, central banks’ unprecedented actions undertaken in response to the Great Recession failed to significantly raise the demand for loans and to revive the economy. The sluggish economic growth despite the ultralow interest rates and huge QE programs undermines the traditional view of the monetary transmission mechanism. The mortal sin of that view is that it assumes that monetary policy works the same way all the time. However, it operates differently during economic upturns and downturns, and it works especially distinctly during balance sheet recessions (when the private sector is deleveraging). As Borio and Zabai (2016, 24) point out, monetary policy should take into account the broader economic context. After the Great Recession, it was, 

the laborious recovery from a balance sheet recession. […] The recession ushered in by the financial crisis is less amenable to traditional monetary policy measures because of the legacy of the previous financial boom. Easier monetary conditions struggle with an impaired banking system, which obstructs the transmission of policy and misallocates credit— the basic constraint is capital rather than liquidity. Easier conditions also have a hard time stimulating an overly indebted private sector, focused on repairing balance sheets rather than spending. And they cannot do much to correct the misallocation of resources fostered by the boom.[31] 

## 22 _The interest rate channel_ 

Hence, the unconventional monetary policy of recent years is subject to diminishing returns,[32] as “the supply of loans may become less responsive to reductions in policy rates when interest rates are already low” (Borio and Gambacorta 2017, 2), and as “there is clearly a limit to how far risk premia can be compressed, expectations guided and interest rates pushed into negative territory” (Borio and Zabai 2016, 25).[33] 

This chapter adds to the literature about the limits of monetary policy in general and the effectiveness of the unconventional monetary policy in particular. The analysis suggests that the monetary policy is a less effective tool for countercyclical stabilization policy than is widely believed. 

Economists focus on the problem of lags, instability of the demand for money, and the effectiveness of particular tools, such as targeting interest rates when they reach the zero lower bound as the main limitation of traditional monetary policy in general and the interest rate channel in particular. However, these concerns are somewhat misplaced as central banks can also aim to affect directly asset prices and long- term yields (although not without some unintended consequences). The more important problem is that monetary policy can be thwarted by perverse responses of markets, including offsetting changes in the velocity of money. As Williams (1931, 580) puts it, “in monetary terms, the crux of the matter is that, while banks control the physical quantity of deposits, depositors control the spending of them, and changes in quantity may be offset by changes in velocity or in direction.” 

Indeed, the unconventional monetary policy implemented in the aftermath of the financial crisis of 2007–8 failed to revive the credit supply, productivity growth, and the overall economy. In a sense, this should not be surprising, as productivity and long- term growth are influenced by structural policies regarding property rights, taxes, incentives, innovation, and the like rather than by central bank actions (sustained economic growth depends essentially on how many people work and how productive they are). 

However, such developments startled some economists who hold the erroneous view about the monetary transmission mechanism that pays too much attention to the traditional interest rate channel. I showed that that channel suffers from several significant problems, which make it a wrong specification of the monetary transmission mechanism. There is no liquidity trap; instead, there is a trap of using wrong macroeconomic models (Meltzer 2001). Because of debt overhang, monetary policy was not effective at spurring strong recovery after the Great Recession. However, it was not ineffective because of the zero lower bound on interest rates. 

In other words, there is no mechanical link between monetary policy and the supply of loans and thus economic growth. Instead, monetary policy’s effects are likely to operate through portfolio rebalancing, asset prices, and revisions in risk perception. As Meltzer (1995, 56) notes, “relative price changes and their effects on spending would not be eliminated by a liquidity trap for interest rates.” 

_The interest rate channel_ 23 

I discuss the portfolio- balance and risk- taking channels in later chapters. Here, I would like to make a few more general points. First of all, this chapter confirmed the proverb that “you can lead a horse to water, but you can’t make him drink.” The central bank can provide commercial banks with opportunities to expand their credit creation, but it cannot force them to do so. Similarly, it can make it easy for borrowers to incur new debts, but it cannot compel them to borrow and spend. This corroborates another popular belief, namely that the power of monetary policy is asymmetrical: monetary policy is more effective against inflation than against deflation, more capable of restraining than stimulating, more capable of halting a boom than escaping a depression (Ellis 1958; Wallich 1958; Mayer 1968; Chick 1973). As Hard (1958, 193–94) says, 

Monetary policy, as is well known, is more effective in damping an inflationary excess of demand than in stimulating demand when business is slack: ‘You cannot push on a string,’ is the familiar cliché. The main duty of monetary policy in a recession is to avoid action that might make a bad situation worse. 

This chapter extends the literature by explaining this asymmetry. In a sense, it can be explained very easily: easy monetary policy creates favorable conditions to expand businesses, but the ultimate decisions are banks’ and entrepreneurs’; tight monetary policy raises interest rates and limits the pool of available funds, so not all investment projects will be completed, even if banks and entrepreneurs want to continue them. Hence, Mayer (1968, 220) points out that “Although monetary policy can choke off a boom, it cannot start a recovery because if businessmen have no incentive to invest, the availability of funds does little to stimulate investment.” 

But this part of the book goes on to articulate the reasons for banks’ and entrepreneurs’ reluctance to lend and borrow in the presence of easy monetary policy: debt overhang, balance sheet repair, deleveraging, and uncertainty. When households deleverage, they reduce their demand for loans. After the decline in asset prices and households’ net wealth, banks might also find it hard to find creditworthy borrowers. Moreover, when banks have to increase their capital because of imposed requirements, they are reluctant to grant new loans. The debt overhang and balance sheet repair, together with other factors such as new economic policies and government regulations, also create uncertainty about the future. As Ueda (2012) points out, “deleveraging attempts by individual economic agents in the aftermath of large financial imbalances can generate significant negative macroeconomic externalities.” 

The chapter shows that monetary policy does not work the same way all the time. Or, in other words, there is heterogeneity in monetary policy and business cycles— their development and consequences will differ depending on the macroeconomic context.[34] As Roosa (1956, 237) says, 

## 24 _The interest rate channel_ 

The effectiveness of monetary policy in promoting stability, and the means by which it can be most effective, depend upon the phase of the business cycle and upon the nature of the monetary influence at work in the particular cyclical situation. 

This chapter suggests also that monetary policy works worst during bad times (in the aftermath of the financial crisis), when it is considered the most needed. It questions the pursuit of countercyclical monetary policy.[35] Central banks do not have tools to bring the economy out of recessions. A recession is not simply a time of subdued aggregate demand, but a process of significant readjustments. As Machlup (1976, 23) notes, “Monetary factors cause the cycle but real phenomena constitute it.” It’s not clear how easy monetary policy might help with deleveraging or resource reallocation, for example. The financial crisis is the result of too much credit, too high asset prices, and excessive risk. So, unless the restructuring does not reduce the amount of credit, the level of asset prices, and the level of risk- taking, the central banks’ ability to stimulate economy will be limited (Rzońca 2014). Unless the misallocation of resources and the underlying disequilibrium are fixed, the economic agents will remain pessimistic about the future and reluctant to invest. The rider can lower the hurdle, but it’s the horse that decides whether to jump. 

Actually, ultralow interest rates may hamper the readjustment process, weakening people’s incentives to repair their balance sheets. It’s true that such interest rates can buy some time, but when maintained low for a prolonged period, they pose risks of financial stability and productivity slowdown. Indeed, the “zombification” of economy and the reliance of zombie companies on easy money can explain why monetary stimulus met with so little success after the global financial crisis.[36] Perhaps, as Humphrey (2010), following Henry Thornton and Walter Bagehot, argues, the Fed and other central banks should adhere to the classical model of the lender of last resort— that is, rescuing only solvent institutions and charging them penalty rates against good collateral instead of lending to too- big- to- fail firms at below- market rates on junk collateral. 

The analysis suggests that economists wrongly see the interest rate as merely a hurdle rate, “which naturally leads to a monetary policy of manipulating the interest rate.” (Fuller 2013, 394). Such a view favors focusing on the volume on investment and disregarding the quality of investment. The question is then how many investment projects will be undertaken, not which projects will be chosen. The concept of malinvestments is irrelevant in such an approach, which reduces the perceived costs of the policy of ultralow interest rates. 

The economists also pay too much attention to the interest rate channel. In consequence, they neglect money and monetary (or credit) aggregates. The New Keynesian macroeconomic model, the basis of contemporary monetary theory and practice, does not even include money (or credit). Thus it understates the risk of financial crisis due to excessive money (credit) supply. 

_The interest rate channel_ 25 

The model wrongly suggests that as long as actual output is less than potential output, easy monetary policy will not generate any negative effects. 

Last but not least, the chapter touches on the debate about exogenous versus endogenous money (Sieroń 2019b). I showed that monetary policy is weakened or does not work as intended during recession. This means that the effectiveness of monetary policy depends on the functioning of the economic system. So monetary policy (and money supply) is not a fully exogenous factor driving economic behavior. It might be the other way around: the economy’s performance drives the monetary policy (and money supply). After the outbreak of the Great Recession, the Fed unprecedentedly increased the US monetary base, but it failed to increase credit in the private sector and to stimulate significantly the economy because banks could not or were not eager to lend, while households and companies could not or were not eager to borrow and invest. Postcrisis economic developments and changes in monetary policy clearly show that the simple money- multiplier model is wrong. The rider can lead the horse to water, but it’s the horse that decides when to drink and, in turn, whether to continue riding. 

## **Notes** 

## 1 Chick (1973, 2–15): 

The analysis of monetary policy centers around two major questions: how does monetary policy affect the economic system and how effective is it (or would it be) in achieving its aims. […] The debate on monetary policy centers around what is called the transmission mechanism: how does monetary policy affect the economy; through what channels does monetary policy operate? 

- 2 In later chapters, I show that the standard description of the interest rate channel also does not take into account several unintended consequences. 

- 3 I discuss this in the second chapter. With the increasing influence of minimumcapital requirements on bank behavior, the bank- capital channel has also gained importance recently (Borio and Zhu 2008). 

- 4 See also Ireland (2005) and Peek and Rosengren (2013). 

- 5 As King (2016, 44) notes, “Central banks have thrown everything at their economies, and yet the results have been disappointing.” 

- 6 Economists do not always specify which interest rates they have in mind. Eichenbaum (1994) rhetorically asks “Which rate?” regarding what matters for investment, implying lack of consensus. 

- 7 The belief that changes in the interest rate entail significant effects on economic activity stems from Keynes’s _Treatise on Money_ (1930). However, in _The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money_ (1936), Keynes is, as he describes himself, “somewhat skeptical of merely monetary policy directed toward influencing the rate of interest” (164). 

- 8 This explains concerns about the zero lower bound. I discuss them briefly in the conclusion to this chapter and, later, in Chapter 6. 

- 9 According to Thornton (2014b, 205), 

The efficacy of the interest rate channel of monetary policy is based on two fundamental propositions: (i) that the Fed can and has controlled the overnight federal funds rate and (ii) that policy-i nduced changes in the overnight rate are transmitted throughout the structure of rates. 

26 _The interest rate channel_ 

_The interest rate channel_ 27 

28 _The interest rate channel_ 

## **2 The portfolio-balance channel; or are monetarist better than Keynesians?** 

When interest rates were slashed almost to zero in response to the financial crisis of 2008–9, central banks faced the zero lower bound. The traditional interest rate channel broke down. To further stimulate economy, the central bank implemented quantitative easing (QE). One of its main objectives was to spur a shift in portfolio balances and to raise asset prices, stimulating investment and consumption through the wealth effect. In this chapter, I a nalyze that channel of the monetary transmission mechanism. 

## **How does quantitative easing work?** 

As one central banker once famously said, “The problem with QE is it works in practice, but it doesn’t work in theory” (Bernanke 2014). Actually, economists differ not only in their understanding of how quantitative easing operates in theory but in their estimates of the magnitude of its effects on long-t erm interest rates and the overall economy (Williams 2014). The literature focuses on two main possible channels through which the largescale asset purchases could affect the economy: the signaling channel and the portfolio-b alance channel (Bean et al. 2015).[1] It’s not easy to disentangle the two channels, but the literature suggests that although both of them work, the portfolio-b alance channel has been somewhat stronger (Williams 2014).[2] That is why I focus on it. 

According to the first channel though, central bank asset purchases provide an indirect signal of the central bank’s future actions. The signal affects the public’s expectations of future short- term interest rates, lowering current long- term interest rates. The problem with signaling lies in the fact that a signal has to be credible and interpreted in the same way by all market participants. 

The portfolio-b alance channel, meanwhile, works as follows: When central banks buy securities, the sellers of these securities want to restore the old proportions between bank deposits and financial assets that existed before the transaction with the central bank. So they acquire other financial assets, including foreign assets,[3] increasing their prices and lowering their yields. Remember that when the central bank purchases securities, it reduces the 

## 30 _The portfolio-balance channel_ 

amount available in the market, raising their prices and pushing their yields down. This effect is known as the supply- induced portfolio- balance effect on long- term yields. But there might be an additional reserve- induced effect if the central bank purchases assets from nonbank entities. Since nonbanks do not have reserve accounts with the central bank, they receive proceeds as deposits with commercial banks. This means that the latter now have more reserves, so they may increase demand for long- term assets, bidding up their prices (Christensen and Krogstrup 2018). 

Then, lower yields, or reduced costs of issuing bonds or shares, and the wealth effect working through the higher asset prices stimulate spending and investment (Gagnon et al. 2011). As Bernanke (2010a) explains, 

The channels through which the Fed’s purchases affect longer- term interest rates and financial conditions more generally have been subject to debate. I see the evidence as most favorable to the view that such purchases work primarily through the so- called portfolio balance channel, which holds that once short- term interest rates have reached zero, the Federal Reserve’s purchases of longer- term securities affect financial conditions by changing the quantity and mix of financial assets held by the public. Specifically, the Fed’s strategy relies on the presumption that different financial assets are not perfect substitutes in investors’ portfolios, so that changes in the net supply of an asset available to investors affect its yield and those of broadly similar assets. Thus, our purchases of Treasury, agency debt, and agency MBS likely both reduced the yields on those securities and also pushed investors into holding other assets with similar characteristics, such as credit risk and duration. For example, some investors who sold MBS to the Fed may have replaced them in their portfolios with longer- term, high- quality corporate bonds, depressing the yields on those assets as well.[4] 

So the portfolio- balance channel rests on the assumption that markets are segmented. As investors have so- called preferred habitats for certain types of investments, assets are imperfect substitutes for each other (Culbertson 1957). In consequence, the central bank purchases disrupt the relative supply and demand of assets, affecting the marginal investor’s valuations and the assets’ prices. 

Another important element of the portfolio- balance channel is the inducement to take more risk. Although Bernanke in the quote above suggests that central bank purchases of assets push investors to hold other assets with similar characteristics, this conjecture is debatable. If we accept the assumption that assets differ, there might be no other assets with similar characteristics. When the central bank buys one type of assets, their prices go up while their yields decline. Market participants will then look for assets offering higher yields, which will most likely be riskier assets. 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 31 

In that way, central banks encourage the risk- taking they are supposedly so worried about. As Pill and Reichlin (2017, 14) put it, it is inherent in the portfolio- balance channel that 

the central bank pushes asset managers into securities with risk profiles they would otherwise be skeptical about at prices they would otherwise deem too high.[5] 

Similarly, Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 145–46, 251–52) note, 

The basic idea behind QE was that, in a Tobinian approach of imperfect asset substitution, the exchange of bonds in the portfolios of private investors with Fed reserves would have pushed yields on bonds lower and give an incentive to investors to move towards riskier assets, like equities or foreign assets, thus reducing their return as well. 

[…] 

Indeed, the clearest channel through which QE affected the real economy was the so- called portfolio balance effect. By reducing the yield on the securities bought under the QE programmes, investors were pushed to purchase riskier as well as foreign securities, thus providing cheaper finance to riskier sectors of the economy and pushing the exchange rate down.[6] 

Of course, mere portfolio adjustments are not the ultimate aim of quantitative easing. Through them, the central banks want to lower long- term interest rates and to increase asset prices. The rise in asset prices is assumed to then increase aggregate levels of income and spending through the wealth effect.[7] More specifically, the boost in asset prices represents an increase in net wealth for the assets’ owners. These capital gains should then stimulate spending by households.[8] 

In other words, the purpose of quantitative easing is to increase the supply of broad money and aggregate demand by directly increasing the monetary base rather than by influencing the price of bank reserves. In doing so, QE tries to directly affect asset prices and long- term interest rates (Bowdler and Radio 2012). The idea was not new: in 2000, Friedman (2000) already argued that a zero nominal interest rate is not a problem for monetary policy because in such a situation, central bankers “can buy long- term government securities, and they can keep buying them and providing high- powered money until the high- powered money starts getting the economy in an expansion.” 

Hence, central banks, through quantitative easing, injected new money into the economy in an attempt to influence long- term interest rates and the economy while bypassing the banking sector.[9] 

32 _The portfolio-balance channel_ 

## **Portfolio- balance channel in monetary policy and economic thought** 

The portfolio- balance channel was already recognized by Mises ([1912] 1953), Meltzer (1951), Tobin (1961), Friedman (1969), Tobin (1969), and Brunner and Meltzer (1972). Mises points out that monetary theory must “show the way in which subjective valuations are affected by variations in the ratio between the stock of money and the demand for money” (Mises [1912] 1953, 136). 

Somewhat similarly, Meltzer (1951) argues that open market operations affect the interest rate not because they alter the quantity of money but because they affect the quantity of privately held securities. He even claims that “any monetary disturbance which does not affect private security holdings will leave the equilibrium interest rate unchanged” (Meltzer 1951, 111). 

Tobin (1961) also argues that monetary policy affects the economy through the public’s portfolio choices. Thus he proposes a systematic application of relative- price theory to financial assets— just like the monetarists, actually, although Tobin would probably not agree (Brunner 1968). Indeed, according to the monetarists, “monetary impulses are transmitted to the economy by a relative price process which operates on money, financial assets (and liabilities), real assets, yields on assets and the production of new assets, liabilities and consumables” (Brunner 1968, 15). Similarly, Brunner and Meltzer (1988, 446–47) argue that “monetary impulses are conveyed by a process of general substitution driven by changes in relative prices (1) of various assets, (2) of real assets and their services, and (3) of assets and output.” 

Friedman (1969) also notices that an increase in the money supply will result in changes in the balance sheets of entities that then want to adjust to the new conditions by buying other assets. These expenditures will contribute to the increase in the prices of these assets, and thus the effect of the increase in the money supply will gradually spread through the economy like ripples in a lake. Changes in the price of assets will result in changes in the structure of relative prices and the distribution of income and wealth, which in turn will result in further adjustments in the economic system (Friedman 1969). 

In particular, he described the effects of open market operations. According to him, the seller of the bond will have more money, so she will want to spend it on other assets, which will raise their prices and make the remaining assets relatively cheaper. Over time, newly acquired money will spread across the economy, affecting the prices of ever more financial assets. The rise in their prices will, however, make nonfinancial assets relatively more attractive, which will encourage investors to buy them, transmitting the effects of monetary expansion into the real sector.[10] 

Although the literature lists several channels of monetary transmission, Chick (1973) argues that they all can be reduced, on a general level, to two basic views: the classical, revived later by the monetarists, and the Keynesian.[11] The former mechanism implies direct effects of monetary policy on spending, while the latter is supposed to work only indirectly, through the 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 33 

interest rate, which determines the cost of borrowing. The debate is somewhat futile, as both sides have a point. I mean that in the contemporary monetary system, new money enters the economy via the credit market, affecting the economy through interest rates in the first place. However, this is not a logical necessity, and the monetary expansion would affect the economy even without any changes in interest rates.[12] 

Anyway, there is little recognition in the Keynesian literature that monetary policy works through the supply schedules and prices of financial assets. In reality, economic agents react to changes in relative prices, adjusting their portfolios and thereby transmitting the monetary impulses to income and spending and the real sector. Nonetheless, the substitutability between money and financial assets is central in both the Keynesian and monetarist views (Cagan 1972). The quarrel is more about “different properties to the major variables in the linkage sequence, for example, in terms of timing, importance, and response” (Mann 1969, 541). 

According to the monetarists, the activities of the central bank disrupt the structure, which individuals see as optimal, of assets and their yields. Individuals will thus rebalance their assets, trying to recreate the optimal structure. For example, an increase in the money supply will result in changes in the balance sheets of entities that will now want to adjust to the new conditions by buying other assets. These expenditures will contribute to the increase in the prices of these assets, and thus the effect of the increase in money supply will gradually spread throughout the economy. Changes in the prices of assets will result in changes in the structure of relative prices, which in turn will result in further adjustments in the economic system (Friedman 1969). 

This approach has several strengths. The literature often boils them down to the fact that monetarists analyze a much wider spectrum of assets than Keynesians (Meltzer 1995). Although true, much more important is that, contrary to the Keynesian transmission mechanism, which just assumes that the central bank determines the cost of capital and then examines its impact on large aggregates, the monetarist approach tries to show how central banks’ actions actually affect the economy. 

Monetarists also do not abstract from money and do not treat monetary policy as a matter of merely regulating interest rates. After all, central banks directly control their balance sheets, not market yields. In other words, the power of central banks and their ability to affect the economy is ultimately based on their monopoly of currency and reserve creation. Moreover, with its focus on changes in cash balances of individuals and their demand for money, the monetarist transmission mechanism is based on microeconomic fundamentals and price theory. Indeed, the basic idea is that monetary impulses are transferred further through the relative- price mechanism. 

It is actually quite ironic that central banks implemented quantitative easing while relying on the portfolio- balance channel. Based on the description above, it should be clear that a mere monetary stimulus does not guarantee the direction and magnitude of its effects on prices and real sector, as they 

## 34 _The portfolio-balance channel_ 

depend on the behavior of the public; hence, these impulses can “leak” to higher cash balances or go abroad because of agents’ pessimistic expectations, insensitivity of demand to changes in interest rates, purchases of foreign assets, and the like. The existence of such a complex adjustment process is why monetary policy operates with a nonnegligible delay. This is why Friedman (1969) is critical of discretionary actions and argues for rule- based monetary policy.[13] 

The monetarist approach is, however, not devoid of certain shortcomings. The literature draws attention to three problems (Duwendag et al. 1995). First, it is not certain whether economic actors are able to calculate the yields of all their assets (e.g., human capital). Without known prices or yields, the transmission mechanism might be impaired. 

Second, Keynesian economists argue that monetarists do not take into account quantitative adjustment processes (e.g., credit rationing) and, more generally, pay too little attention to credit. Given that in the modern economy new money is injected mainly through the credit channel, this criticism seems justified. Changes in the supply of credit are not the by- product of portfolio adjustments but the very leaven of them. 

Third, without a relatively stable money- demand function, monetary impulses do not generate predictable effects. This is true, but it is a critique of monetary policy rather than of the theoretical analysis of the economic process itself. 

I would like to comment on three further issues. First of all, the monetarist approach abstracts from the capital structure. It focuses on asset portfolios and boils the critique of the standard IS- LM framework down to the observation that it acknowledges only two assets: money and bonds (Brunner and Meltzer 1972). Although the critique is justified, there are many more problems with Keynesian macroeconomics. For example, it does not take sufficiently into account the misallocation of capital, a key phenomenon for understanding the business cycle (Machaj 2017).[14] 

Second, monetarists maintain that the effects of changes in the money supply will be merely transitory and disappear in the long run. It is true that the economy tends to reemerge balanced after a monetary disturbance, but it enters a _new_ equilibrium path. As Friedman (1969) himself notes, shifts in the real sphere that result from changes in the money supply can influence the monetary sphere and in turn cause further adjustment on the real side. 

Third, although the portfolio- balance channel is similar to Cantillon’s analysis of the sequential effects of monetary injections, there are some differences. The portfolio- balance channel rests on unexpected increases in the money supply— if they were expected, people would have adjusted their portfolios already— while the Cantillon effect hinges on an uneven rise in cash balances. The problem is not that the increase in the money supply is unexpected but that it is not proportional (temporally and quantitatively). In other words, for Cantillonian analysis, what is crucial is where the new money enters the economy, while for the portfolio- balance channel that is 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 35 

not so important, as the price mechanism and arbitrage will eventually ensure a return to equilibrium. Thus, in the portfolio- balance approach, it does not matter how the new money enters the economy; what matters is the discrepancy between actual and desired cash balances (Cagan 1972). Meanwhile, in the Austrian business cycle theory (ABCT), the cause of the boom is not an unexpected increase in money balances but an increase in the supply of loanable funds. 

Moreover, the modern portfolio- balance approach rests on the imperfect substitutability of assets (e.g., Krishnamurthy and Vissing- Jorgensen 2011). The contrary assumption of perfect substitutability does not, of course, hold, as the assets would all be the same and it would be meaningless to give the same asset different names.[15] However, it is not relevant to the discussion, as perfect substitutes cannot be the subject of action, because individuals are indifferent in choosing among them. Hence, in reality, goods and assets are always imperfect substitutes from the perspective of acting people. In other words, it seems that the portfolio- balance approach confuses the assumption of perfect substitutability, in which rates of return cannot diverge, with the assumption about equilibrium properties of markets that while rates can temporarily diverge, arbitrage keeps them generally in line (Chick 1973).[16] Thus in reality the portfolio- balance effect does not rely on imperfect asset substitutability but on unequal growth in the money supply, which affects cash balances only sequentially. 

Another problem with the assumption of imperfect substitutability between assets is that “while market segmentation increases the impact of the Fed’s relatively small operations in particular segments of the market, it limits the Fed’s ability to affect bond yields throughout the market more generally” (Thornton 2014b). 

## **The wealth effect and its critique** 

The wealth effect, a key element of the portfolio- balance channel, seems to be another reincarnation of the mercantilist fallacy that money is wealth. As Smith ([1776] 1904) already pointed out in 1776, people are wealthy to the extent that they possess goods and services, not money. The implication is that the central bank (or government) does not have any tools that enable it to increase the wealth of society (other than by creating the appropriate conditions for thrift, entrepreneurship, and productivity). 

Of course, central bankers are aware of that, and their notion of the wealth effect is more sophisticated. They don’t claim that money is wealth but that the rise in the money supply and reduction in interest rates increase asset prices,[17] which stimulates spending through the perception of the holders of these assets that they are wealthier. 

However, what central banks seem to not notice (or to ignore) is that only a part of the population benefits from the growth of the money supply and higher prices of selected financial assets. Those who receive the new money 

## 36 _The portfolio-balance channel_ 

later, along with creditors and people without assets, are in a worse position because of the Cantillon effect (Cantillon [1755] 1959).[18] So, although central banks perceive the benefits to some people, they forget about those who suffer from the increase in the asset prices— that is, people who cannot afford to buy them because of price increases or limited access to capital markets (Hülsmann 2013). 

This effect shouldn’t be neglected. Lower interest rates and higher asset prices increase the price of acquiring a given future income stream. This means reinvestment opportunities are reduced, so individuals need to spend more to purchase the same income stream, which clearly hurts them. This is particularly important for the young who don’t have their own houses and for older people who have to save more to enjoy the same retirement income stream (Doukas 2015). 

Hence, the widespread belief in the benefits of the wealth effect seems to be another variation of the broken- window fallacy (Bastiat [1850] 2007). It is relatively easy to identify the benefits to the first recipients of new money, as they take the form of higher expenditures and higher prices of assets, goods and services sold on them. It is much harder to notice that because of the Cantillon effect, monetary expansion causes losses for the late recipients of newly created money and the have- nots: it is impossible to observe the unrealized demand of people whose income did not increase as much as the prices of the goods, services, and assets they would like to purchase. 

In other words, an increase in the prices of assets (or goods or services) is not able to create wealth but merely to redistribute it. Pumping money into the asset market does not ensure a better asset market or economy, because when one group of economic agents buys more assets, others buy less (Williams 1931). And an increase in the price of one good means a decrease in the relative price of all other goods (Pesek and Saving 1967). 

Let’s also note that in the current monetary system, the majority of the money supply originates in the process of credit creation. Changes in the supply of money thus affect both assets and liabilities: 

A change in the price of any commodity in one direction is simply a change in the price of all other commodities in the other direction. A price- induced wealth effect in the case of commodity money may be obtained only if we pay attention to the gain of the former and ignore the loss of the latter. 

Most of modern money is said to be, however, ‘credit’ money. This money is an asset of the individual who holds it … however, it is simultaneously a debt of the agency that created this money. […] If this is the case, a change in the level of prices cannot change the level of wealth because the second, positive, and the third, negative, term change by precisely the same, offsetting amounts. The price- induced wealth effect is then an optical illusion. 

(Pesek and Saving 1967, 35–36) 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 37 

Let me note a few further problems with the mainstream view of the wealth effect. First, there is an inconsistency here. It assumes that the rise in asset prices is positive for the economy, but this assumption does not apply to the exchange rate. On the contrary, a lower exchange rate is what is believed to support the economy through exports, which adds to GDP. But there is a more general problem here: society, as a whole, is wealthier when the supply of good and services increases while their prices decrease, not increase, as prices signal relative scarcity. 

Second, the wealth effect is at odds with mainstream economists’ assumption of long- run neutrality. After all, if all prices rose simultaneously, nothing would change. So the wealth effect works when only some prices increase and, in consequence, only some people spend more. However, if this is the case, the composition of expenditures will change in a response to the increase in the money supply and thus the structure of production will reshape too. 

Third, the increase in value of assets people own does not mean that they have more income to spend. The only way to generate funds it to sell or to borrow against the higher value of the asset (King 2016). So, the link between the wealth and consumption spending is not as simple as it is assumed in the mainstream literature about the wealth effect. 

Fourth, perceived increased wealth can be not only consumed but saved. Actually, it is reasonable to expect that rising wealth is positively linked to savings as people satisfy less distant needs first such that getting wealthier is likely to be accompanied by an increased propensity to save. After all, the increase in asset prices benefits more people at the top part of the income distribution,[19] whose propensity to spend is relatively lower (Dynan et al. 2000). 

Fifth, the contemporary wealth effect seems to be at odds with the Pigou effect. Pigou (1943) argues that even with interest rates at the zero lower bound, full employment can be ensured by falling prices, as they lead to higher real cash balances and thus higher spending. But the modern wealth effect relies on rising prices, and Pigou would say that rising prices would increase the desire to save out of income. 

Sixth, the central banks’ attempts to generate the wealth effect in a response to recession may delay the needed rebalancing of an economy, because production goods should be then sold at reduced prices to transform the malinvestments into profitable businesses (Cwik 2008). Higher asset prices thus postpone the investment liquidations and the arrival of the recovery. 

Seventh, central banks’ asset purchases risk crossing the boundary between monetary policy and asset price formation. The markets’ ability to discover prices and yields may be thus impaired, while their role as financial intermediaries in allocating capital diminished (Hannoun 2012). 

Our view of the wealth effect depends on how we define it and wealth itself. For example, Samuelson (1945) claims that the only reasonable criterion of whether a person or institution is better off is the disposable real income over time, not the present value of the assets. Similarly, White (2012, 12) 

## 38 _The portfolio-balance channel_ 

argues that wealth should be defined as “the capacity to have a higher future standard of living.” From this perspective, higher equity prices constitute wealth only when they are justified by higher expected productivity and higher future earnings, not when they result from policy- induced lower interest rates. Instead, higher house prices increase the collateral values against which loans can be taken out to support spending. But loans must be repaid at the cost of future consumption. Hence, there is no wealth created here; there is just an intertemporal shift in consumption. Prices fluctuate all the time; mere changes in signals do not change current wealth. 

However, when people consider the rise in asset prices caused by monetary expansion or artificial reduction in interest rates as a gain, they fall prey to an illusion (Mises [1949] 1998).[20] Thus understood, the wealth effect— that is, a false (too optimistic) assessment of wealth caused by monetary inflation and distorted interest rates— can be integrated into the ABCT. According to this theory, credit expansion misleads households to a falsely optimistic appraisal of their real income and net worth, contributing to overconsumption. Indeed, as Salerno (2012, 30) shows, the rise in households’ net worth after the recession of 2001, 

was based almost solely on paper profits and phantom capital gains on households’ real estate and financial assets. Misled by their inflationbloated balance sheets, households were induced to ‘cash out’ some of their home equity and increase expenditures on consumer goods and services. In the expression of the day, people began ‘using their homes as ATM machines.’[21] 

The key here is the distinction between realized capital gains and paper capital gains. Those who have sold assets whose prices have increased have realized capital gains. But those who have not have only paper capital gains. It should be obvious that the latter group are not able, in the aggregate, to realize those gains (Chick 1973). However, the boom phase of the economic cycle, with low interest rates and a growing money supply, may mislead them to think otherwise and thus to make too optimistic expenditure plans. 

## **Conclusions** 

The main transmission channel of quantitative easing is the portfolio- balance channel. Not only are central banks aware that an increase in the money supply leads to only a sequential rise in incomes and prices, but they rely on that mechanism. They also assume that an increase in asset prices increases the wealth of holders of these assets, resulting in higher spending and economic recovery. However, higher prices cannot increase the general standard of living. 

But they can help some entities— such as banks with impaired balance sheets— to recapitalize themselves by generating increases in asset values. 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 39 

Indeed, as Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 191) note, “The increase in asset prices works in the same manner as capital injections to banks with large holdings of the involved assets.” In this light, quantitative easing can be seen as indirect capital injections to capital- constrained institutions in order to facilitate lending or even as programs for de facto bailing out financial institutions with underperforming assets as central banks take them from the books of these institutions in exchange for safer central bank reserves (Horwitz 2013, 17). As Papadia and Välimäki (2018, 202) explain, this effect works via several channels: 

First, replacing riskier assets with central bank money in banks’ balance sheets reduces the riskiness of their assets, allowing banks to increase their provision of riskier loans. Similarly, the increased valuation of assets on bank balance sheets relieves pressure on value- at- risk limits and capital constraints, providing the banks with the possibility of increasing lending. Third, lower yields of marketable debt instruments give banks greater incentive to lend rather than invest in domestic sovereign bonds. Finally, generally lax financing conditions influence the funding costs of banks, allowing them to lend at lower rates. 

Higher bond prices can also help governments. As I examine in detail in Chapter 7, when central banks purchase Treasury bonds, their prices go up while yields decline, reducing governments’ borrowing costs. Ultralow interest rates make incurring debt more attractive, encouraging fiscally imprudent behavior on the part of governments. 

Keynesians are wrong, while monetarists are right: monetary policy does not become totally powerless when interest rates reach the zero lower bound. It affects the economy through the relative prices of assets, goods, and services. However, the monetarist presentation of the portfolio- balance channel is not free of defects. It wrongly relies on the substitutability of assets and the wealth effect, instead of the Cantillon effect. Actually, the portfolio- balance channel is not a distinctive transmission channel of monetary policy; it is just the application of price theory to the sequential character of monetary expansion. It is thus disturbing that economists have to distinguish this “channel” and debate its empirical relevance. 

But what is really disturbing is this: the central bank purchases assets on a large scale to increase asset prices. This affects the real economy either through lower yields or the wealth effect. However, as I showed in the previous chapter, lower interest rates do not necessarily substantially spur investments, and as I showed in this chapter, the wealth effect is dubious. The implications are rather bitter: the unconventional monetary actions boil down to redistributive policy. They do affect wealth but only that of some agents, such as financial institutions, governments, and homeowners. 

Indeed, the Figure 2.1 shows the changes in US consumer prices and home prices from 1988 to 2018. As one can see, the latter were increasing much

faster in the years preceding the global financial crisis. In 2012, home inflation started to outpace general consumer inflation again. 

Such increases in the price level of existing single- family homes in the United States (this is what the S&P/Case- Shiller Index measures) should be puzzling: as houses depreciate, their value should decline, ceteris paribus. However, credit expansion with certain government policies focused on increasing the homeownership rate by raising the demand for houses and thus their prices. The sustained monetary expansion and rising home prices led more and more people to think of houses as an excellent investment, spurring the demand and prices even more. As Reisman (2014) pointed out, 

Inflation and the continuing rise in home prices that it fuels, led to owner- occupied housing, a consumers’ good as much as one’s personal automobile or furniture, coming to be thought of as a capital good and thus a vehicle for investment, thereby diverting substantial sums away from actual investment and into home purchases. Massive credit expansion pouring into the real estate market resulted in the last decade in millions of homes being constructed for buyers who could not afford to pay for them, thus representing a massive unremunerated transfer of wealth and causing a corresponding deficiency in the capital of producers throughout the economic system. And inflation has led to artificially increased profits and correspondingly increased taxes on those profits, despite the fact that the additional profits are needed merely to meet the rise in replacement costs that results from inflation and thus are not at all any kind of genuine gains. 

_The portfolio-balance channel_ 41 

This chapter thus strengthens the criticism of (too easy) monetary policy. It turns out that no matter whether it operates via the interest rate or asset prices, it entails dangerous distortions in the structure of prices and the whole economy. In particular, unconventional monetary policy, instead of increasing society’s wealth, results in asset mispricing[22] and hampered price discovery, and elevated asset prices or even asset- price bubbles (such as the US real estate bubble in the 2000s), which creates the risk of financial crisis. Hence, there is no such a thing as neutral monetary policy, and it seems that Ellis (1958, 162, emphasis in original) is right: “While creating more money is one of the easiest things under the sun, the injection of new purchasing power into the economy in ways which are not demoralizing to the economy is _not_ easy.
