## **3 Should we be afraid of venturers? Monetary policy and risk- taking** 

Changes in monetary policy may affect economic agents’ perception and pricing of risk[1] through the so-c alled risk-t aking channel. Borio and Zhu (2008) define the channel as the situation in which banks take on risk that is not reflected in the risk premium. There are many forms of risk- taking. For example, the level of risk taken may increase because of additional liquidity or higher asset valuation (both of which lower risk aversion), weakened valueat- risk constraints, or mitigated adverse selection (which reduces screening). 

We can analyze the riskiness of either existing loans or new projects. Financial institutions can either acquire more assets of the same risk, or they can shift their funds toward riskier assets. In other words, they can either increase their leverage or engage in riskier ventures. When it comes to banks, they can 

- lower loan- loss provisions (i.e., hold smaller buffers against unexpected credit losses; see Genay and Podjasek 2014 and Bikker and Vervliet 2017)[2] ; 

- • generate more income from riskier-t han-t raditional sources, such as foreign markets, asset trading, or securitization (Rajan 2005)[3] ; 

- 4 

- • invest in assets of greater duration or higher risk; 

- lower credit standards (increase credit risk); 

- • employ greater leverage. 

In this chapter, I distinguish three main ways the risk- taking channel operates. First, cuts in interest rates can lead to more borrowing and “search for yield.”[5] Second, lower interest rates boost asset prices and collateral values, which can increase risk tolerance and thus encourage taking more risk. Third, the “Greenspan put” and the assumption that some institutions are too big to fail may also encourage more risk-t aking.[6] 

The risk-t aking channel has recently gained attention. Although researchers and policy makers consider it one of the monetary transmission channels— a s I already noted in Chapter 2, inducing a shift toward riskier assets is actually the basis of the portfolio- balance channel, the key channel of quantitative easing[7] —they are increasingly concerned about its long-t erm negative effects. As Yellen (2011) notes, “The shift toward riskier assets is a normal channel through which monetary policy supports economic activity. But taken too far, 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 45 

this dynamic has the potential to facilitate the emergence of financial imbalances”. Similarly, Bernanke (2013) worried, 

The Committee is aware that a long period of low interest rates has costs and risks. […] Another cost, one that we take very seriously, is the possibility that very low interest rates, if maintained too long, could undermine financial stability. For example, investors or portfolio managers dissatisfied with low returns may “reach for yield” by taking on more credit risk, duration risk, or leverage. 

However, despite its significance and the rising acknowledgement of its existence, risk- taking remains a relatively poorly studied channel of monetary policy.[8] This chapter tries to fill this gap. I show how monetary policy affects risk- taking. In the following three sections, I analyze the links between monetary policy and risk- taking I mentioned earlier. In the fourth part, I discuss how this channel relates to the theory of the business cycle. The fifth section concludes. 

## **Searching for yield** 

First of all, ultralow interest rates combined with central bank asset purchases can lead entities to invest in high- yielding but riskier assets to boost profits.[9] Although there are a few reasons for that, the literature focuses on “sticky” rate- of- return targets in light of the principal- agent problem and inappropriate incentive structures for investment managers (Rajan 2005).[10] 

Rate- of- return targets might be sticky either because individuals form reference points for investment returns or because financial institutions are formally (e.g., they have entered into guaranteed- investment contracts) or informally pledged to achieve a specific rate of return. Among these financial institutions are not only pension funds, insurers, and real estate investment trusts but also commercial banks. If they want to have some chance of survival (or are just dissatisfied with low rates of return), they will search for higher yields, even beyond what the end holders of the risk would probably prefer (Chodorow- Reich 2014).[11] 

However, we don’t have to make any assumptions about the stickiness of the targeted yields or the incentive structure of investment managers (although these explanations might be valid as well). It is enough to note, in line with portfolio- allocation theory (Markowitz 1952), the increase in relative attractiveness of risky assets when the risk- free rate declines. Or it is sufficient to notice that the risk- free rate is a benchmark used to price risky financial instruments. 

Let’s assume the risk- free rate is 5 percent, while a risky asset yields 10 percent. In such a situation, the risky asset is twice as attractive as the riskfree security (in terms of nominal rate of return). But when the risk- free rate declines to 1 percent, the risky asset now yields 6 percent (we hold the risk 

## 46 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

premium at 5 percent), which is six times more attractive than the alternative (Lian et al. 2018). 

Hence, a low rate of return on (relatively) safe assets makes it more expensive to accumulate a portfolio composed of them. In consequence, individuals are tempted to look for other, riskier investments (such as emerging- market debt, low- grade bonds, buy- to- lease properties). In other words, reducing the risk- free rate lowers the hurdle rate for risky investment projects. Investors will thus increase their spending on riskier projects, or, in other words, on projects with lower risk- adjusted returns.[12] This is because the, 

hurdle rate depends on both the cost of capital and the project’s riskiness. When the risk- free interest rate declines, newly viable projects have either lower expected returns or higher risk. If projects on the margin of funding mostly have higher risk, total project risk in the economy will rise. 

Chodorow- Reich 2014, 161 

Searching for yield is strongly linked to adding leverage. This is because when interest rates are (ultra)low, the cost of borrowing is also (ultra)low. This encourages individuals and companies to add leverage, which can be considered one of the strategies to boost profits in a ultralow interest rate environment. In particular, economic agents adopt a more risky balance sheet financing structure, switching from equity finance to loan finance, because the latter is cheaper (Palley 2019). 

Additionally, as I show in Chapter 7, an environment of persistently low interest rates supports debt- financed investment in highly leveraged longlived assets, such as housing. The process is of enormous importance as debt overhang among households lowers financial stability, making the economy more vulnerable to negative shocks (Jordà et al. 2014; Bean et al. 2015). In other words, excessive leverage during the boom causes slow growth in the wake of a crisis. In line with intertemporal substitution of spending, low interest rates and accumulation of debt can stimulate the economy today but only at the expense of lower growth tomorrow. 

Moreover, as debt service is easier at low interest rates, entities’ credit ratings improve, which lowers the risk premium they have to pay. This applies both to companies and governments, as the European debt crisis clearly showed (Bagus 2010). 

Indeed, low interest rates create a massive arbitrage opportunity: investors can borrow at low rates and buy higher- yielding assets. This carry- trade investment strategy reinforces the downward pressure on yields, thereby compressing the spread across assets (Blundell- Wignall 2007).[13] 

This phenomenon applies both to the domestic economy and to global markets. Since markets are integrated, there are international spillovers. For example, the advanced economies maintained very low interest rates after the global financial crisis, which led to an outflow of capital to developing and riskier countries and an increase in the latter’s asset prices.[14] 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 47 

There is an important feedback loop here, as the capital inflows strengthen the currency of the recipient economy. This currency appreciation improves the balance sheets of borrowers, so they seem now less risky to banks, which are inclined to lend even more to them (Bruno and Shin 2014). 

Thus, the lax monetary policy in the twenty- first century conducted in advanced economies triggered or at least intensified the boom in many developing countries, which were considered unstable and risky. What is more, investments in sectors producing higher- order goods (such as mining or agriculture), which dominate in these countries, seem particularly risky. According to Becker (2013), many African countries experienced an intense boom in the first years of the twenty- first century (World Bank 2015) because they produced mainly higher- order goods and were characterized by a relatively high risk premium, which attracted capital seeking the highest (expected) rates of return. 

## **Higher risk tolerance** 

Lower interest rates boost asset prices and collateral values. This can in turn modify bank measures of credit risk and default probability, encouraging them to continue to grant risky loans. Higher asset prices and collateral values imply higher risk tolerance— that is, higher buffers against defaults (Borio et al. 2001; Guillemette and Finke 2014). 

Moreover, excessive liquidity, low interest rates, and rising asset prices produce a low- volatility environment, which may also distort the perception of risk and encourage banks and investors to increase their exposure to risk (Gambacorta 2009). The ample liquidity enables investors to buy assets anytime volatility rises and prices decline, hence suppressing volatility. Implicit assurances provided by central banks also lower volatility. 

When higher asset prices and lower realized volatility feed into banks’ value- at- risk (VaR) models, banks can become more complacent about downside risks and increase their leverage (Chodorow- Reich 2014). As commercial banks tend to target a certain level of VaR, they tend to increase leverage when asset prices increase (and decrease leverage when asset prices decrease), which amplifies the procyclical effects of monetary policy (European Central Bank 2008). It is also worth noting that accounting approaches to valuation that are very sensitive to market values underestimate risk, which encourages imprudent behavior (Borio et al. 2001). 

This underestimation occurs because rising asset prices are usually associated with lower price volatility, which lowers measures of portfolio riskiness such as VaR. This mechanism encourages the inflow of capital to risky assets, which increases their prices further, narrowing the risk spreads. This is exactly what we observed in the run- up to the financial crisis of 2007–9. On the flip side, when the crisis burst, the risk premium surged, asset prices plunged, and VaR soared. 

In other words, lower nominal rates make liquidity cheaper and raise leverage, resulting in lower risk premia and higher asset prices (Drechsler et al. 

48 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

2014). Thus, monetary policy that lowers interest rates and boosts asset prices compared to the counterfactual without central bank intervention may expose investors to model risk.[15] As Dowd (2002, 178) notes, “Model risk in financial models always boils down to pricing error.” Thus, when interest rates are lowered by central banks, their signaling and allocation functions are weakened, which leads market participants to costly pricing errors.[16] And in periods of low volatility and reduced risk premiums, uncorrelated risks may become significantly correlated (Taleb 2007). 

## **Moral hazard** 

Another mechanism through which the risk- taking channel may be activated is the perception of the central bank’s reaction function, or the interaction with the central bank. When individuals assume some banks or companies are too big to fail, they invest in them more (or undertake less prudent decisions if they work for these entities). As King (2016, 254) notes, “the toxic nexus between limited liability, deposit insurance and lender of last resort means that there is a massive implicit subsidy to risk- taking by banks.” The safety net and monetary easing central banks provide in downturns thus contribute to moral hazard. Farhi and Tirole (2012) show that banks have incentives to correlate their risk exposures in order to be bailed out in the event of failure. When central banks fail to take risk- taking into account in their reaction function, it may thus lead to the buildup of financial imbalances (White 2004). 

The best example of moral hazard might be the US housing boom in the 2000s. As Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac had the implicit support of the government, they could borrow in the market at lower interest rates and be less prudent in their investment decisions (Mueller 2001; O’Driscoll 2008; White 2009). Importantly, the problem of moral hazard has been noted by policy makers: 

In the midst of the crisis, providing support to a too- big- to- fail firm usually represents the best of bad alternatives; without such support there could be substantial damage to the economy. However, the existence of too- big- to- fail firms creates several problems in the long run. First, too- big- to- fail generates a severe moral hazard. If creditors believe that an institution will not be allowed to fail, they will not demand as much compensation for risks as they otherwise would, thus weakening market discipline; nor will they invest as many resources in monitoring the firm’s risk- taking. As a result, too- big- to- fail firms will tend to take more risk than desirable, in the expectation that they will receive assistance if their bets go bad. Where they have the necessary authority, regulators will try to limit that risk- taking, but without the help of market discipline they will find it difficult to do so, even if authorities are nominally sufficient. The buildup of risk in too- big- to- fail firms increases the possibility of a 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 49 

financial crisis and worsens the crisis when it occurs. There is little doubt that excessive risk- taking by too- big- to- fail firms significantly contributed to the crisis, with Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac being prominent examples. 

(Bernanke 2010b)[17] 

However, moral hazard may be caused not only by the government’s guarantees or the central bank’s role as lender of last resort but also by asymmetric monetary policy. The Fed, as well as other major central banks, cuts rates sharply when the economy falters, but fails to raise them quickly when the economy rebounds. Such an asymmetry enables the financial sector to worry less about potential problems and to lower expectations of downside risks, encouraging risk- taking (Gambacorta 2011).[18] As Diamond and Rajan (2009) put it, asymmetric monetary policy creates incentives for banks to seek out more illiquidity than is good for the system. In other words, “changes in interest rates can have an asymmetric impact on risk- taking, that is, reductions encourage risk- taking by more than the equivalent increases in discouraging risk- taking” (Ramayandi et al. 2014, 2). 

Backus and Wright (2007), European Central Bank (2008), and Adrian and Shin (2010) link moral hazard and the decline in the risk premium to improved transparency and communication of the central banks. When combined with certain characteristics of central banks’ reaction functions (viz., central banks accommodate the financial sector after negative shocks), the transparency and communication reduce the uncertainty surrounding future short- term rates and thus encourage more risk- taking. Tristani (2007) also shows that reducing investors’ uncertainty about the future course of monetary policy can lower the equity premium. 

## **Monetary policy and risk- taking: review of empirical studies** 

Using data from individual US banks over the period 1980–2000, Berger and Udell (2003) show that banks eased credit standards over the lending cycle, which contributed to bank- loan cyclicality. Acharya and Naqvi (2012) show that when the banking sector has access to abundant liquidity, for example because of accommodative monetary policy, it engages in too much risktaking, inducing excessive credit volume and asset- price bubbles. 

The run- up to the financial crisis of 2007–9 is a great example of searching for yield. In the 2000s, commercial banks substantially raised the maturity of their securities portfolios, taking much more risk than officially measured (Wang 2017). Kandrac and Schlusche (2017) document that reserves created in quantitative easing (QE) programs led to an increase in the share of riskier loans, such as commercial real estate, construction, commercial and industrial (C&I), and consumer loans, within banks’ loan portfolios, while Lysandrou and Shabani (2018) show that the search for yield was a major driving force 

50 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

behind the explosive growth of the US asset- backed commercial paper market between 2004 and 2007. 

Wang (2017) finds that US commercial banks searched for yield after the financial crisis by taking on risk other than credit risk. Buch et al. (2014) focus on the subgroup of small banks, revealing the presence of an active risktaking channel in US monetary policy for the period 1997–2008, as the banks significantly increased new loans to high- risk borrowers after expansionary monetary- policy shocks while charging a lower risk premium. 

Paligorova and Santos (2013) investigate banks’ corporate- loan policies in the United States from 1990 to 2010, showing that monetary policy was an important driver of banks’ risk- taking. In particular, they charged riskier borrowers lower premiums during periods of easy monetary policy than in periods of tight monetary policy. To be precise, the authors find that in the former periods the average difference between the loan spreads for belowinvestment- grade borrowers and investment- grade borrowers was 87 percent, while in the latter it was 117 percent. 

Similarly, based on the analysis of the Fed’s confidential data on banks’ internal ratings on the quality of loans to businesses over the 1997–2011 period, Dell’Ariccia et al. (2016) find that the quality of new loans (measured by the risk rating) declines when short- term interest rates decrease. 

Dubecq et al. (2009) analyze why US financial intermediaries paid low risk premia between 2000 and 2007 in spite of their increasing exposure to risk. They argue that it was possible because of investors’ underestimation of risk, which was amplified by the low interest rates. 

Altunbas et al. (2010), using data for 600 European and US- listed banks over the 1999–2008 period, show that an unusually low- interest- rate environment over an extended period contributes to an increase in bank risk- taking. Analyzing a dataset of the euro area and US banking lending standards, Maddaloni and Peydró (2010) reach the same results: low short- term interest rates soften lending standards for both firms and households. But, interestingly, they do not find convincing evidence that low long- term rates soften bank lending standards. As short- term rates are under stronger influence of the central bank, the findings link monetary policy to higher financial stability. 

Based on 18,000 annual observations on eurozone banks over the period 2001–8, Delis and Kouretas (2010) also present strong empirical evidence that low interest rates strengthen bank risk- taking significantly. In the lowinterest- rate environment, banks increased the share of risky assets in their portfolios. 

Hau and Lai (2016) analyze the eurozone countries in the period 2003–10, as the eurozone has a single short- term nominal interest rate but monetarypolicy conditions measured by real short- term interest rates varied substantially across countries during that period. They find that fund investors in countries with decreased real interest rates shifted their portfolio investment out of the money market and into the riskier equity market, in line with the risk- taking channel of monetary policy. 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 51 

Tsiaras (2018) examines the ECB’s long- term refinancing operations (LTRO). His results suggest the existence of a risk- shifting channel of monetary policy in recent ECB operations. Specifically, he shows that although banks’ health improved following the LTRO, the macroeconomy would have been better off had that liquidity policy not taken place, as banks granted loans to riskier firms, which increased the firms’ default on new credit and worsened the performance of the economy. 

Wischnewsky and Neuenkirch (2018) provide evidence for a risk- taking channel of monetary policy transmission in the euro area that works through an increase in the shadow banks’ total asset growth and their risk assets ratio. 

Memmel et al. (2017) analyze German banks in the period 2005–14 and find that if their operating income fell below a certain threshold, they started to increase their exposure to interest rate risk even though the expected returns from term transformation decreased. 

Jiménez et al. (2008) use micro data from the Spanish Credit Register from 1984 to 2006. They find that in the medium term, banks, because of higher collateral values and the search for yield, tended to soften their lending standards and grant riskier loans.[19] Analyzing Bolivian data over 1999–2003, Ioannidou et al. (2009) draw similar conclusions: banks increased the quantity of new risky loans. Moreover, they charged risky borrowers relatively lower interest rates compared to less risky borrowers (they charged more but less than proportionately to the level of risk). This suggests not only the risktaking channel but also the risk- pricing channel of monetary policy. 

Karapetyan (2016) documents the risk- taking channel of monetary policy in Norway, finding that a lower benchmark interest rate (interbank rates or overnight rates) induces the average bank to grant more loans to risky firms. 

Ramayandi et al. (2014) find the same results for Asia— that is, too low interest rates increase bank risk— while Weistroffer (2013) shows that Japanese banks developed new sources of income to maintain profitability during a long- lasting ultralow- interest- rate environment. Regarding Japan, Ueda (2002, 130) also claims that, because yield differences between corporate and government bonds narrowed, “the ZIRP [zero interest rate policy] forced investors to take risks, resulting in declines in risk premiums in many places.” Similarly, Bean et al. (2015, 61) argue that the ultralow interest rates in that country led to increased investing overseas, including a yen carry trade. This enabled investors to achieve higher yields, but at the expense of exposing them to foreign exchange risk: 

The very low interest rates available on Japanese assets also encouraged investors to take advantage of the higher returns available overseas. But holding assets denominated in another currency also exposes the investor to foreign currency risk: if the home currency appreciates (depreciates), then the investor suffers a loss (gain). The difference in (expected) rates of return should therefore reflect any expectation of exchange rate changes. Many investors behaved, however, as though yen exchange rate 

## 52 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

appreciation was unlikely (or they could liquidate their foreign currency positions before such appreciation occurred). Moreover, not only did investors purchase foreign assets, many of them also borrowed in yen in order to do so, with the result that investor net worth became more sensitive to exchange rate movements. Some estimates suggest that, on the eve of the financial crisis, as much as one trillion dollars had been staked on this so- called yen carry trade. 

I focused above on commercial banks as they are the key players in credit creation. However, the above considerations about commercial banks apply to other lenders and financial institutions as well. Andonov et al. (2017) find that US public pension funds increase allocations to risky assets when expected asset returns decline, which worsens their performance. Kräussl et al. (2017) notes that the share of the US pension fund assets invested in hedge funds increased from 1.5 percent in 1998 to 8.3 percent in 2015. Frame and Steiner (2018) find that during QE3, agency mREITs reached for yield, increasing their leverage along with the Federal Reserve’s purchasing activity. 

Aramonte et al. (2015) find that nonbank financial institutions, like insurance companies, pension funds, and, in particular, collateralized loan/debt obligations (CLOs/CDOs), increase the credit risk of their syndicated- loan investments when longer- term interest rates are low. Their results are consistent with the presence of a risk- taking channel of monetary policy. 

Lee et al. (2017) find that lower US interest rates encourage lenders to grant riskier loans to non- US borrowers in the global syndicated- loan market. Similarly, Di Maggio and Kacperczyk (2016) notice that money market funds changed their portfolios by investing in riskier assets in response to low interest rates. As the returns on traditional money market instruments declined to ultralow levels, a change in a business model was the only chance to survive in the market. 

Bean et al. (2015) note the strong growth in the issuance of leveraged loans and high- yield bonds by US corporations, as well as in the amount outstanding of cov- lite and second- lien loans (i.e., the riskiest segments) in the years after the crisis, while Greenwood and Hanson (2013) show that the credit quality of corporate- debt issuers deteriorates during credit booms, in particular declining when yields on Treasury bonds are low or have recently fallen. The results of Choi and Kronlund (2017) also suggest a risk- taking channel of monetary policy exists. They find that corporate- bond mutual funds search for yield when the level and slope of the yield curve are low and when the default spread is narrow. 

Similarly, Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (2017a) finds that the protracted period of low interest rates in the aftermath of the Great Recession caused the surge in risk- taking by bond investors. In particular, the strong appetite for risk encouraged the proliferation of highrisk debt security issuance, such as noninvestment grade bonds, and a weakening of covenants. 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 53 

Beakert et al. (2013) show that a lax monetary policy increases risk appetite (decreases risk aversion) in the stock market after about nine months, with a persistent effect lasting for more than two years, while Becker and Ivashina (2015) document the search for yield among insurance companies. The latter find that, conditional on credit ratings, insurance portfolios are systematically biased toward bonds with higher yields and higher Credit Default Swap spreads. Importantly, such a phenomenon appears to be procyclical, which contributes to the business cycle and increases systemic risk. The search for yield leads to bigger equity losses in economic downturns among insurance companies that engage in it and to higher issuance of risky assets by corporations. 

Indeed, from 2002 to 2007, spreads between risker debt instruments and those considered more secure fell significantly. For example, the risk premium for US corporate bonds with AAA ratings decreased in this period from 490 to 65 basis points, while that on BBB- grade European corporate debt fell from 390 to 55 basis points (World Bank 2010, 55). 

The chart below (Figure 3.1) shows the decline in the risk premium in the US corporate- bond market in the postcrisis era (the ICE BofAML US Corporate BBB Option- Adjusted Spread— i.e., the spread between a computed OAS index of all bonds with a BBB investment- grade rating and a spot Treasury curve— was used as a proxy). 

The Figure 3.2 also pictures the decline in risk premium in the US corporate- bond market, but using different proxies. The line measured on the left axis presents the spread between Treasuries and securities with investment rating of AAA, while the line measured on the right axis presents the spread between Treasuries and securities with investment rating of CCC or below. 

The Figure 3.3 depicts the low- volatility environment after the Great Recession. Volatility also declined in the mid-2000s when the Fed cut interest

The Figure 3.5 shows the Chicago Fed National Financial Conditions Leverage Subindex, a subindex of the Chicago Fed National Financial Conditions Index. It consists of debt and equity measures— and, as one can see, since 2012 it has been negative, which means the index has been looser (leverage has been higher) than on average. 

There is also evidence for the international risk- taking channel. For example, Rey (2015) argues that the global financial cycle can be related to monetary conditions in the world’s financial center and to changes in risk aversion and uncertainty. 

## 56 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

Ammer et al. (2018) analyze detailed securities- level data on foreign investors’ holdings of US corporate bonds for thirty- six countries for the period 2003–16, providing evidence of searching for yield. They find that the more the interest rate declined in investors’ home countries, the greater the likelihood that the composition of investments in specific US corporate bonds shifted toward higher- yielding investments, with the strongest effect seen at low levels of the home- country interest rate. Importantly, the effects they document are economically significant: a decline of 215 basis points in the sovereign rate corresponds to a predicted change in holdings toward riskier securities that implies a yield- spread rise (depending on the specification and subsample) in the range of fifteen to thirty- six basis points and a decline in credit rating of 0.1–0.5 notches. 

Morais et al. (2015) also find evidence for the international risk- taking channel. They analyze the impact of foreign monetary policy on the supply of credit of foreign banks to Mexican firms, showing that low foreign monetary- policy rates and expansive foreign QE increase disproportionally the supply of credit to borrowers with higher ex ante interest rate on loans (i.e., the reach for yield) and with substantially higher ex post loan defaults. 

To sum up, the impact of monetary policy on risk- taking varies, as it depends, among other things, on the degree to which institutions have skin in the game (companies with higher equity are less risky).[20] However, the lowinterest- rate environment deteriorates financial intermediaries’ performance, which can make them inclined to take more risk (after all, managers and shareholders tend to favor risk- taking, as when they gamble for resurrection). This is why the impact of low interest rates is not always clearly seen in profitability, as banks and other financial intermediaries can remain profitable at the expense of assuming greater risk. 

## **Risk- taking and the business cycle** 

So far, I have analyzed how monetary policy affects risk- taking, showing that low interest rates contributed to the excessive risk- taking before the Great Recession. It is a well- known fact that financial leverage and risk- taking rise during booms. The best example might be the decline in lending standards during the US housing boom in the 2000s, which led to the subprime mortgage crisis (Dell’Ariccia et al. 2008). 

Hence, it’s clear that monetary policy can lead to financial imbalances. Austrian economists have been saying this for a long time. Actually, it is the pillar of the Austrian business cycle theory (ABCT). According to it, credit expansion lowers the market interest rate below the natural interest rate, which triggers the boom- and- bust cycle. 

However, the ABCT focuses on the policy- induced changes in the time structure of production. Its key element is that lowering of interest rates encourages entrepreneurs to engage in longer investment (or more capitalintensive) projects. Maybe it should take a broader view, in line with 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 57 

Lachmann (1978), and incorporate the risk structure, as Young (2012) argues it should and as Garrison (1994) actually does in his analysis of the boom preceding the 1990–91 US recession. Garrison argues that the Fed’s capacity to monetize Treasury debt kept Treasury bonds from carrying a risk premium. And as the lender of last resort, together with policies of the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and changes in the regulatory environment in the banking sector, the Fed kept bank securities from carrying a risk premium. In consequence, risk was externalized, which led the business community to take risk beyond the society’s risk preference: 

During the 1920s, the low time discount signaled by artificially depressed interest rates did not accurately reflect people’s actual willingness to save; during the 1980s, the low risk premiums built into interest rates did not accurately reflect people’s actual willingness to accept the risks of increasingly speculative investments— much less the additional risks attributable to the government’s irresponsible fiscal policy. 

[…] 

Like the time- consuming production processes that were out of line with time preferences, speculative loan portfolios that were out of line with risk preferences generated an artificial boom in the 1980s that belonged to the same general class as that of the 1920s. 

**==> picture [102 x 13] intentionally omitted <==**

Or perhaps business cycle theorists should base their theories on risk, as Cowen (1997) tries to do. After all, the bust results from the accumulation of entrepreneurial errors or from the failure of marginal firms insufficiently capitalized for the risks they took (Wood 1984, 146). And other questions also arise: Is it possible that the lowering of interest rates disrupts the risk dimension of the investment process? Could changes in risk- taking generate the business cycle as well? Are the risk structure and time structure independent, or are they intertwined, as Young (2012) believes? 

Let’s consider these problems. The market interest rate is conceptually composed of the pure time value (originary interest; in finance called, not quite correctly, the risk- free rate), the risk premium (or the entrepreneurial component[22] ), and the price premium (inflation- expectation premium). Hence, when the credit expansion lowers the market interest rate, it may, in theory, affect also the risk dimension of the investment process.[23] 

There are a few good reasons why that will be generally the case. First of all, the expansion of credit draws in marginal entrepreneurs (or business projects). With low interest rates and easy credit, funds are less limited, so even entrepreneurs (or business projects) of relatively high risk profile[24] receive funds (Evans and Baxendale 2008; Engelhardt 2012).[25] Indeed, Schmidt (2018) shows that when interest rates are too low for too long, banks grant credit to less creditworthy borrowers, which might cause systemic imbalances in the financial system and thwart monetary policy makers’ intentions 

## 58 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

of stabilizing financial markets. Similarly, Coimbra and Rey (2019) find that when interest rates are very low, a further decreases benefit the most leveraged risk- taking intermediaries, driving out the more prudent ones. The resulting distribution of assets toward the more risk- taking intermediaries, which have a higher default risk, leading to an increase in systemic risk, and generating a trade- off between economic activity and financial stability. 

Second, the lowering of interest rates, as well as the arrival of credit expansion via the asset market, increases asset prices. That translates into higher collateral values, enabling investors to take more risk on their balance sheet. In other words, excessive risk tolerance leads to an excessive willingness to finance capital goods, which, with market interest rates below the natural rate, leads to malinvestments. 

Third, duration, which measures the risk of changes in the net present value of a project, is inversely related to the interest rates. The lower the interest rates, the higher will be duration, or longer the average time until the cash flows are paid (Blocher and Stickney 1979). 

Fourth, longer projects are usually more risky. In that way there is a link between the lengthening of the structure of production and the risk structure. As Young (2012, 26) notes, 

All three dimensions (i.e., both of the time structures and the risk structure) cannot be viewed as independent. In particular, as the time involved in production and/or consumption increases the risk involved will all else equal tend to increase. As the time contemplated increases, so do the possibilities for unanticipated outcomes as well as the number of probabilistic outcomes.[26] 

But although _usually_ it will be true that length and riskiness of the investment project are linked— as it is more difficult to predict the more distant future[27] —there is no simple link between the two. Long- term high- grade bonds may be less risky than short- term junk bonds. Hence, we could say that the link suggested by Young (2012) holds under the ceteris paribus clause, or when we compare projects identical in all other dimension than length— but projects are rarely comparable in this way in real life. 

Summing up the considerations above, policy- induced changes in the interest rates generally distort not only the time structure but also the risk structure. In real life, there is no such thing as a risk- free rate, which means that although it is conceptually possible to disentangle the time and risk dimensions, it is hard in practice. They are interconnected, and each alteration in the interest rates will affect both the pure interest and risk premium. 

Indeed, a reduction in short- term nominal rates leads investors to rebalance their portfolios toward riskier but also longer- term bonds to maintain their overall yield. They might also take more interest rate (duration) risk. That raises the prices of the long- term bonds and lowers the long- term yields (Stein 2013). Such changes tend to lengthen the structure of production. 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 59 

However, the shift toward riskier assets does not trigger the business cycle itself. Let’s assume that we start in equilibrium, so entrepreneurs make optimal risk- return trade- offs. An increase in risk- taking may thus occur only because something makes entrepreneurs eager to bear more risk.[28] Keynesian economists believe in “animal spirits,” but they do not explain why entrepreneurs are suddenly guided by these instincts. The Austrian explanation for the change is that entrepreneurs are misled by the market interest rate (which is below the natural rate). The point is that the ultimate cause of the business cycle is the increase in money supply, which decreases the market rate.[29] That in turn increases risk- taking, which amplifies the effect of the interest rate cut. 

## **Conclusions** 

This chapter focused on the link between monetary policy and risk. I showed that risk- taking is a very important channel of monetary policy, but, despite its significance, it remains relatively poorly studied. Although its existence has recently been increasingly acknowledged by mainstream researchers and policy makers, they focus on the search for yield during the period of ultralow interest rates. Meanwhile, the Austrian school pays more attention to distortions in the market interest rate. 

In a sense we have a revival of the old debate about the role of incentives and economic calculation. During the socialist calculation debate, the Austrian economists argued that the fundamental economic problem related to socialism is not the lack of adequate incentives but the impossibility of rational calculation without market prices. Similarly, the main problem of ultralow interest rates is not that they create bad incentives (although they do) but that they make the proper assessment of risk much more difficult, if not impossible. 

Without interventions, the market allocates risk efficiently, in accordance with the willingness of each individual to bear risk. However, government policies create a discrepancy between risk willingly taken by market participants and risk actually borne (Garrison 2001). In other words, 

ordinarily markets allocate both capital and labor efficiently— or at least more efficiently that any alternative allocation mechanism. But a market system whose credit markets involve risks that are partially concealed from the lender and partially shifted to others will be biased in the direction of excessive risk- taking. And excessive risks are converted in time into excessive losses. 

(Garrison 2001, 119–20) 

When interest rates are kept at ultralow levels, their allocation and signaling functions are impaired. Entrepreneurs engage in longer and riskier projects. This is because with low interest rates and excessive availability of liquidity, not 

60 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

only do entrepreneurs become less prudent, but less prudent people become entrepreneurs. But even more important is that when monetary policy induces changes in the interest rate, lowering it to a very low level, it leads to the misalignment of asset prices and makes it difficult to adequately assess the risk premium (entrepreneurial component).[30] In other words, low interest rates affect valuations, incomes, and cash flows, which in turn can affect banks’ measurements of risk. Last but not least, the lowering of short- term, risk- free rates makes the riskier assets more attractive. There is a risk- pricing effect as well. 

This all also suggests a significant role of the structure of interest rates. Let’s assume that all market interest rates decline simultaneously and in the same proportion because of the policy- induced credit creation. In such a scenario, the business cycle will occur as normal, as the market rates are below the natural rates. However, the riskier assets are just as attractive as before. Of course, this scenario is clearly unrealistic because, in fact, the new money enters the economy only through certain channels, affecting particular interest rates differently. Hence, the Cantillon effect may also explain the risk- taking effect of the increase in the money supply. 

Although financial stability is one of its goals, there is more and more evidence that monetary policy actually encourages more risk- taking,[31] which can lead to significant financial imbalances, including capital inflows to riskier assets, often located in foreign countries.[32] Ironically, this is the basic mechanism of the portfolio- balance channel of quantitative easing, which means that the increase in risk- taking might be a not- so- unintended consequence of the asset- purchase program.[33] 

Indeed, as Brunnermeier and Sannikov (2016) note, monetary policy works through wealth/income effects— that is, it affects the prices of assets held by constrained agents, reducing risk premia and helping the intermediary sector to maintain the capacity to diversify idiosyncratic risk. Their paper suggests that the aim of quantitative easing was actually bank recapitalization in order to revive risk- taking and overall economic activity. In other words, not necessarily signaling, but rather the risk- taking channel (involving distortions in interest rates and asset prices) and reaching for yield may be central to our understanding of how unconventional monetary policy works (Stein 2013). 

This means that monetary policy in general, and the setting of interest rates in particular, is not neutral to financial developments and thus to the neutral interest rate (I elaborate on this in Chapter 5). This is because monetary policy affects not only the quantity of bank credit (and corporate debt) but also its quality. The nonneutral character of monetary policy should not be surprising, as— given the distinct risk characteristics of different instruments— there are multiple interest rates. Hence, there are naturally multiple possible dimensions of monetary policy (Araújo et al. 2013). 

In other words, monetary policy affects the preferences of the public. In normal times, risk is seen as something negative, and individuals try to avoid it if possible. However, in an environment of very low interest rates, risk becomes more desirable and worth seeking. 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 61 

Monetary policy also affects the price of risk, which is neglected in mainstream monetary economics, which focuses on the signaling effect. Shortterm interest rates are believed to be smoothly transmitted, via the central bank’s signaling, into long- term interest rates, seen thus as risk- adjusted expectations of future short- term rates. But short- term rates also matter because they affect the term spread, which in turn determines the net interest margin and the forward- looking capital of the banking sector. When the central bank lowers the policy rates, the yield curve steepens, and the term spread and net interest margin increase. As a result, bank capital strengthens, which increases the risk- bearing capacity of the bank and hence its ability to expand lending (Adrian and Shin 2010).[34] Now it should be clear that monetary policy is not neutral, as it affects the slope of the yield curve and the profitability of financial intermediaries. 

The postcrisis consensus is that macroprudential policy, not monetary policy, should be used to achieve financial stability. I don’t argue with the thesis that is it difficult to spot asset price bubbles before they burst or that there may be a trade- off between price stability and financial stability. But in light of evidence that monetary policy encourages excessive risk- taking, the most obvious policy recommendation is to abandon easy monetary policy rather than to use macroprudential regulations to curb the exuberance caused by the easy monetary policy itself. As Borio et al. (2019, 31, emphasis in original) point out, “it would arguably be unwise to expect prudential policy to do the whole job on its own,” given that “monetary policy ultimately sets the _price of leverage_ .” 

The risk- taking channel, and gambling for resurrection in particular, is an argument against counter- cyclical monetary policy. The common perception is that monetary policy has a symmetrical effect on risk- taking. But both monetary easing and tightening can spur risk- taking. This is because monetary tightening pushes financial institutions closer to financial distress, which may prompt them to gamble for resurrection and to increase risk- taking. In other words, simply reversing the decline in interest rates might not be sufficient, and might even be harmful, as it may put the institutions into trouble, which may encourage them to take more risk (Landier et al. 2011). 

This suggests that financial imbalances can build even when economic growth is sluggish, resources are not fully employed, and inflation remains at or below the target. Economics is about relative values. The excessive risks are taken when they are underpriced, or when interest rates are kept below the natural rate. As I will show in the next chapter about the zombification of the economy, ultralow interest rates may cause excessive risk- taking and sluggish recovery at the same time (and both these phenomena can actually reinforce each other). 

There is another postcrisis puzzle: the dissonance between sluggish investment and exuberance in the financial markets. This chapter suggests that the explanation might lie in the excessive risk- taking— or, to be more precise, in the _wrong forms_ of risk- taking (Borio 2017). As El- Erian (2016, 134–35) 

## 62 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

notes, “the post-2008 world has been characterized by too much risk taking in financial markets but insufficient economic risk taking when it comes to companies investing in plant equipment and people.”[35] When economic actors are encouraged to take more risk in restructuring and deleveraging an economy characterized by uncertainty, they engage not in investment, but in more speculative ventures. Note that when commercial banks invest in speculative activities, which are riskier but more profitable, their risk- weighted assets increase, but their own capital does not change, which leads to the decline of traditional lending activities and hence economic growth (Papadia and Välimäki 2018). In other words, when banks’ resources are allocated to the riskier alternative, fewer resources are available for traditional lending. Thus, lowering interest rates below certain level crowds out productive real investment. As Acharya and Plantin (2018, 35) put it, “monetary easing triggers a large amount of financial risk- taking at the expense of capital expenditures.”[36] 

This chapter developed monetary economics in one more dimension. In conventional models of monetary economics, the financial sector has not played a prominent role in the transmission of monetary policy. In contrast, focusing on risk- taking channels strongly suggests that monetary policy operates via the balance sheet management of financial intermediaries and their role as a determinant of risk appetite, which significantly affects bank lending and the financial cycle. This analysis more closely approaches the real- world monetary- policy transmission mechanism. 

This chapter made a strong argument against easy monetary policy with ultralow interest rates. Although risk- taking may help stimulate the economy in the short term, excessive risk- taking can aggravate the business cycle and fuel asset- price booms that endanger financial stability in the long run. There is an important trade- off between monetary policy and financial stability. As Summers (2015, 13) puts it, 

It is not that secular stagnation means that the economy will always be stagnant. It is that the monetary policies that are necessary to counter secular stagnation will be able to achieve growth for a time, but at the price of considerable financial unsustainability. 

A moderate conclusion is that the models of monetary- policy transmission should take asset prices and risk- taking, including changes in risk premiums, into account, while a more radical conclusion is that monetary policy has negative long- run effects and thus cannot improve economic welfare. 

## **Notes** 

- 1 Following Knight (1921), I refer to the situations in which the outcome of an event is unknown but the distribution of the outcome in a group of instances is known. Thus, when I talk about taking risk, I refer to exposing oneself to such 

_Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 63 

situations. Applying the concept to finance, I mean situations in which an actual return on an investment will differ from the expected return. The riskier assets are thus those whose possible outcomes are more dispersed. 

- 2 Other financial institutions than banks or individuals could also not demand proper compensation for potential losses. 

- 3 Securitization encourages commercial banks to soften their lending standards, as they do not keep the granted loans on their balance sheets any longer (Sieroń 2016). 

- 4 OECD (2017a) notes that duration of global investment grade, fixed- rate corporate debt has reached in 2017 historically high level, making bond investors more sensitive to interest rate fluctuations. 

- 5 Note that the changes in the policy rate translate directly into shifts in the slope of the yield curve, which also affects the risk- taking capacity of financial intermediaries (Adrian and Shin 2010). 

- 6 Of course, the monetary policy might affect risk- taking, not to mention the overall financial stability, in many more ways. For example, Dell’Ariccia and Marquez (2006) point out also that low interest rates reduce adverse selection in credit markets, which decrease banks’ incentives for screening loan applications. Less thorough screening lowers the quality of loans, increasing risk in the economy. Yellen (2011) notes that “with interest rates at very low levels for a long period of time, and in an environment of low volatility, investors, banks, and other market participants may become complacent about interest rate risk.” Some other effects I discuss later. 

- 7 As a reminder, the central banks’ purchases remove duration from fixed- income markets, increasing the demand for risky assets (Silva and Rungcharoenkitkul 2017). Or, in other words, the buying of assets by central banks alters investors’ portfolios. As a result, investors try to rebalance their portfolios. As the purchased assets, commonly government bonds, are now more expensive, a natural response for investors is to buy slightly riskier assets that are now relatively cheaper than assets purchased by the central banks. This process continues until all asset prices are adjusted. 

- 8 This applies also to Austrian economists. Machaj (2017) is perhaps the only recent exception, as he includes the risk- taking channel as a part of monetary transmission mechanism. However, he describes it briefly and does not elaborate on it. 

- 9 As King (2016, 32) describes the years immediately preceding the financial crisis of 2007–8, “with interest rates so low, financial institutions and investors started to take on more and more risk, in an increasingly desperate hunt for higher returns, without adequate compensation.” 

- 10 Rajan (2005) notes that because managers’ compensation relates to returns, there is typically less downside than upside from generating returns, which encourages taking risk. He also argues that, induced to outperform their peers, managers have an incentive to take tail risks that are concealed from ultimate investors. Acharya and Naqvi (2012) point out that in the pre-2008 era, loan officers received huge bonuses based on the volume of assets they originated and purchased instead of on long- term profits these assets generated, which led to excessive risktaking. Bean et al. (2015) also point out that the rates of return might be sticky because of certain accounting and remuneration practices. 

- 11 Of course, the cut in interest rates and the resulting low levels would not be a problem for these institutions if financial institutions matched the maturity of their assets and liabilities. However, the maturities are not fully matched. For example, since 2007, the average coverage ratio of Dutch pension funds decreased by no less than forty percentage points, while the official estimates of US public 

## 64 _Should we be afraid of venturers?_ 

pension fund shortfalls range from $700 billion to $1 trillion (Boubaker et al. 2015). Domanski et al. (2015) argue that the hunt for duration by the insurance companies in the euro area in 2014 could be not a typical case of search for yield, but rather the consequence of insurers’ attempts to contain the financial risks represented by duration mismatches. 

- 12 In terms of portfolio theory, when the risk- free rate declines, investors have to take more risk to obtain the same expected rate of return (they will move upward along the security market line [SML]). 

- 13 Usually, the carry- trade investment strategy is related to the strategy that uses interest rate differences between countries by borrowing (taking a short position) in the currency of a low- rate country and investing these funds (taking a long position) in the currency of a high– interest rate country (Frankel 2008). However, it can be generalized as a strategy that uses interest rate differences between assets in any currency, even the same currency. In other words, it is a manifestation of the interest rate arbitrage process. 

- 14 For example, McKinnon (2012, 1) argues that “the Fed’s reduction of the interest rate on Federal Funds to virtually zero in December 2008 (a move that was followed by other industrial countries) exacerbated the wide interest rate differentials with emerging markets and provoked world monetary instability by inducing massive hot money outflows by carry traders into Asia and Latin America.” 

- 15 This is why Basel capital requirements are not sufficient to prevent risk- taking. They are based on certain models of risk that are not perfectly accurate. Also, when the price of risk is underestimated, investors will take more such risk, up to the capital limits. Moreover, the assets are not fully dehomogenized depending on their risk levels— hence, investors may take more risk by staying within the same risk- weighted class. 

- 16 In terms of portfolio theory, the mispricing of assets misguides investors. In consequence, the risk premium, or the slope of the SML, is lower than it should be. In other words, the mispricing of risk put investors on a flatter SML than they would have been on otherwise. 

- 17 See also Bernanke (2008). 

- 18 This applies particularly to banks that have access to the Fed’s safety net. Indeed, Paligorova and Santos (2013) find that bond investors, which are not protected by the safety net, are not likely to take as much risk as banks when monetary policy is loose.

Moreover, the relatively restrained risk- taking evidenced by financial institutions in recent years may reflect tighter regulation following the GFC.

The 1980s may best be understood, then, as a decade in which risk externalization attributable to legislative actions and policy innovation gave rise to a substantial but ultimately unsustainable economic boom.”

returns, investors take on additional risk; (3) lower interest rates induce currency depreciation, increasing the burden of foreign- currency- denominated debt.

## **4 Should we be afraid of zombies? Monetary policy and resource allocation** 

One of the crucial assumptions about monetary policy is that it is neutral— that is, that in the long run an increase in the money supply does not impact real variables, but only nominal variables. As Mayer (1968, 159) notes, “One of the main claims made in favor of monetary policy is that it does not interfere with resource allocation, that it changes aggregate demand in a neutral manner so that there are no unfavorable side effects _._ ” In the previous chapter, I demonstrated that this is not true, as monetary policy affects the price of risk and the level of risk people take. 

In this chapter, I show that monetary policy can impact not only the general price level but also the volume of production and its structure. In other words, the monetary policy does not only affect aggregate demand but also entails some resource- allocation effects that affect the productivity. In particular, I will focus on zombification, an important side effect of easy monetary policy that significantly inhibits creative destruction and slows economic growth. 

This is a very significant avenue of research because rising capital misallocation is emerging as a key explanation of the recent productivity slowdown in some countries. Although this pattern was on the rise before the financial crisis of 2008–9, the continued increase in the share of “zombie” firms in its aftermath is significant given that recessions should be a breeding ground for productivity- enhancing reallocation through the exit or restructuring of low-productivity firms. 

## **Monetary policy and reallocation of resources during the boom** 

As mainstream economists focus on large aggregates, they tend to treat both capital and investment as homogenous variables.[1] In contrast, Austrian business cycle theory is basically a theory of malinvestment that examines the effects on the capital structure of credit expansion and the resulting drop of the market interest rate below the natural rate. Such a distortion of interest rates encourages entrepreneurs to invest in processes that generate consumer goods in a far more distant future than consumers would like (as the market 

## 68 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

rate is lowered below the level established by societal time preference). Such decisions thus lead to an imbalance in the form of an initial artificial boom and a subsequent inevitable crisis when consumers’ preferences can finally be fully manifested. According to the Austrian school, the problem is thus “not just that too much investment is taking place (a concept more consistent with a homogeneous conception of capital) but that the _wrong kinds_ of investment are occurring” (Horwitz 2000, 49; emphasis in original). 

Hence, monetary policy, which affects credit expansion, leads to changes in the structure of production because some sectors are more sensitive to interest rates and credit conditions. There is an intertemporal misallocation of resources. During the boom, entrepreneurs invest too much in goods of a higher order, which lengthens the structure of production.[2] But in a bust, it turns out that many of these investments were wrong. It is therefore necessary to reallocate resources to integrate them properly into the capital structure. As capital goods are relatively specific to particular uses, the process is not easy and it might be the case that some resources have been wasted and their value has dropped significantly. The misallocation of resources during the business cycle generates important costs for society. Indeed, using data from twenty- one advanced economies, Borio et al. (2016) find evidence that credit booms undermine productivity growth, mainly through the misallocation of resources. Similarly, Cecchetti and Kharroubi (2015) find that financialsector growth disproportionately benefits high- collateral/low- productivity projects, reducing growth in total factor productivity.[3] And Cette et al. (2016) show that the sharp decline in real interest rates that took place in southern Europe after the introduction of the euro triggered unfavorable resource reallocations, which led to zero or negative TFP growth. 

In short, easy monetary policy may lead to malinvestments and trigger boom- and- bust cycles, in line with Austrian business cycle theory (Hayek [1935] 2008). What is important is that these financial booms cause labor to be misallocated (directed into relatively unproductive activities whose output is easy to pledge as collateral), which “can significantly weaken productivity in the following years when the financial boom ends up as a financial crisis” (Borio et al. 2016, 2). 

Moreover, easy monetary policy and loose credit expansion stimulate overindebtedness. As a result, when a boom ends, economic agents experience debt overhang. This was the case during the last cycle. That debt overhang translated into sluggish demand for credit along with low consumer expenditures and investment.[4] This means there is no shortcut to economic development. In the short run, it is possible to “borrow” aggregate spending from the future. But the future finally arrives. The economic slowdown in the aftermath of the crisis seems to be when the bill must be paid.[5] 

More and more mainstream economists believe that too expansionary monetary policies during the 2000s sowed the seeds for financial exuberance and therefore the following crisis (e.g., Taylor 2007). However, the unintended consequences of monetary policy during the bust are less explored. 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 69 

## **Monetary policy and the reallocation of resources during busts** 

As we have seen in the previous section, Austrians consider busts as the period when malinvestments reveal themselves. Thus the most urgent problem is the reallocation of resources. Meanwhile, mainstream theories of business cycles assume that recession results from a decline in aggregate demand. This is why they focus on stimulating it during recessions; they assume “that the existing production structure can also meet future needs” (Starbatty and Stark 2017, 53). 

The major central banks responded to the Great Recession on the basis of these ideas and radically loosened their monetary policies. However, it is questionable whether loose monetary policy can help with resource reallocation. Any recession involves unavoidable readjustments that take time. As Hayek (1972, 119, emphasis in original) noted, “Monetary policy can do no more than prevent disturbances by _monetary_ causes: it cannot remove those which come from other sources.”[6] Although he referred in that passage to rigidities in the price mechanism caused by trade unions, the message is clear: monetary policy is not an appropriate tool to fix structural problems. 

Actually, easy monetary policy during economic downturns may prolong the process of restructuring the economy, thereby reducing potential growth rates. As Hayek ([1935] 2008, 275) notes, 

And, if we pass from the moment of actual crisis to the situation in the following depression, it is still more difficult to see what lasting good effects can come from credit expansion. The thing which is needed to secure healthy conditions is the most speedy and complete adaptation possible of the structure of production to the proportion between the demand for consumers’ goods and the demand for producers’ goods as determined by voluntary saving and spending. If the proportion as determined by the voluntary decisions of individuals is distorted by the creation of artificial demand, it must mean that part of the available resources is again led into a wrong direction and a definite and lasting adjustment is again postponed. And, even if the absorption of the unemployed resources were to be quickened in this way, it would only mean that the seed would already be sown for new disturbances and new crises. The only way permanently to ‘mobilize’ all available resources is, therefore, not to use artificial stimulants— whether during a crisis or thereafter— but to leave it to time to effect a permanent cure by the slow process of adapting the structure of production to the means available for capital purposes.[7] 

Lachmann (1978, 122–23) also is critical of using macroeconomic policy to get the economy out of recession: 

This creates a problem for monetary policy. In all probability m obile resources cannot be withdrawn and capital combinations will not be 

## 70 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

reshuffled without pressure being brought to bear on owners and managers of specific resources. In some cases it may not be possible at all without actual bankruptcy. […] It follows that any policy designed merely to restore the status quo in terms of ‘macro- economic’ aggregate magnitudes, such as incomes and employment, is bound to fail. The state prior to the downturn was based on plans which have failed; hence a policy calculated to discourage entrepreneurs from revising their plans, but to make them ‘go ahead’ with the same capital combinations as before, cannot succeed. Even if business men listen to such counsel they would simply repeat their former experience. What is needed is a policy which promotes the necessary readjustments. […] It follows that a policy endeavouring merely to ‘maintain effective demand’ by stimulating consumption will simply defeat the very purpose of readjustments by making it profitable for those who should deflect the flow of their services elsewhere, to let them flow where they did before. 

How can easy monetary policy disrupt the process of reallocating resources? In a sense, monetary policy always affects the free market pattern of exchanges. Money is not a veil, and interest rates are not meaningless numbers. Changes in the supply of money always affect the price structure. The structure of incomes and the structure of production also change. The manipulation of interest rates only adds to these alterations. However, in this chapter I am interested in a specific hindrance to resource reallocation among firms. In the next section, I describe the zombie channel of monetary policy— that is, how easy monetary policy can affect business dynamism and the process of reallocating resources. 

## **Monetary policy and zombies: theory** 

Studies about zombie firms emerged with Kane’s study on the savings and loan industry in the United States (Kane 1989). He writes, 

As in a George Romero zombie movie, capital forbearance brings dead firms back to a malefic form of quasi- life in which they attack the living, turning the prey they feed on into zombies, too. In a kind of Gresham’s Law scenario (an analogy suggested by Joseph Stiglitz), ‘bad’ zombie thrifts tend to drive out healthy competition. 

Indeed, easy monetary policy encourages commercial banks— especially weakly capitalized banks (Okamura 2011)—to offer advantageous loan terms to their existing customers, even when they might be insolvent, to avoid having to report impaired loans as nonperforming and realize losses on their own balance sheets.[8] With interest rates extremely low, banks can roll over loans to nonviable firms at nearly zero cost.[9] 

These funds help zombie companies— that is, the nonviable companies that would typically exit or be forced to restructure in a competitive market— stay 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 71 

afloat.[10] This reduces productivity because it enables unproductive firms to persist and makes their profits dependent on cheap central bank liquidity provision (Caballero et al. 2008). Such a misallocation of credit significantly hinders the economic recovery. As Huerta de Soto (2009, 437) notes, “The systematic concession of new loans to repay the old ones delays the painful investment liquidations, postponing, even indefinitely, the arrival of the recovery.” 

Hence, the sluggish recovery after the financial bubble in Japan burst in the early 1990s (Peek and Rosengren 2003) and after the global economy entered the Great Recession may be partially explained by the fact that “monetary policy preempted the creative destruction that could have formed the basis for a new upswing in growth” (Sinn 2016). 

There are four channels through which zombification reduces productivity and economic growth. First, lending to weak companies does not really help the banks. It can disguise their true financial situation, but it cannot fix it (unless zombie companies recover). To the contrary, subsidizing unproductive companies or employing the strategy of capital forbearance rather than classifying loans as nonperforming weakens the banks’ health further because the quality of the loan portfolio deteriorates, reducing financial stability. 

Second, giving loans to weak companies keeps them alive. As their efficiency is lower than average, it reduces the productivity of the economy. Supporting low- productivity or unproductive firms inhibits firm exit. This is of great importance because “the exit of low productivity firms and the reallocation of their resources to more productive firms is key for aggregate MFP growth” (Andrews and Petroulakis 2017, 7). 

Moreover, Sekine et al. (2003, 88) argue that “forbearance lending not only props up inefficient firms, it also encourages inefficient firms to avoid making the efforts necessary to raise their profitability.” There is a moralhazard problem here: as zombie firms anticipate that banks will help them, they lower their effort and shoulder more risk. This may explain why Sekine et al. (2003) find that zombie lending actually reduces the profitability of the supported companies.[11] 

Third, lending to zombie companies implies less credit channeled to other, more productive and creditworthy companies. It makes them potentially more financially constrained (compared to firms in industries without prevalent zombie lending) and thus less likely to grow.[12] 

Fourth, zombification distorts market competition. In an unhampered market, the impaired companies dismiss workers and lose market share. But lending to distressed borrowers, which keeps them artificially alive, congests the market. The result is that healthy firms, which compete in the same market with subsidized companies, have to pay more for inputs (which includes higher wages), cut product prices,[13] or both. This implies lower profit margins in the zombified market, which weakens incentives of incumbents to expand[14] and discourages new players from entering the market.[15] Also, assets are locked into their current applications, which blocks competitors from entering (because new companies must draw resources from old ones). 

## 72 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

This asset locking is very harmful, given that an environment in which new firms can experiment and potentially thrive is a key to economic growth because start- ups bring new ideas and are major drivers of creative destruction.[16] Although existing firms can also innovate, new entrants account for virtually all the productivity growth in certain sectors (Haltiwanger 2012). Lower firm entry may thus result in a persistent decline in productivity and output growth through a “missing generation” effect: new firms that would have been created never appear, which lowers competition (Gourio et al. 2016). The lock- in effect also means that scarce resources are not allocated to the most productive businesses but are kept by firms that do not use them productively, which is a welfare loss.[17] 

In other words, normally, given that exiting businesses are less productive than continuing ones, while new firms, conditional on survival, are even more productive, the process of entering and exiting of companies increases the economy’s productivity (Haltiwanger 2012). But when the economy is zombified, this churning process is impaired, which hampers the pace of economic growth. 

The overall result of zombie lending is thus a perverted banking sector, which promotes misallocation of credit and resources, and curtailed firm dynamics, with both entry and exit rates curbed.[18] This is because “the least productive are able to remain in the market, congesting markets and thus hampering entry and hindering the growth of viable firms” (Gouveia and Osterhold 2018, 4). The prices of capital goods and other assets are artificially supported, which moves the market away from clearing and hampers the process of reallocating those assets (Hutt [1939] 2011). The companies’ and banks’ balance sheet repair is delayed, while productivity, both within and across companies, declines.[19] Moreover, the overhang of the nonperforming loans and the underemployed assets add significantly to uncertainty in the private sector because individuals do not know what the new structure of the economy will look like (Rzońca 2014).[20] Following the rise in uncertainty, the value of real options increases and individuals delay irreversible investments, which slows the recovery when there is restructuring of malinvestments originating in the boom. Sluggish recovery makes the economy more sensitive to negative shocks, as increasing specificity of capital over time, slow adjustments in existing companies and fewer new firms decrease the share of entities well adapted or quickly adapting to economic changes. 

## **Monetary policy and zombies: review of empirical studies** 

The literature shows that zombification contributed to Japan’s lost decade. In the 1990s, banks in distress failed to foreclose on unprofitable and highly indebted firms. Under soft budget constraints, the troubled banks were incentivized to supply credit to weak firms. In consequence, the proportion 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 73 

of zombie companies increased, which is thought to be the main reason for Japan’s long- term economic decline (Kawai and Morgan 2013).[21] 

Weistroffer (2013) notes that after Japan’s asset- price bubble burst in the early 1990s, regulatory forbearance allowed banks to not fully reveal their nonperforming loans while the lowering of interest rates by the Bank of Japan enabled them to continue providing funding to zombie firms to prevent them from going bankrupt. Although this helped commercial banks in the short run, the low interest rates merely postponed needed readjustments. Moreover, as unrealized losses remained on both lenders’ and borrowers’ balance sheets, banks were unwilling to provide new loans that could have revived the economy. In consequence, the ratio of credit to the private sector to GDP declined substantially, hampering investments and labor productivity. This is why, despite very low interest rates, Japan fell into long stagnation in the 1990s.[22] 

Similarly, using plant- level panel data for the manufacturing sector from 1981 to 2000, Kwon et al. (2015) find that zombie lending distorted resource reallocation in such a way that the contribution of resource reallocation to aggregate productivity growth in the Japanese manufacturing sector deteriorated in the 1990s and was actually negative during the late 1990s. The authors’ results suggest that without zombie lending, aggregate productivity would have grown about one percentage point faster annually during the 1990s. 

The high number of zombie firms in Japan is reflected by the steady decline in bankruptcies and low proportion of businesses leaving the market (around 5 percent). As Figure 4.1 shows, that figure is half the size of that in other developed economies (Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development, 2017b).[23]
