## 74 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

It seems that Europe’s sluggish economic recovery after the crisis is a replay of Japan’s experience in the 1990s. Indeed, “in 2013 in Portugal, Spain, and Italy, for example, 50, 40 and 30 percent of debt, respectively, was owed by firms which were not able to cover their interest expenses out of their pre- tax earnings” (Acharya et al. 2017, 1). Urionabarrenetxea et al. (2017) show that in Spain, in 2014, there were 111,259 firms with negative equity, accounting for 5.6 percent of GDP, while throughout Europe nearly 20 percent of companies, which collectively produce about 10 percent of Europe’s GDP, have negative equity. Bauer et al. (2020) find that zombie congestion has a negative impact on the economic performance of healthy firms in Europe. 

Blattner et al. (2018) find that Portuguese banks exposed to increased regulatory capital standards after the crisis responded by reallocating credit to a subgroup of distressed firms whose loan losses banks had been previously underreporting. After analyzing a set of firm- level data for Portugal, covering all firms from 2006 to 2015, Gouveia and Osterhold (2018) confirm the high prevalence of zombie firms in Portuguese economy. 

Similarly, Acharya et al. (2017) show that because of the zombie lending motives of banks, the Outright Monetary Transactions (OMT) program conducted by the European Central Bank failed to revive real economic activity such as employment or investment. Although the credit supply increased, the undercapitalized banks used OMT windfall gains and granted loans mostly to low- quality firms that had preexisting lending relationships with them to avoid incurring losses on their loan portfolios. While these zombie companies mainly used the newly acquired funds to increase their cash reserves, the more productive firms suffered from the credit misallocation, which slowed the economic recovery. 

Andrews and Petroulakis (2017) demonstrate that the capital- reallocation process in Europe is less productivity enhancing in industries exposed to weaker banks because zombie companies crowd out growth opportunities for more productive firms. The delayed balance sheet repair in the eurozone, particularly in Greece and Italy, is reflected by the still significantly high level of nonperforming loans, as can be seen in Figure 4.2. 

Schivardi et al. (2017) focus on Italy, using a unique dataset that covers almost all bank- firm relationships in the period 2004–13. They find that during the Eurozone financial crisis undercapitalized banks were less likely to cut credit to nonviable firms, which increased the failure rate of healthy firms and reduced the failure rate of nonviable firms.[24] 

The pace of business dynamism has recently declined in other Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries as well (Criscuolo et al. 2014; Decker et al. 2018).[25] The Bank of Finland reports that the proportion of zombie firms in Finland has increased over the years 2000–15. Although the share of zombies is lower than 5 percent of all companies, their economic impact is more significant: about 8 percent of capital and 10 percent of labor are sunk into them (Vanhala and Virén 2018).

Andrews et al. (2016) provide strong evidence of zombification of the economy in the OECD economies. They find that the recent productivity slowdown had one striking feature: it was caused not so much by lower productivity growth at the global frontier as by increasing labor- productivity divergence between the global frontier and laggard (nonfrontier) companies. This suggests that the productivity slowdown did not result from a diminishing pace of technological progress, but rather from a slowdown in the diffusion process due to the rising entry barriers and declining contestability of markets caused by zombification and the asset- locking effect.[26] 

Indeed, McGowan et al. (2017) show that the prevalence of zombie firms has risen since the mid-2000s, which has congested markets, curbed the increase in business investment, and widened the average multifactorproductivity gap between zombie and nonzombie firms. In particular, they find that “that business investment by the typical non- zombie firm would have been on average 2 percent higher in 2013, had the zombie share not risen from its 2007 level” (McGowan et al. 2017, 6). And, 

had the zombie share not risen from pre- crisis levels, the contribution of capital reallocation to aggregate MFP in 2013 would have been around 0.7 to 1 percent higher in Italy and Spain, respectively. In other countries, reducing zombie congestion to the lowest level observed within each industry could yield gains to MFP of up to 0.5 percent. 

(McGowan 2017, 7) 

Banerjee and Hofmann (2018) find that the prevalence of zombie firms has ratcheted up since the late 1980s because of the less pressure to reduce debt 

## 76 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

and increase productivity in the face of low interest rates, whether they use broader or narrower measure of zombification. The broader measure identifies a firm as a zombie if its interest- coverage ratio has been less than one for at least three consecutive years and if it is at least ten years old. According to the latter, zombie companies are also required to have a Tobin’s q below the median within their sector in any given year. The researchers analyze listed firms in fourteen advanced economies over the period 1987–2016 and find that the share of zombie companies rose, on average, from around 2 to 12 percent under the broad definition or 1 to 6 percent according to the narrow measure. Moreover, the probability of a zombie remaining a zombie in the following year increased from 60 to 85 percent in 2016 (broad measure) or from 40 to 70 percent (narrow measure). Banerjee and Hofmann (2018) also show that zombie companies congested the market and negatively affected overall productivity. Their estimation results suggest that a 1-percentagepoint increase in the narrow zombie share in a sector lowered the capitalexpenditure rate of nonzombie firms by around 1 percentage point while productivity growth declined by around 0.3 percentage points. 

According to Borio (2018), there is a very tight relationship between the incidence of zombies and a decline in nominal interest rates, which can explain why zombie companies in the twenty- first century have been able to survive for longer than they did in the 1980s and 1990s. He suggests that low interest rates reduce the pressure for these companies to reduce debt. Low borrowing costs thus keep zombie companies alive. 

According to a Bank of England paper (Arrowsmith et al. 2013), the low interest rates and forbearance by banks and government may be responsible for the very low level of businesses entering bankruptcy in the UK since the financial crisis of 2007–8, despite the substantial fall in output. Supporting unproductive firms hampers the reallocation of resources and reduces firm entry rates,[27] and it may explain why productivity growth in the UK has been so weak since the Great Recession.[28] 

Regarding Canada, Deloitte (2018) notes that 16 percent of public companies on the TSX and TSXV stock exchanges are zombie firms. The company also finds that roughly 44 percent of firms aged ten years or older recorded stagnant or negative three- year revenue growth rates between 2009 and 2016, but business exits are at their lowest level in decades. In consequence, more than 40 percent of Canadian business are fifteen years or older, up from just over 30 percent a decade ago. Grieder and Ortega (2020) find that in 2018, about 25 percent of Canadian firms were zombies under the broad definition and about 10 percent under the narrow definition. 

Business dynamism has also declined recently in the United States (Hathaway and Litan 2014). Job reallocation and both establishment entry and exit rates are near forty- year lows, as one can see in Figure 4.3.[29] They remained in a fairly narrow range from 1990 through 2007. The entry rate fell in 2008 and has remained subdued since then. Importantly, that slowdown in the establishment entry has been an important factor behind the slow recovery of employment in the United States after the Great Recession (Ayres and 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 77 

Raveendranathan 2016).[30] Indeed, Foster et al. (2014) note that in contrast to previous recessions which were periods of accelerated reallocation, in the Great Recession, the intensity of reallocation fell rather than rose and the reallocation that did occur was less productivity enhancing than in prior recessions. 

Moreover, the Great Recession also saw the lowest job creation and job destruction since at least 1977, as Figures 4.4 and 4.5 show.[31] However, the downward trend started in 2001, when the Fed slashed interest rates to unprecedented low levels in a response to the burst of the dot- com bubble and the terrorist attacks on September 11, 2001.

## **Conclusions** 

This chapter’s discussion has centered on the claim that monetary policy affects resource reallocation. Although that is always true, I focused on the unconventionally easy monetary policy implemented in the aftermath of the Lehman Brothers collapse. In that way, I demonstrated some overlooked but very important unintended consequences of the ultralow interest rates. I argued that the very slow recovery might actually be one of them because of the zombification of the economy and misallocation of resources; hence, I presented an explanation of the slowdown in productivity and economic growth that is an alternative to the secular stagnation theory or liquidity- trap conjecture. If anything, there is a monetary- policy trap: abundant liquidity and ultralow interest rates contribute to the zombification of the economy, which lowers the pace of economic growth, justifying even more easy monetary conditions and even lower interest rates. 

Another conclusion is that monetary policy is not neutral, even in the long run, as at least some of these reallocations may be irreversible, and they also can lead to hysteresis. I mean here that it’s easy to “zombify” the economy, but it’s not so simple to “de- zombify” it. Zombification slows business dynamism, dragging down productivity growth— and it’s not a temporary and transitional, but a permanent effect— at least until the interest rate increases and the government introduces structural reforms. 

This chapter also touched on entrepreneurship. Entrepreneurs are the drivers of innovation and economic progress (through effective resource 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 79 

reallocation). When interest rates are kept at ultralow levels, their reallocation and signaling functions are impaired. All borrowers and all projects look sound with interest rates close to zero. All borrowers— risky or not— pay very similar rate. Banks and investors cannot effectively reallocate resources any longer. With eased financial constraints, they obtain cheap funds and they either become the living dead or engage in speculative activities.[32] 

In other words, the quasi- soft budget constraints due to low- cost liquidity provision reduce competitive pressure and incentives for innovation and, in turn, economic efficiency and the pace of economic growth (Leibenstein 1966; Freytag and Schnabl 2017). Hence, ultralow interest rates influence not only the allocation of resources but the allocation of entrepreneurship between activities of varying productivities as well (Baumol 1990). They also draw in “marginal” entrepreneurs (or entrepreneurial projects), lowering the average quality of entrepreneurship (Evans and Baxendale 2008; Engelhardt 2012). The negative impact on entrepreneurship is particularly harmful during recessions, “when it is essential to salvaging whatever can be saved” (Huerta de Soto 2009, 424) in order to allow the economy to recover.[33] 

The analysis also refers to the theory of economic growth and its microfoundations. Economic growth is driven by entrepreneurs who allocate resources efficiently. It is striking that despite the Austrian and Schumpeterian theoretical insights that economic growth in a market economy necessarily involves restructuring and reallocation[34] and despite all the empirical research on firm heterogeneity done over the last several years, the macroeconomics literature still hasn’t grasped its importance. The mainstream models often focus on the efficiency of a representative firm, but the misallocation of resources across firms can have important effects on aggregate total factor productivity (TFP).[35] 

The quantity of inputs is important, but multifactor productivity is the real engine of economic growth. As Schumpeter ([1934] 1961, 68) put it, 

The slow and continuous increase in time of the national supply of productive means and of savings is obviously an important factor in explaining the course of economic history through the centuries, but it is completely overshadowed by the fact that development consists primarily in employing existing resources in a different way, in doing new things with them, irrespective of whether those resources increase or not.[36] 

The estimates of the share of multifactor productivity in output growth differ substantially, but they are generally quite high. For example, according to Easterly and Levine (2001), it might, on average, account for 60 percent of growth of output per worker.[37] But what really matters is that all factors of production must flow somewhere. And where they go and how they are used is of great importance. 

A decline in aggregate demand may be worrisome, but it is worth remembering that “depressions are not simply evils, which we might attempt to 

## 80 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

suppress, but … forms of something which has to be done, namely, adjustment to … change” (Schumpeter 1934, 16). Total demand and output are large aggregates and thus statistical artifacts in a sense; in reality, economic growth works through microeconomic mechanisms of effective resource allocation; hence, we should go beyond the focus on aggregate demand and pay more attention to the supply side of the economy. 

38 

The legacy of the Great Recession is excess capacity in the world. Thus, Dugger and Ubide (2004) argue that extreme monetary ease may paradoxically explain the stubbornly low inflation rate after the Great Recession. They note that it prevents restructuring and thus prevents the elimination of excess capacity from exerting deflationary pressure. In consequence, the macroeconomic environment looks like a liquidity trap (low GDP growth and low inflation despite extreme monetary easing), but the real cause is a structural trap with overcapacity. 

Similarly, the quantity of money and credit (and investment) is very important. But economists should not forget the qualitative aspect. Ultralow interest rates and excess availability of liquidity tempt banks into making bad loans and entrepreneurs into making bad investments, which results in inefficient resource allocation and weak economic growth.[39] 

Last but not least, this chapter may enrich labor economics. One of the most intriguing features of the last US recovery is the unusually high level of involuntary part- time work, about 30 to 40 percent above its level in earlier recoveries (Valletta and List 2015), as one can see in Figure 4.6. Zombification of the economy leads to reduced labor- market dynamics and lower demand for full- time jobs. The increase in part- time employment might be the result. 

This chapter suggests that easy monetary policy not only cannot support the process of resource reallocation during recession but can actually hamper it, leading to a slowdown in economic growth. By 

**==> picture [323 x 125] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 4.6_ US involuntary part- time employment from May 1955 to November 2019 (thousands of persons). 

Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019l), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved September 15, 2019. 

_Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 81 

injecting liquidity, lowering and keeping interest rates at ultralow levels, and c alming markets, central banks might prevent a serious depression with high unemployment, but only at the expense of tying up labor and capital in low- productivity projects, which results in “the persistence of the unadapted and unlivable” (Schumpeter 1911, 367, cited in Schnabl 2013, 16).[40] They subsidize the banking system in a way that generates distortions in the price system. It is true that the social costs of business bankruptcies and job losses might be high, but we should not forget about the long- term benefits of the unhampered churning process. In other words, keeping resources underemployed in zombie companies could limit the depth of recessions, but it can add to the length of recoveries (Garrison 1994). The painful process of liquidation and asset fire sales, and perhaps the level of unemployment, is limited but at the expense of more underemployed resources. 

Given that the process of creative destruction is so crucial for the economy (Schumpeter [1943] 2003), its impairment presents a strong case against easy monetary policy and ultralow interest rates. A slowdown in productivity growth means a tepid increase in the standard of living, which can strengthen calls for a populist agenda and more- intense redistribution of income (Herok and Schanbl 2018). 

It seems that structural policies— such as deregulation, liberalization, better insolvency regimes that would make firm entry and exit less costly, opening the economy to increase competition, making the economy more flexible, and enacting reforms strengthening commercial banks’ capital— would be more appropriate during postcrisis periods because the natural restructuring of the structure of production to fit society’s preferences cannot be aided through monetary policy (Andrews et al. 2017).[41] 

If we want to have a higher pace of economic growth, we should not suppress interest rates. As Lachmann (1978, 124) notes, “There is one price in particular which, owing to its strategic importance, we should attempt to make as flexible as possible: the rate of interest.”[42] Although it may be harmful for some individuals, we should support the reallocation of resources: 

Without the often painful pressure of the forces of change there would be no progress in the economy; without the steady action of the entrepreneurs in specifying the uses of capital and modifying such decisions, as the forces of change unfold, a civilized economy could not survive at all. 

(Lachmann 1978, 127) 

In other words, economic growth in the postcrisis era is likely to be shaped mainly by the supply side of the economy adjusting to the new pattern of demand (i.e., the pace of resource reallocation across industries). If true, the unconventional monetary policy might have little real impact and might only prolong the readjustment period or even fuel another asset- price bubble (Weistroffer 2013). 

## 82 _Should we be afraid of zombies?_ 

Although central banks’ response to a crisis could prevent a financial crash and support the economy during the early stage of the economic recovery, holding rates too low for too long could actually hurt growth. It’s ironic because very easy monetary policy is rationalized by the recent slow growth that was caused partially by it in the first place.

## **5 Are central banks neutral? Monetary policy and the neutral interest rate** 

One of the crucial justifications of the zero interest rate policy in the postcrisis era is that the neutral interest rate declined. Because what really matters for the stance of monetary policy is the market interest rate relative to the neutral rate, the ultralow policy interest rates are claimed by the policy makers to be appropriate and to not necessarily indicate very easy monetary policy. 

There are many explanations for today’s alleged “secular stagnation” and the accompanying decrease in natural interest rates, such as demographic changes, a savings glut, and a lack of investment opportunities. But all of them fall within the tradition of real analysis; they assume that monetary factors have no impact on the natural rate of interest. In this chapter, I examine these arguments. I also analyze the proposition that monetary policy does not merely react to exogenous changes in the neutral interest rate, but might actually affect it. 

## **Natural rate of interest in economic theory** 

It was, of course, Knut Wicksell who made the distinction between the natural and market rates of interest. It was adopted later by many economists, and contemporary macroeconomics still relies heavily on it, at least rhetorically. This applies both to mainstream economics and the Austrian school. However, their interpretations differ significantly, partly because Wicksell himself formulated a few definitions, which were not necessarily consistent with each other, as noted by Myrdal (1939, 37–38): 

The ‘normal rate of interest’ must now, according to Wicksell, (1) equal the marginal technical productivity of real capital (i.e. the ‘real’ or ‘natural’ rate of interest); (2) equate the supply of and the demand for savings; and, finally, (3) guarantee a stable price level, primarily of consumption goods. Wicksell assumes that these three criteria for the normal rate of interest are equivalent— i.e., never mutually inconsistent; but he cannot prove it.[1] 

These different definitions and not always clear terminology—t he “Wicksellian muddle” as Laidler (1999, 57) calls it— resulted in debates among 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 87 

economists how to interpret the whole concept. One description defines the natural rate as a rate at which investment and savings are equal. In Wicksell’s words (1935, 193, emphasis original), 

The rate of interest at which _the demand for loan capital and the supply of savings_ exactly agree, and which more or less corresponds to the expected yield on newly created capital will then be the normal or natural real rate. 

What is crucial here is that the natural rate of interest is determined by real factors independently from monetary factors, as it refers to a world in which capital is lent in kind. Hence, only thrift and productivity— the real forces that stand behind the demand for and supply of loanable funds— determine the natural rate of interest (Rogers 1989). Wicksell ([1898] 1958, 84) wrote, 

It is now possible to observe quite clearly, be it only under certain simplifying conditions, the phenomena of capital and interest on capital, as they would appear if liquid capital […] was in reality lent in kind without the intervention of money _._ In the former case, i.e. if capital was lent in kind, there would undoubtedly develop, through the supply of and demand for the available capital a certain rate of interest on the lending market, which would be the natural rate of interest on capital in the strictest sense.[2] 

That description was picked up by Mises and other Austrian economists. For them, the natural interest rate is “the rate that reflects the time preference of market participants and allocates resources among the temporally defined stages of production” (Garrison 2006, 2), or, in more- macroeconomic terms, “the rate that governs the allocation of resources between current consumption and investment for the future” (Garrison 2006, 2). For Austrian economists, deviations from the natural interest rate are related to the Cantillon effect, which basically says that the impact of an increase in the money supply depends on where newly created money is injected into the economy. They note that because the new money enters the economy as loanable funds, the market interest rate falls below the natural interest rate, ceteris paribus. This is also what Wicksell had in mind, as his aim was to extend the quantity theory of money to a credit economy (Rogers 1989). Indeed, he wrote, 

If, then, we test the assumptions on which the quantity theory rests, we easily find that this doctrine would be quite true, assuming a state of affairs where everybody buys and sells for cash and with money of their _own,_ that is to say, neither commodity credits nor money loans exist …. […] [U]nder these conditions the quantity theory is perfectly true and correct; but it need hardly be pointed out how little they conform to reality, at any rate with present day developments in the monetary system […] in reality, at least in the business world proper, all purchases are 

## 88 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

made against credit for a longer or shorter period, and every businessman, however solvent, repeatedly has occasion to seek monetary credit for his business. 

(Wicksell [1898] 1958, 73–75, emphasis in original) 

However, Wicksell’s mainstream interpreters used to focus on price stability. According to them, to ensure price stability, market interest rates must be kept in line with the natural (neutral) rate of interest, and only then will there be neither upward nor downward pressures on the price level. As Wicksell ([1898] 1958, 82, emphasis in original) puts it, 

At any moment in time in any income situation there is always a certain rate of interest, at which the exchange value of money and the general level of commodity prices have no tendency to change. This can be called _the normal rate of interest_ ; its level is determined by the current natural rate of interest, the real return on capital in production, and must rise or fall with this. If the rate of interest on money deviates _downwards_ , be it ever so little, from this normal level, prices will, as long as the deviation lasts, rise continuously; if it deviates upwards, they will fall indefinitely in the same way. 

Wicksell (1936, 102) wrote alike: 

There is a certain rate of interest on loans which is neutral in respect to commodity prices, and tends neither to raise nor to lower them. This is necessarily the same as the rate of interest which would be determined by supply and demand if no use were made of money and all lending were effected in the form of real capital goods. It comes to much the same thing to describe it as the current value of the natural rate of interest on capital.[3] 

Similarly, for Woodford (1999, 35), who revived the ideas of Wicksell and incorporated them into modern macroeconomic modeling, the key variable in the analysis of inflationary or deflationary pressures is “the gap between the current level of the ‘natural rate’ of interest and the interest rate controlled by the central bank.” Indeed, in the standard new- Keynesian approach, monetary policy gradually moves the riskless short- term policy rate toward its natural- rate counterpart (Cukierman 2016). 

Austrian economists reject this approach. To be sure, they agree with Wicksell’s observation that in a monetary economy, the market interest rate may differ from the natural rate because the demand for and supply of capital meet in the form of money, the quantity of which is altered by the banking system. So it is true that when the banking system creates money in the form of credit, this must lower the market interest rate and increase prices (Hayek [1935] 2008). However, for Austrian economists, the general rise in prices is 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 89 

of less concern that the changes in relative prices resulting from the lowering of market interest rates below the natural level. 

And they point out that there is no rigid connection between the rate of interest and changes in the general price level. Equality of monetary and natural interest rates would not imply an unchanging price level, but only a lack of monetary impulses that could affect it. Hence, price stability would not mean neutrality, because in a growing economy there is a natural tendency for prices of goods to decline because of increasing productivity. This implies that to maintain stable prices, it is necessary to lower the interest rate and increase the money supply, and such an increase is not neutral for the economy. As Hayek ([1935] 2008, 24–27) puts it, 

Now, so long as the money rate of interest coincides with the equilibrium rate, the rate of interest remains ‘neutral’ in its effects on the prices of goods, tending neither to raise nor to lower them. When the banks, however, lower the money rate of interest below the equilibrium rate, which they can do by lending more than has been entrusted to them, i.e., by adding to the circulation, this must tend to raise prices; if they raise the money rate above the equilibrium rate— a case of less practical importance— they exert a depressing influence on prices. From this correct statement, however, which does not imply that the price level would remain unchanged if the money rate corresponds to the equilibrium rate, but only that, in such conditions, there are no monetary causes tending to produce a change in the price level, Wicksell jumps to the conclusion that, so long as the two rates agree, the price level must always remain steady …. 

Nevertheless, it is perfectly clear that, in order that the supply and demand for real capital should be equalised, the banks must not lend more or less than has been deposited with them as savings (and such additional amounts as may have been saved and hoarded). And this means naturally that (always excepting the case just mentioned) they must never allow the effective amount of money in circulation to change. At the same time, it is no less clear that, in order that the price level may remain unchanged, the amount of money in circulation must change as the volume of production increases or decreases. The banks could either keep the demand for real capital within the limits set by the supply of savings, or keep the price level steady; but they cannot perform both functions at once. 

It is true that the Austrian business cycle theory draws from Wicksell, as the key element of the boom- bust cycle is the divergence of the monetary rate from the natural rate. However, Austrian economists interpret the neutral/ natural rate differently. They write about the “natural rate,” rather than the “neutral rate.”[4] This is because they have in mind the interest rate that would occur on the unhampered market without credit expansion, rather than the hypothetical rate that would equalize the demand for and supply of capital in kind and at the same time ensure price stability. 

## 90 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

For them, the natural interest rate is the rate consistent with people’s demand for loanable funds and willingness to save. It is thus the ratio of prices between present consumption and future consumption, which is determined by capital investment in the real sector of the economy. The loan rate of interest is just a reflection of the natural rate, closely tracking it. Thus, each intervention in the credit market results in a deviation between market and natural rates of interest and between propensity to save and propensity to invest. What triggers the business cycle is not that the market interest rate is below the neutral rate, but that it is lower than the natural rate— that is, it is lower than it would have been without the credit expansion. 

Meanwhile, the mainstream economists write rather about the neutral interest rate, and they consider it not as the real yield of capital in production but as the interest rate that is consistent with full employment of resources at a nonaccelerating inflation rate. This is why, according to Salerno (2016), the mainstream economists’ perspectives are actually drawn from Keynes’s work, not from Wicksell’s. 

Indeed, in _The General Theory of Employment, Interest, and Money_ (1936, 242–43), Keynes rejected the usefulness of the Wicksellian natural rate. He argued that there might be a natural interest rate for each hypothetical level of employment so that the economy could be in equilibrium with less than full employment. Therefore, Keynes argued that the natural rate should be replaced by the “neutral” rate of interest, that is, the interest rate, which is consistent with full employment, or more technically the interest rate “which prevails in equilibrium where output and employment are such that the elasticity of employment as a whole is zero.” 

One can use the framework of the possibilities frontier depicting combinations of consumption and investment, to summarize the differences between the Austrian and mainstream views on the natural/neutral interest rates. While the former approach focuses on movements along the frontier or adjustments from one frontier to another, the latter is concerned with staying on a given frontier, thereby preventing the economy from sinking into recession (below the frontier) or from overheating (movement in nominal spending beyond the frontier) (Garrison 2006). 

## **Problems with the neutral interest rates** 

The consensus in the modern macroeconomics is that the neutral rate of interest is an useful benchmark for the central banks in conducting their monetary policy and that tracking this rate would stabilize the output and inflation (Barsky et al. 2014). 

However, there are several problems with the mainstream interpretation of Wicksell’s fundamental idea. First of all, the neutral rate is not an observable phenomenon, but a theoretical construct. As Williams (1931, 578–79) notes, “the natural rate is an abstraction; like faith, it is seen by its works.” Hence, it must be inferred from the data and interpreted within a particular 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 91 

model, which might not be correct. This is why neutral- rate estimates and assessments of monetary policy vary considerably (Lubik and Matthes 2015). For example, the three measures of the neutral rate used by Williams (2015) differ by as much as 1.5 percentage points.[5] Different methods of estimation imply that there are numerous neutral rates and Taylor rules[6] aiming at achieving them (Machaj 2016).[7] The existence of several alternative ways to estimate the neutral rate of interest raises concerns that the whole concept is vogue and thus should not be used as a monetary policy benchmark. 

Orphanides and Williams (2002) demonstrate that sampling uncertainty alone yields 95 percent confidence intervals with widths between three and four percentage points in the neutral rate of interest. When we allow for the possibility that the neutral rate may change over time, the 95 percent confidence intervals for a stochastically time- varying neutral rate of interest rise to over seven percentage points. With such large confidence intervals, in a statistical significance sense, one might not be able to accurately say that the neutral rate has fallen. 

The neutral rate of interest in modern macroeconomic modeling is treated as “the real interest rate consistent with output equaling potential and stable inflation” (Laubach and Williams 2001, 1063). Estimating it thus requires calculating the inflation gap and, more importantly, the output gap— that is, the difference between potential and actual output— which is also an unobservable variable. The problems related to the estimation of the output gap are so significant that Orphanides and Norden (2002, 582) state that “even if the reliability of the underlying real- time data were to improve, real- time estimates of the output gap would remain unreliable.”[8] Similarly, Clark and Kozicki (2005) argue that “the real time estimation problems make it very difficult to rely on the equilibrium real rate in current policy analysis.” Beyer and Wieland (2019, 1) also argue that “due to the great uncertainty and sensitivity that accompany these equilibrium rate estimates, the observed decline in the estimates is not a reliable indicator of a need for expansionary monetary and fiscal policy.” Moreover, the finance models of bond risk premia and inflation expectations generate a path for the natural rate at odds with the macroeconomic models (Davis et al. 2019). 

Another well- known challenge associated with estimating the neutral interest rates, as discussed by Laubach and Williams (2001), is the “pile- up” problem associated with estimating a small permanent time- series component with substantial short- run variation, which may lead analysts to underestimate the neutral interest rate (Kiley 2015). 

So it seems that “unfortunately, we have as yet devised no method to estimate accurately and readily the natural rate of either interest or unemployment” (Friedman 1968, 10). All these limitations of the measurement and estimation of the neutral interest rate are substantial and comprise a significant degree of data and model uncertainty, thus being a strong argument against using the neutral interest rate as a benchmark for monetary policy (Weber et al. 2008). 

However, the problems related to the concept of the neutral interest rate go beyond its being unobservable and limited practical usefulness for the central 

## 92 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

banks. Contemporary monetary policy is based on the new- Keynesian approach, which assumes imperfect competition in goods and labor markets and assumes inflexible prices and wages. The assumption of inflexible prices is crucial because in flexible- price models the interest rate gap is zero. Hence, in contrast to the Austrian school, which sees the natural interest rate as resulting from the time preference, a categorial requisite of human action within the new- Keynesian paradigm, the neutral interest rate is defined as “the one- period equilibrium real rate of return that would prevail in the hypothetical equilibrium with completely flexible prices, that is, in the absence of nominal price rigidities” (Weber et al. 2008, 53). Similarly, Woodford (2003, 238), who revived the ideas of Wicksell, wrote that 

it is only in an environment with sticky prices that one is able to introduce the crucial Wicksellian distinction between the actual and the natural rate of interest, as the discrepancy between the two arises only as a consequence of a failure of prices to adjust sufficiently rapidly. 

Central banks believe they know the neutral rate because their models return certain values. But these values are based on certain assumptions and estimates that are relatively fixed over time and might not be correct, as the economy is constantly evolving because of changes in technology and preferences.[9] So even if by chance policy makers guess the correct neutral rate, time lags will frustrate their actions (Friedman 1961). Thus, targeting the neutral rate is a real challenge, and the data suggest the Fed has failed to meet the challenge. 

In particular, as Figure 5.1 shows, the Fed maintained the federal funds rate below the level suggested by the Taylor rule from 2002 to 2005, which may have contributed to economic instability.[10] More generally, Selgin et al. (2010) argue that the decades under Fed guidance from 1914 to 2010 were characterized by more rather than fewer symptoms of monetary and macroeconomic instability than the decades prior to the Fed’s establishment. Similarly, Orphanides and Williams (2002) suggest that underestimating the unreliability of real- time estimates of natural rates may lead to policies that destabilize the economy. 

What is important is that policy makers do not see neutral interest rates; they interpret certain developments as symptoms of a decline in those rates. Wicksell suggests that policy makers should pay close attention to changes in the price level. And this is exactly what central banks do. Relying on the Phillips curve, central banks interpret a fall in inflation as an indication that output is below potential and that the natural rate is below market rates. 

However, inflation may change in response to supply factors as well. This is why, according to Beckworth (2017, 2), inflation- targeting is prone to Hayekian knowledge problem: 

Inflation is caused by both supply and demand shocks. Monetary policy can only productively address the latter, but discerning which type of shock has caused inflation in a particular instance is almost impossible for Fed officials to do in real time. 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 93

And some symptoms of unsustainable booms may appear not in the form of rising consumer inflation but of rising asset prices and financial imbalances (Borio 2017).[11] Indeed, if there are other relevant variables for economic stability than the output gap and inflation rate, targeting the neutral rate is not optimal (Svensson 2003).[12] Christiano et al. (2010) argue that inflation is low during stock market booms, so that an interest rate rule that is too narrowly focused on inflation destabilizes asset markets and the broader economy. This is why credit growth should be assigned an independent role in monetary policy. Similarly, Fahr et al. (2011) argue that an excessive focus on short- term inflation developments can exacerbate the boom- bust behavior and destabilize the economy. Blanchard et al. (2010) also point out that inflation may be stable, but the output gap may still vary, or even both inflation and the output gap may stay on target, but the behavior of asset prices or the composition of output may be undesirable. 

Thus, a perceived decline in the neutral interest rate might result from shifts in regulatory and monetary policies that have been omitted from the models. As Taylor and Wieland (2016, 2) put it, “what appear to be trends in the equilibrium interest rate may instead be trends in other policy variables that affect the economy.” 

Similarly, Cukierman (2016) argues that existing estimates of the natural rate are likely to be biased downward, in particular since the Great Recession. This is mainly because standard macroeconomic models do not include credit rationing; hence, analysts erroneously interpreted the decline in aggregate demand after the global financial crisis due to increased credit rationing as a decrease in the neutral rate of interest. Moreover, some estimates, such as those included in Curdia et al. (2015), suffer from an additional downward bias because they do not include central banks’ motivation to provide financial stability. As a consequence, analysts interpret central banks’ highly 

## 94 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

expansionary policies in reaction to the Great Recession as a decrease in the neutral rate, further biasing this estimate downward (Cukierman 2016). The models of Curdia et al. (2015) and Gali (2008) suffer from an additional downward bias, as they conceptualize the neutral rate as the rate of interest that would exist in the absence of distortions from monopolistic competition, which exist in reality (Cukierman 2016). 

This means that central banks that use macroeconomic models lacking a well- specified financial sector— as was the case before the crisis of 2007–8— might underestimate the risks to financial stability stemming from low interest rates. Actually, in these models not only is the financial sector absent, but asset- price bubbles never occur. 

The second problem with the mainstream interpretation of Wicksell’s idea is that the neutral interest rate does not take into account the capital structure.[13] It focuses on the division of changes in total spending between changes in the price level and changes in real output, or on the trade- off between inflation and unemployment, rather on the trade- off between consumption and investment. If the unemployment rate is too high, lowering the policy rate should increase total spending. However, doing so might cause prices to rise too fast, which would require raising the policy rate. Hence, central banks have to find an interest rate that will balance the problems of unemployment and inflation. Such an approach conceals the problem of coordinating various markets and stages of production and thus reduces the problem of macroeconomic stability to the adequate volume of spending and to the level employment, abstracting from their appropriate allocation of them within the structure of production (Machaj 2016). 

Moreover, the natural interest rate is nothing but the basic rate of return on real investment in the structure of production dictated by the people’s voluntary consumption/saving preferences, so it is determined in the real economy and it governs the rate of interest on the financial markets, not the other way around (Salerno 2017). Hence, any attempts by central bank to target the neutral rate create a divergence between the actual and natural interest rates, which shifts the relative prices and distorts the structure of production, triggering the business cycle and leading to the macroeconomic instability. 

Third, Wicksell’s neutral interest rate is the rate that would supposedly appear in the barter economy as a result of the interaction between the demand for and supply of physical capital goods. However, capital goods are heterogeneous, so they cannot be aggregated and the rate of return on capital cannot be calculated. Moreover, capital is computed in terms of money, so the concept of a capital market in a world of barter is nonsensical (Mises [1949] 1998). 

Fourth, in reality there is not one interest rate, but a whole structure of interest rates.[14] So it is not clear which market and neutral rates should be compared with each other.[15] Cukierman (2016) points out that neutral interest rates may be conceptualized either as short term or long term, and as riskless or risky, which gives four possible combinations.[16] The choice is important because the short- term riskless rate was estimated to be at the zero lower bound in the postcrisis era, while longer- term, risky rates were substantially 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 95 

above zero over the same period (Cukierman 2016).[17] Moreover, as I show in Chapter 3, monetary policy affects risk- taking and the risk premium and thus also the risk- neutral interest rate. 

As Geanakoplos (2014, 314) notes, “The credit conditions of the economy cannot be summarized accurately by a single riskless interest rate, but rather by an entire surface.” Targeting a single neutral rate abstracts from the risk dimension and the microeconomic choices investors make in the real world. Investors compare the market rate of interest at which they can borrow not with an economy- wide neutral rate, but with the expected future real rate of return in a specific sector of the economy (Laidler 2011). The attempt to construct a measure of the economy and monetary policy based on a few aggregate variables is doomed to failure. 

Fifth, the _neutral_ rate may be negative. This is actually the core of the zero- lower- bound problem. If the neutral rate is below zero, but the nominal policy rate cannot turn negative, policy makers assume policy is uncomfortably tight. Hence, the need for quantitative easing and other unconventional monetary policy tools. However, in the Austrian view, the _natural_ interest rate cannot never be negative, as it would contradict the laws of economics. The concept of time preference suggests people always prefer to achieve a given end sooner rather than later (Mises [1949] 1998). 

Indeed, as the Figure 5.2 clearly shows, the capitalists have not become investing in real capital goods at a negative rate. Duarte and Rosa (2015) estimate that the expected equity risk premium— the expected return on stocks in excess of the risk- free rate— has not fallen over the period of the decline in the neutral rate. Even more importantly, the rates of return for the US nonfinancial corporations have not plunged into negative territory after the Great Recession.[18] They declined, but they rebounded quickly, or even increased for a while above its precrisis level.

## 96 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

Regarding the longer timeframe, Bullard (2018) points out that ex- post real returns on claims to capital as measured from the US national accounts have been fairly constant since the 1980s. Brand et al. (2018) also show that the return on capital in the euro area and the United States has been broadly stable over time. That evidence on returns on productive capital suggests that, in contrast to many mainstream estimates, the natural rate of interest may not be declining, and there is no a lack of adequate private investment opportunities. Hence, the postcrisis recovery is not an example of secular stagnation. 

A connected problem is that the gap between the neutral and market rates tracked by central banks reflects the stance of conventional monetary policy— that is, the management of the short- term nominal interest rate. It does not reflect, at least not directly, changes in the size and composition of central banks’ balance sheets (Justiniano and Primiceri 2010).[19] 

Despite all these problems related to the concept of the neutral interest rate, it remains the key element of contemporary monetary policy.[20] Policy makers’ reasoning is as follows: price stability is understood as keeping inflation at a particular target. For a central bank to achieve price stability, it must set its policy rate at the sum of the neutral real rate of interest and the inflation target. Only then will aggregate demand be equal to potential output. The central bank tries to keep inflation at its target because a lower (higher) rate of interest than the corresponding neutral rate would generate excess demand (supply) and inflation above (below) the target (Bean et al. 2015). 

In other words, the neutral rate of interest is the rate that would prevail if output was at its potential level and inflation was stable. The implication is that the policy rate should only deviate from its neutral level temporarily if the central bank wants to keep inflation stable. However, the Fed maintained its policy rate at ultralow levels for years after the Great Recession, as Figure 5.3 shows. 

Policy makers’ explanation has been that the neutral real interest rate itself declined, partly because the inflation rate stuck below the target,[21] so the 

**==> picture [323 x 124] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 5.3_ Effective federal funds rate from July 1954 to December 2019. Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019b), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved December 31, 2019. 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 97 

movements in policy rates have just been responses to the underlying economic forces driving down neutral rates (Gerlach and Moretti 2014). Thus the slow economic recovery and interest rates’ persistently low level have led some economists to revive Hansen’s hypothesis about “secular stagnation.” 

## **The secular stagnation hypothesis** 

The secular stagnation hypothesis has its origins in the Great Depression. The United States’ poor economic performance led Hansen (1939) to suggest investment opportunities in the United States were narrowing because of such factors as slowing population growth and a decline in the rate of innovation, the latter being an issue to which Gordon (2012, 2014) returned many years later, after the Great Recession. Meanwhile, Summers (2013, 2014) focuses on the demand side of the economy, arguing that natural interest rates fell below zero.[22] Hence, when interest rates reach the zero lower bound and fiscal accommodation is insufficient, resources will be underutilized (aggregate demand will exhibit structural deficiency), negatively affecting output growth because persistent weakness in demand erodes workers’ skills and inhibits capital accumulation.[23] Similarly, Eggertsson et al. (2017) define the secular stagnation as a persistently low or negative natural rate of interest leading to a chronically binding zero lower bound. 

This hypothesis should be taken seriously because long- term real rates, which are thought to be reflected in neutral rates, started to decline in the 1980s, long before the crisis and before central banks set the policy rate close to zero, as one can see in Figure 5.4. Indeed, before the crisis Greenspan (2005) famously described the downward trend in the long- term risk- free real rate as a “conundrum.” And postcrisis growth has been exceptionally disappointing, falling short of almost all forecasts, while inflation has been stubbornly low despite the massive monetary stimulus.

## 98 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

Explanations of secular stagnation can be divided into two groups.[24] The first group refers to an increase in the supply of savings, while the second focuses on a decrease in demand for investment.[25] Let’s start with the former. The literature provides several reasons for the increased savings, but the most important are demographic changes (Carvalho et al. 2016; Lunsford and West 2017; Goodhart and Pradhan 2017) and the global saving glut. 

The population, especially in advanced countries, is ageing. If older people have a higher propensity to save, savings should increase.[26] Savings might be increasing, given that increased longevity has not been matched by later retirement. The implication is that people close to retirement need more savings to finance it. However, demographic factors cannot fully account for the slowdown of long- run growth. According to Cooley and Henriksen (2018), changing demographics may account for about one sixth of the level of economic growth in the period of 1990–2007 for Japan and the United States. What is more, in Japan, the aggregate household savings rate has declined steadily since the early 1990s to around 2 percent in 2008 (Tokuoka 2010), so— contrary to the cliché— the stagnant GDP growth was not caused by the ageing of the population and the following increase in savings. 

More fundamentally, the savings are necessary to finance investments which increase later the consumption, so the commonly assigned link in monetary economics between the demographic changes, savings, and the pace of economic growth may be nonexistent or even the reverse. For example, Singh (2010) and Alguacil et al. (2004) examine the savings- growth nexus in India and Mexico, respectively, supporting the predictions of the neoclassical model of economic growth that higher savings leads to higher economic growth (Solow 1956). In a paper more related to the demographic changes, Acemoglu and Restrepo (2017) conduct the cross- country analysis, finding that there is no negative relationship between ageing of population and economic growth in the data. If anything, countries experiencing more rapid aging have grown more in recent decades, thanks to the more rapid adoption of automation technologies.[27] 

Summers (2013, 2014) and Rachel and Summers (2019) argue that the economy might be in a long- term stagnation with a very low equilibrium real rate. In such an macroeconomic environment, the zero lower bound on nominal interest rates would lead to the chronic weak demand. However, the US unemployment rate ended up below the CBO estimate of the natural rate in 2017, as the Figure 5.5 shows, which is inconsistent with the claim that the American economy fell chronically below the potential. 

Actually, if we assume that the OECD estimates are correct, the US output gap has been closed, as the Figure 5.6 presents. It seems that Summers (2013, 2014) misinterprets an unusually severe business cycle as a chronic long- term condition (Hamilton et al. 2016). 

Moreover, neither the share of the personal consumption expenditures in the US GDP nor the US personal savings rate are exceptionally low or high, respectively (see the Figure 5.7). In the 1990s, the Americans were saving

and the countries of the former communist bloc with the global capital market in the 1990s, these savings added to the world’s pool of savings. That “global saving glut” exerted downward pressure on real interest rates (Bernanke 2005). There are, however, some problems with that hypothesis. First of all, the neutral interest rate is not a price determined on the market. As Mises ([1949] 1998, 523–30) says, 

Originary interest is not a price determined on the market by the interplay of the demand for and the supply of capital or capital goods. Its height does not depend on the extent of this demand and supply. It is rather the rate of originary interest that determines both the demand for and the supply of capital and capital goods. It determines how much of the available supply of goods is to be devoted to consumption in the immediate future and how much to provision for remoter periods of the future. 

[…] 

People do not save more because the rate of originary interest rises, and the rate of originary interest does not drop on account of an increase in the amount of saving. Changes in the originary rates of interest and in the amount of saving are— other things, especially the institutional conditions, being equal— two aspects of the same phenomenon. 

Therefore, if Mises is right and increased savings and lowered originary interest are two sides of the same coin— that is, lowered time preference— we cannot explain the decline in the neutral rate of interest by excess savings compared to investment demand. 

Moreover, it is not clear how the rise in savings among Chinese citizens could translate into lower interest rates on the global capital market given

China’s capital controls, and why it would lead to the disequilibrium instead of simply new equilibrium at lower interest rates. 

Another problem is that the data show actually little evidence of a savings glut. As one can see in Figure 5.8, from 1980 to 2016, global gross savings— defined as gross income less total consumption— have fluctuated between 22 and 25 percent (with a short spike above from 2005 to 2008).[29] 

Last but not least, even if global savings rose, monetary ease itself contributed to the rise. Proponents of the global- savings- glut hypothesis blame Asian and oil- exporting countries for increasing their foreign exchange reserves and using them to buy American securities, exerting downward pressure on those securities’ yields. But these reserves could not be accumulated without the increase in the supply of dollars, and they result from the postBretton Woods monetary system. 

The second group of explanations relates to the investment side of the equation. The International Monetary Fund (2014) argues that a decline in the investment rate in advanced economies after the global financial crisis partially explains the drop in neutral interest rates. Indeed, as Figure 5.9 shows, the share of total investment in US GDP, as well as the world median, did decline after the global financial crisis. 

Proponents of this type of hypothesis claim that the propensity to invest declined, which lowered the neutral rate of interest. The main reason for the decline is a lower rate of innovation.[30] Gordon (2012, 2014) argues that the 250 years since the start of the Industrial Revolution, which saw rapid progress, could be a unique episode in human history, as it is unlikely that future inventions will improve labor productivity to the extent that previous innovations did.[31] 

Gordon is right that productivity growth cannot be guaranteed. And, indeed, productivity growth has slowed recently, as the Figure 5.10 shows.
