However, it remains unclear why the pace of technological progress should diminish. It’s true that some of the improvements we’ve seen can happen only once (such as urbanization), but human needs are infinite. The character of needs may change, but unless we live in the Garden of Eden, some valuable 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 103 

ends will be unsatisfied because resources are scarce (Mises [1949] 1998). Whenever technology solves one problem, it creates another need (Mokyr 2013). Pessimists have been consistently wrong in predicting slowing rates of invention and innovation for centuries. Indeed, experts have a poor track record in forecasting, and they often underestimate the potential for further progress. It should not be surprising, as history cannot predict the future and, by definitions, technological breakthroughs are impossible to predict (King 2016). It might be thus not an accident that the secular- stagnation hypothesis gains in popularity after each major recession. However, contrary to Hansen’s gloomy predictions, the economy accelerated after World War II. Similarly, the full productivity implications of the digital revolution are possibly yet to come (Brynjolfsson and McAfee 2011).[32] 

Importantly, as I note in the previous chapter, Andrews et al. (2016) find that the recent productivity slowdown was not caused by lower productivity growth at the global frontier, but by increasing divergence between labor productivity on the global frontier and at laggard (nonfrontier) companies. This suggests that the productivity slowdown has not resulted from a diminishing pace of technological progress due to a lower rate of innovation or a lack of profitable opportunities, as Gordon (2012, 2014) believes, but rather from a slowdown in the diffusion process due to rising entry barriers and declining contestability of markets in turn caused by zombification of the economy and increased uncertainty about monetary, fiscal, and regulatory policies following the crisis.[33] 

Ohanian et al. (2012) show that an increase in postcrisis regulatory activity and in policy uncertainty is a substantial element explaining the delayed recovery. When policy uncertainty is high, even very low interest rates might not be sufficient to induce entrepreneurs to invest, despite the presence of many otherwise potentially attractive projects. As Ferrero and Neri (2017) note, this is because uncertainty not only reduces levels of investment, hiring, and consumption, but lowers the elasticity of investment and makes economic actors less sensitive to changes in business conditions. Higgs (1997) uses the concept of regime uncertainty to explain why the Great Depression lasted so long. Baker et al. (2015) find a secular rise in policy uncertainty since the 1960s due to fiscal expansion and an increase in regulatory activity, while Bloom et al. (2013) show that the recovery from the Great Recession has experienced bouts of elevated uncertainty.[34] 

But what is the key argument is that the secular stagnation would be selfreverting. This is because the lower the rate of interest the higher the present value of the investment project. Thus, with very ultralow rate of interest, “the number of investments there would be with present value above their capital cost and the total size of these investments would be easily large enough to bring the economy to full employment” (Poole 2011).[35] 

Moreover, secular stagnation hypothesis should be analyzed in the global context. In an open economy, the validity of the hypothesis requires that the returns to capital investment be permanently low everywhere, not just in the home economy, which is clearly not true. In other words, any tendency to secular stagnation in one country should be mitigated by foreign investment and trade.[36] 

## 104 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

Last but not least, some economists claim that real or neutral interest rates are low because the pace of economic growth is low. In the mainstream view, although the policy interest rates (relative to the neutral rates) are negatively related to the economic growth, the neutral interest rate is related positively to the growth rate of potential output, because it increases the investment demand, and raises future earnings, making consumers to consume more. The net effect of higher investment and lower savings is lower neutral rate of return. However, as Hamilton et al. (2016, 25) noted, “the relation between average output growth and average real rates is so noisy that other factors play a large, indeed dominant, role in determination of average real rates.” Similarly, Leduc and Rudebusch (2014) examine private- sector professional forecasts, finding little evidence of a linkage between growth and interest rates. 

## **Financial drag, or the monetary policy– drag hypothesis** 

I have rejected the hypothesis about secular stagnation, as it cannot stand under critical theoretical and empirical examination. In this section, I argue that monetary policy can actually affect both the neutral and natural interest rates.[37] I thus agree with the financial- drag hypothesis, according to which monetary factors (such as monetary policy,[38] credit, debt, leverage, asset prices, wealth), and not only real factors, are responsible for the postcrisis slowdown (Reinhart and Reinhart 2010; Jordà et al. 2012; Rogoff 2015; Borio 2017; Gavin 2018; Gourinchas and Rey 2019). In other words, monetary policy shocks and financial cycles have significant and long- lasting effects on short- term real interest rates (International Monetary Fund 2014). According to Juselius et al. (2016), once financial factors are taken into account, the neutral interest rate is higher and falls by less than prevailing empirical approaches would suggest, at least since 2000. Borio et al. (2019) also show that taking financial factors into account in estimating the neutral rate, or incorporating the additional requirement that the financial gaps are closed in equilibrium, generates a rate that is generally higher, and that declines by less in recent years, than the standard, Laubach- Williams estimate. 

The secular stagnation hypothesis thus confuses a delayed recovery with chronically weak aggregate demand. Credit expansion bites back.[39] The problem is thus that policies that aim to fight the alleged secular stagnation, such as ultralow interest rates, do not address the real cause of the recession and may prolong the recession or even lead to the stagnation they are meant to fight. As Borio et al. (2019, 27) point out, “a lower interest rate today raises the likelihood of a future bust, whose materialisation justifies even lower interest rates: in this sense, low rates beget lower rates.” 

What are these long- lasting effects of monetary policy on the real economy? First of all, financial busts cause very long- lasting damage to the economy. A banking crisis results in permanent output losses and may even reduce the pace of growth. For example, Reinhart and Reinhart (2010) estimate that 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 105 

the median post– financial crisis GDP growth decline in advanced economies is about 1 percent. When there is a debt overhang, households and companies reduce their spending and investments, as they focus on paying off debts. Gourinchas and Rey (2019) argue that in the bust, asset prices collapse, and people try to deleverage, which leads to the increase in desired savings and reduced real interest rates. Lo and Rogoff (2015) suggest that high debt overhang (including public, household, corporate, financial, and external debt) after the 2008 financial crisis impeded recovery in a negative feedback loop between debt overhang, deleveraging, and growth. As the demand for loans decreases— because of already- high indebtedness, a fall in collaterals’ values, and uncertainty about the economic outlook— the neutral interest rate may decline, leading the central bank to further monetary easing. 

However, from the Austrian perspective this will only aggravate the situation because with the relative decline in output, time preference is likely to increase. When central banks lower policy rates when time preference rises, it widens the divergence between the market and natural rates. According to the Austrian business cycle theory, recessions are caused by overconsumption— that is, real savings are insufficient to support all investments started during the boom. As ultralow interest rates discourage saving, they lengthen the economic recovery. 

Moreover, both financial busts and financial booms are harmful. Austrian economists have been saying for a long time that booms are periods of accumulation of entrepreneurial errors. White (2012) lists several types of both horizontal and vertical malinvestments that arose during the upswing of the credit cycle leading to the global financial crisis, such as a massive increase in infrastructure, construction, and housing investments. Hence, malinvestments undermine productivity growth as resources are suboptimally allocated. Cecchetti and Kharroubi (2015) find that financial- sector growth benefits disproportionately high- collateral/low- productivity projects, reducing growth in total factor productivity.[40] 

Borio et al. (2015) have recently come to the same conclusions. Analyzing a sample of over forty countries over forty years, they find a misallocation of resources toward lower- productivity growth sectors, notably construction, which has undermined productivity. According to them, the impact of the misallocations that occur during booms is twice as large in the wake of a subsequent banking crisis. In particular, over the period 2008–13, the previous financial boom shaved off some 0.5 percentage points per year of productivity growth in the affected countries. The slower productivity growth translates in turn into lower interest rates. Hence, the lax monetary policy that led to the unsustainable boom should be seen as a problem, not a solution. 

The sluggish growth of productivity after the Great Recession is a key piece of evidence that the neutral rate has fallen. Indeed, according to Williams (2015), the decline in trend growth accounts for nearly half of the decline in neutral- rate growth in the period 2007–14. The zero interest rate policy was hence considered justified. However, the unconventional monetary policy 

## 106 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

and ultralow interest rates might have contributed to this slowdown in productivity growth by increasing uncertainty,[41] shrinking the pool of real savings,[42] directing more funds into government coffers,[43] and zombifying the economy.[44] Reinhart and Rogoff (2009) argue that such behavior is typical after large economic crises. But, as Gavin (2018) points out, the low levels of turnover has been a significant anomaly in the postcrisis period of low interest rate policy, suggesting that that the low productivity growth rate and reduced turnover of jobs and firms are not exogenous with respect to a monetary policy and the ultralow interest rates. Similarly, Rzońca (2014) shows that ultralow interest rates hampers the economic restructuring after crisis, which slows down the growth in TFP, decreasing the neutral interest rates. 

The idea of a secular decline in the neutral interest rate stems from the presumption that monetary factors are “neutral” in the long run, that is, they do not affect the real variables. However, Sieroń (2019a) shows that changes in the money supply are always nonneutral, mainly because of the Cantillon effect, not only in the short- term but also in the long- term run. When a central bank injects the new money into the economy or indirectly affects credit creation by commercial banks, it distorts the structure of relative prices, the distribution of income and wealth, and the structure of production. These changes in turn affect the time preference of heterogeneous agents and other real factors that determine the neutral rate of interest (Laidler 2011). This is very important, as, today, the new money enters through the credit market, or more generally, through the financial markets. This, of course, exerts downward pressure on bond yields and other interest rates. Thus, the decline in the market rates itself does not constitute any particular theoretical puzzle. It is just the Wicksell’s effect in operation, although rather prolonged. 

In other words, the “secular” decrease of long- term interest rates might be caused by monetary policy, at least partially. It should be generally straightforward: after all, the changes in policy rates affect investments and thus the stock of capital and its marginal productivity, having an impact on the neutral interest rates. When the central bank continuously try to push inflation up through cutting policy rates and buying assets, nominal and hence real interest rates would trend downward, affecting also the neutral rate of interest (Borio et al. 2017). 

The asymmetrical monetary policy is also very important factor. Because the accumulation of debt and the distortions in the structure of production induced by low interest rates hinder a return of rates to previous levels, the central banks tend to ease aggressively but tighten only gradually (Hoffman and Schnabl 2009; Beckworth 2017).[45] As this “reaction function” is known to the market participants, they might expect lower short- term interest rates in the future, which exerts downward pressure on long- term interest rates, in line with expectations theory. It’s another paradox of monetary policy: such policy depends on market participants’ expectations, which depend on monetary policy. In this policy- induced Keynesian beauty contest, the central 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 107 

banks try to determine how investors will react to their actions, while the investors try to forecast the central banks’ reactions. 

Importantly, the increase in the quantity of money would inevitably change people’s expectations, and some of these changes would put downward pressure on the interest rates. As Shackle (1949, 115) notes, 

in a ‘cheap- money drive’ nothing succeeds like success; for if once a consensus of market opinion can be swung, by words and deeds of the monetary authority or its masters, to the view that bond prices will go yet a little higher, the resulting preponderance of orders to buy will push them that little higher, and justify the suasion that induced the buying; and thus step by step the monetary authorities can drive bond prices up and up: possunt quia posse videntur. 

The consequences of monetary policy are thus more serious than is often believed. US economic growth in the precrisis years was relatively slow, not despite lax monetary policy and a financial boom, but because of the lax monetary policy and boom, which misallocated resources. 

Note that low neutral interest rates provide justification for keeping policy rates low. Policy makers implicitly assume that monetary policy cannot influence neutral interest rates. Central banks can only (indirectly) observe them and act accordingly. But the perceived profit opportunities that affect the demand for investment funds depend partly on credit conditions, which are partially determined by monetary policy. Moreover, as Tristani (2007) shows, uncertainty about the future course of monetary policy affects the neutral rate. 

The quantitative easing can also lower the neutral interest rate, as it reduces the supply of effective risk- free assets (Filardo and Nakajima 2018). The central banks’ asset purchases also push down the sovereign bond yields, thereby generating capital gains for commercial banks who hold a significant amount of Treasuries. It means that, contrary to the mainstream narrative, changes in “neutral” rate estimates co- move with unexpected changes in the unconventional monetary policy. 

Acharya et al. (2017) point out that such an indirect recapitalization of banks can perpetuate misallocation of credit due to “zombie lending,” undermining productive capacities, and lowering the neutral interest rate. Last but not least, large- scale asset purchases extract term premia from market interest rates, which also lowers the corresponding estimates of the neutral interest rate. All these channels somewhat blur the role of the neutral interest rate as a benchmark interest rate that is independent of monetary policy (Brand et al. 2018).[46] 

Hence, as central banks’ actions can actually change neutral interest rates, monetary policy cannot be understood only as an independent factor that only tries to set the policy rates in line with given neutral rates. In that very process, central banks affect neutral rates, which provides justification for further 

## 108 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

actions. Low policy rates become self- justifying because they create the conditions that validate them, to mention only that low interest rates incentivize borrowers to take on more debt, making eventual rises in rates more costly. Too low policy rates in the past cause lower rates today. This means that neutral interest rates would be higher in the absence of central banks. And all levels of policy rates, no matter how low, can be justified by saying that the neutral rates have declined even more. This is perhaps most clearly seen in models— such as that built by Curdia et al. (2015)—that rely on monetary rules to identify the path of the neutral rate. In those models, the decline in policy interest rates is a sign of a decrease in neutral interest rates. Hence, in such models, policy interest rates adequately track the neutral rates by definition. 

In a sense, Goodhart’s law— which states that when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure— operates here (Goodhart 1984). Economists use the relative level of the neutral rate to assess the state of the macroeconomy and stance of monetary policy, but that rate presents a distorted picture because monetary policy itself is not neutral; it affects the economy and the measure of itself.[47] In other words, to get market rates to align with neutral rates, central banks intervene in the market. Then they use the observable data, which are the results of these interventions, as model inputs that are supposed to correspond to the market- clearing rates compatible with a stable price level. In consequence, central banks react to the symptoms of their own past policies, conflating them with nonmonetary changes. 

## **Conclusions** 

This chapter’s discussion centered on natural and neutral rates in economic theory and central banking practice. Central banks’ actions are based on Wicksell’s contribution and are guided by the neutral interest rate. However, the policy rate is not necessarily in line with the natural rate, which would ensure sustainable growth. It is more likely than not that the neutral lies below the natural rate because it focuses on the trade- off between inflation and unemployment and abstracts from the capital structure and financial imbalances. It is impossible for the central bank to guess the neutral interest rate, but even if it did, it would cause some disturbances in attempting to match it.[48] 

Moreover, this chapter enters the debate about the reasons behind the recent decline in interest rates. It provides strong arguments that it was, at least in part, not a natural phenomenon (due to secular structural changes), but an artificial one in the sense that it was a result of central banks’ bias toward low interest rates. This chapter argues that ever- looser monetary policy was not merely the consequence of a fall in long- term rates, but also its cause. Not only policy rates do not passively reflect some exogenous forces, but they do also shape the economic conditions. 

Taylor and Wieland (2016) provide evidence that deviation from the monetary policy implied by the Taylor rule, rather than a decline in the neutral interest rate, shifted the Fed’s policy rate down. It means that the low policy 

_Are central banks neutral?_ 109 

rates might be not an adequate response to a decline in neutral interest rates, but a result of central banks’ errors, of a monetary policy that has been systematically too accommodative (Hofmann and Bogdanova 2012).[49] 

Indeed, as I showed, central banks’ actions can actually lower the neutral interest rate, which provides the justification for keeping policy rates low, further lowering the neutral interest rate, in a vicious cycle. Therefore, compared to the secular stagnation hypothesis, the financial- drag hypothesis, or monetary policy– drag hypothesis, offers a better explanation of the recent decline in neutral rates (or long- term real market rates). This means that the recent decline in neutral interest rates reflects not just structural change in the economy, but also monetary policy, which, through its impact on the financial cycle,[50] influences the evolution of real interest rates over the medium term (Juselius et al. 2016). 

What also matters is the monetary regime. Borio et al. (2017, 2019) show that in contrast to savings- investment factors, monetary regimes appear to be systemically correlated with real interest rate levels and trends. For example, because gold acted as a monetary anchor in the classical gold standard, real interest rates were remarkably stable, and countries that did not join the gold/metallic standard had a 1.74 percentage points higher real rate on average relative to those that did. Meanwhile, in the post- Bretton Woods era they become more volatile, rising in the mid-1970 and declining in the post- Volcker era— according to the authors’ regression, adopting an inflation targeting regime lowers average real rates by 1.05 percentage points relative to the post- Bretton Woods regime.[51] It is another argument that monetary factors are not neutral, but they do have an impact on the real variables, affecting the neutral interest rate as well. 

In other words, the economic growth below potential might not result from the decline in the neutral interest rate driven exclusively by some deep underlying real factors, but from the adopted monetary regime and monetary policy errors which put the economy on an unsustainable path. For instance, the debt overhang which emerged after the Great Recession is not an exogenous factor, but the consequences of the past policy decisions to keep the interest rates at low level. It means that there is a path dependence of interest rates. As Borio et al. (2019, 29) note, 

if central banks do not lean against the build- up of financial imbalances but ease aggressively and persistently after the bust, they may impart a downward bias to nominal and real interest rates. If, as a result, debt continues to rise in relation to GDP or does not adjust sufficiently, a ‘debt trap’ might emerge: it would become harder to raise interest rates without causing damage to the economy owing to the large debt overhang. […] As a result, low rates beget lower rates. 

The neutral interest rate- targeting leads to three paradoxes. The first one lies in the fact that it lowers the neutral interest rates, which narrows the difference between them and the policy rates, making cuts in the latter less 

## 110 _Are central banks neutral?_ 

accommodative. The second inconsistency is that to achieve price stability, central banks have to manipulate interest rates and the supply of money, which “prevents the natural rate from appearing spontaneously as the outcome of a market discovery process” (van Riet 2018, 194), adding to economic instability, causing economic distortions, financial imbalances and generating the boom- bust cycle. The third is the paradox of policy, which means that policymakers targeting the neutral interest rates adopt measures that, although might be desirable in the short term, are actually opposite to those needed in the long term (King 2016). 

Macroeconomic models consider the created credit as an increase in genuine savings (although they are different phenomena), which would lower neutral interest rates. But such an interpretation will only be a modelers’ failure to distinguish between credit backed by savings (commodity credit) and credit created ex nihilo (circulation credit). 

In other words, under fractional- reserve banking, commercial banks create money, which lowers interest rates, triggering the boom. When the boom turns into a bust, the central bank responds by providing ample liquidity and fixing the policy interest rate at a level below the natural interest rate to help the banking sector and stimulate the economy (each downturn leads central banks to cut policy interest rates to lower levels). That mechanism exerts downward pressure on the interest rates. Hence, given that the boom- bust cycles are inscribed in the nature of the current financial system, it may not be possible to define a neutral rate of interest independently of the monetary regime (Borio et al. 2019). 

The ultralow interest rates, by zombifying the economy and lengthening the economic recovery, can only aggravate the problem of slow productivity. It means that the neutral rate of interest is not independent of the monetary policy. So there is no such thing as a neutral- interest rate policy. The central banks should thus stop setting interest rates if they are unable to get them aligned with the natural interest rates and allow markets to freely set interest rates.[52] 

Or, given that the neutral interest rate is endogenous to the monetary policy it is supposed to guide, it should not serve at least as a policy benchmark. As Blinder (1998, 33) puts it, 

the neutral real rate of interest is difficult to estimate and impossible to know with precision. It is therefore most usefully thought of as a concept rather than as a number, as a way of thinking about monetary policy rather than as the basis for a mechanical rule.

_Are central banks neutral?_ 115 

## **6 Is there life below zero? Monetary policy and negative interest rates** 

## **Introduction** 

The major problem of monetary policy in the aftermath of the Great Recession in the mainstream literature has been the zero lower bound (ZLB). It was believed then by many economists and policymakers that when policy interest rates were cut almost to zero, central banks could not stimulate aggregate demand through the traditional interest rate channel. So central banks resorted to quantitative easing, forward guidance, and other unconventional measures. 

A negative nominal interest rate has long been considered impossible, as lenders would not accept repayment of a loan below its nominal value. If interest rates went into negative territory, households and companies would rather allocate their savings to cash, which does not give a negative return, but instead yields zero nominal interest. So households and businesses could withdraw their deposits from banks, which could threaten financial stability.[1] Thus, the ZLB is binding. As Hicks (1937, 154–55) puts it, 

If the cost of holding money can be neglected, it will always be profitable to hold money rather than lend it out, if the rate of interest is not greater than zero. Consequently the rate of interest must always be positive.[2] 

For a long time, it seemed that Hicks’s dictum must be true. After all, throughout the majority of human history, the nominal interest rate never went negative (Homer and Sylla 2005). Yet the belief that the neutral interest rate had declined to a really low level, perhaps even a negative one, and the observation that holding cash is not costless (so the lower bound is below zero), prompted several central banks to set negative policy rates after the Great Recession, which is unprecedented.[3] The Riksbank become the leader in 2009. Danmarks Nationalbank (DN) followed suit in 2012.[4] 

The ECB joined the club on June 11, 2014, when the ECB reduced the rate it pays on overnight deposits with the central bank in excess of the reserve requirement to −0.10 percent. The ECB made further rate cuts in its deposit facility rate to −0.20 percent on September 10, 2014, to −0.30 percent on 

_Is there life below zero?_ 117 

December 9, 2015, to −0.40 percent on March 16, 2016, and finally to −0.50 on September 18, 2019, as one can see in the Figure 6.1 below. 

The ECB was followed by the Swiss National Bank (SNB) in 2014–15 and Norges Bank (NB) in 2015. In 2016, the Bank of Japan (BoJ), the National Bank of Hungary (NBH), the Bulgarian National Bank, and the Central Bank of Bosnia and Herzegovina also introduced a negative interest rate policy (NIRP).[5] 

While all these central banks introduced some negative policy rates, the design and operational details of NIRP varied significantly across economies. Although all the above central banks started to charge a negative interest rate on reserves deposited at them, only some of them also lowered the target for the key policy rate below zero. For example, NB and the NBH cut their deposit rates below zero but maintained positive interbank rates.[6] 

Moreover, several economies have tiered reserve systems, in which only a share of the reserves is subject to the negative marginal rate. Other central banks maintain exemption thresholds, which means that only reserves above these thresholds face the negative marginal rate. Such measures imply that the average rate and the marginal rate differ. Thus, economies with the most negative marginal rates may not be those with the most negative average rates (Bech and Malkhozov 2016; Angrick and Nemoto 2017). 

The motivation for implementing NIRP also differed. DN and the SNB aimed at discouraging capital inflows and, consequently, stopping the appreciation of the Danish krone (pegged to the euro within the European Exchange 

**==> picture [322 x 193] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 6.1_ ECB’s deposit facility rate from January 1999 to September 2019. Source: European Central Bank (2019), sdw.ecb.europa.eu, retrieved September 19, 2019. 

## 118 _Is there life below zero?_ 

Rate Mechanism II system) and the Swiss franc (pegged to the euro from 2011 to 2015) (Rohde 2015; Swiss National Bank 2015). Both Bulgaria and Bosnia and Herzegovina set negative deposit rates simply as a consequence of their currency board arrangements with national currencies tied to the euro. 

Meanwhile, the official motivation of other central banks was to revive the economy after the recession and push inflation up to the target. The ECB moved its deposit rate into negative territory in mid-2014 “to provide additional monetary policy accommodation and to support lending to the real economy” (Draghi 2014), while the aim of Riksbank’s reimplementation of the NIRP in early 2015 was “to support the upturn in underlying inflation so that CPIF inflation approaches 2 percent and to ensure that long- term inflation expectations are compatible with the inflation target” (Sveriges Riksbank 2015a).[7] 

Of all the unconventional monetary policies introduced in response to the Great Recession, the NIRP is perhaps the most striking and controversial. How is it supposed to work? 

## **How do negative interest rates work?** 

The literature mentions different channels connecting the NIRP to the real economy, depending on the authors’ views on the monetary- policy transmission mechanism in general. The first and foremost is the traditional interest rate channel. Lowering interest rates is believed to stimulate the economy, so going negative is just a logical continuation of the zero interest rate policy in the face of weak aggregate demand and low neutral interest rates. Any arguments made for low positive rates should apply then even more forcefully to negative nominal interest rates. Moving the policy rate into negative territory can help the real rate adjust downward, compensating for inflation below the inflation target. Cutting the policy rate to negative levels should work just like cutting the rate in positive territory.[8] According to some central bankers, there is nothing special about zero. As Bernanke (2016) puts it, “Logically, when short- term rates have been cut to zero, modestly negative rates seem a natural continuation; there is no clear discontinuity in the economic and financial effects of, say, a 0.1 percent interest rate and a −0.1 percent rate.” 

Similarly, when announcing a negative policy rate, the Riksbank wrote that 

cutting the repo rate below zero, at least if the cuts are in total not very large, is expected to have similar effects to repo- rate cuts when the repo rate is positive, as all channels in the transmission mechanism can be expected to be active. 

(Sveriges Riksbank 2015b, 40–41) 

Likewise, the SNB argued that “the laws of economics do not change significantly when interest rates turn negative” (Jordan 2016, 2). 

What really matters are the real interest rates, and they can be negative even when nominal interest rates are positive.[9] Hence, from the point of view 

_Is there life below zero?_ 119 

of mainstream macroeconomics, the NIRP was perfectly justified. Given that lower interest rates are intended to stimulate the economy, lowering them further can only help. 

The second main channel through which the negative interest rates were supposed to affect the economy was the credit channel. The central banks set negative interest rates on commercial banks’ excess reserves to discourage them from keeping funds in the form of reserves and to encourage them to lend more money to the private sector in order to revive the real economy. The idea is that fees for holding excess reserves at the central banks would induce commercial banks to substitute reserves with other assets, including loans to the real sector. The increase in credit supply would then lower the cost of capital, stimulating the general economy. 

Moreover, the fall of interest rates into negative territory was believed to increase asset prices, in turn increasing both borrowers’ and banks’ net worth (their assets would have longer maturity than their liabilities). That should spur lending, supporting real activity. 

Third, negative interest rates exert downward pressure on the exchange rate because banks have motivation then to use their excess liquid reserves to buy assets on global market in search for higher yields. Lower interest rates also decrease the attractiveness of domestic currency for foreign investors, reducing financial inflows and creating the downward pressure on the exchange rate, which should support external demand. This is why DN and the SNB introduced the NIRP. According to Jordan (2016, 6), “The negative interest rate in Switzerland principally operates via the exchange rate channel.” Thornton and Vasilakis (2019) find that the NIRP indeed resulted in weaker exchange rates in NIRP-adopter countries relative to countries that did not adopt the policy. The problem here is that the exchange rate channel works, if it works, only at the expense of other countries. 

The fourth channel, emphasized especially by the ECB, is the signaling channel. In introducing the NIRP, the central banks showed that they were determined to reflate the economy and that policy rates could break the ZLB, strengthening the transmission of short- term policy rate cuts to long- term rates. Thus, the yield curve would become flatter. As ECB President Mario Draghi (2016) stated, 

Negative rates were introduced for one specific reason: when interest rates reached the zero lower bound, the expectations for the future rates in the long term are only that the rates can go up. So with negative rates we were successful in taking these expectations down. 

Coeuré (2017) expressed a similar view, although in a bit more technical way: 

Our decision in June 2014 to introduce negative deposit facility rates restored our ability to steer market expectations and thereby also medium to long- term rates. Indeed, by signaling to the market that policy rates could go below zero, we ultimately succeeded in shifting downwards the 

120 _Is there life below zero?_ 

entire distribution of future expected short- term rates, thereby providing important additional accommodation. 

The above list does not exhaust all channels through which negative policy rates affect the economy. For example, the risk- taking channel and the portfolio- rebalancing channel also work. For example, Bottero et al. (2019), based on Italy’s credit- register data, find that the NIRP had expansionary effects on credit supply and the real economy through the portfolio- rebalancing channel. 

In sum, the negative interest rates were generally introduced to stimulate the economy through lower interest rates (actual and expected), higher asset prices, a weaker exchange rate, and a larger credit supply. 

## **Negative interest rates and banks’ profitability** 

However, all these theoretical justifications, or channels, have significant shortcomings. In Chapter 1, I showed the limitations of the traditional interest rate channel, while in Chapter 5 I demonstrated the drawbacks of targeting neutral interest rates. Chapter 2 argued that spurring higher asset prices is not necessarily positive for the economy, as it delays the restructuring of the economy. Other chapters pointed out the unintended consequences of ultralow interest rates, including risk- taking, macroeconomic imbalances, and the zombification of the economy. I also presented arguments that an ultralowinterest rate environment is detrimental to banks’ profitability (Borio et al. 2015). Negative interest rates are subject to the same critique. Actually, as negative interest rates are lower than low- but- positive interest rates, my worries become even better founded. A negative interest rate could lead to even more risk- taking, to a more zombified economy, and, as the gap between market interest rates and natural interest rates increases, to larger macroeconomic imbalances. 

Negative interest rates could hit banks’ profitability particularly strongly. Indeed, in the debate about negative interest rates, the effect of the NIRP on banks’ profitability has become a central point of contention. Economists generally acknowledge the risk that negative interest rates may decrease commercial banks’ net interest income. This includes both supporters and opponents of the NIRP as an effective monetary- policy tool. The former believe that positive effects on other components of banks’ profitability and the general economy dominate the harmful consequences of the NIRP on the banks’ net interest income, while the latter disagree.[10] 

The negative effects of NIRP on banks are especially large in the case of banks that rely predominantly on deposit funding (Schwaab 2017). Because customer deposits typically pay positive rates, and because banks fear losing the clients, banks are reluctant to lower their deposit rates below zero in response to negative central bank interest rates.[11] A narrowing spread between lending rates and deposit rates implies a decline in profit margins. Lower profitability 

_Is there life below zero?_ 121 

impairs the banks’ balance sheets and their ability to lend, diminishing also the willingness to hold deposits, a traditional banks’ source of funds. In other words, “to the extent that banks cannot fully pass on the cost of negative rates to depositors, a reduction in bank profitability may reduce the supply of credit” (Jackson 2015, 4). Moreover, as the level of the policy rate moves lower and eventually turns negative, the yield curve becomes progressively flatter, which further compresses net interest margins (Arseneau 2017). 

Hence, monetary policy that was supposed to be accommodative can become contractionary because it may impair bank profitability (and vice versa). Indeed, already in the 1940s, Samuelson (1945, 16) argued that “the banking system as a whole is not really hurt by an increase in the whole complex of interest rates. It is left tremendously better off by such a change.” 

Eggertsson et al. (2017a, 2019) find that once policy rates turn negative, the usual transmission mechanism of monetary policy breaks down because deposit rates stop responding to policy rates; so the NIRP is not expansionary. Moreover, they argue that because a negative interest rate on reserves reduces bank profits, the total effect on aggregate output can be contractionary. According to their model, based on Swedish bank- level data, a policy rate of −0.50 percent increases borrowing rates by fifteen basis points and reduces output by seven basis points. Cavallino and Sandri (2018) also show that when interest rates are lowered below the expansionary lower bound, monetary easing becomes contractionary. Similarly, Brunnermeier and Koby (2017) demonstrate that there is a “reversal” interest rate at which lower rates reverse the cuts’ intended effects on bank lending and become contractionary. 

What are the empirical findings about the effects of the NIRP on banks’ profitability? Several authors find a negative impact. When it comes to Japan’s NIRP, announced in January 2016 (Bank of Japan 2016a), the BoJ (2016b) admits that the following decline in deposit rates was smaller than the decline in lending rates. Therefore, the lending margins compressed, creating downward pressure on banks’ profitability. 

Yoshino et al. (2017) find that the supply of bank loans has decreased, not increased, since the introduction of the BoJ’s NIRP and that the negative interest rates neither boosted private investment and the economy nor raised prices. Instead, Japanese banks increased their demand for investment overseas, which increased exchange rate risk. 

Hong and Kandrac (2018) show that Japanese banks faced a trade- off between profit stability and risk- taking and that they engaged in additional risk- taking to counteract the downward pressure on their profits created by the negative interest rates. 

The ECB’s bank- lending survey from the first quarter of 2016 (European Central Bank 2016) showed a negative impact of the NIRP on banks’ net interest income, as a net percentage of 81 percent of banks reported a decline in their net interest income over the previous six months.[12] 

Bernoth and Haas (2018) point out that recent data for the euro area show that the loan- to- deposit interest margins have declined while the flattening 

## 122 _Is there life below zero?_ 

of the yield curve has eroded the profit margins on the standard banking maturity transformation. Many banks still achieve a return on equity below their cost of capital, having depressed market valuation, with price- to- book ratios below one (Guindos 2019). Moreover, the NIRP did not boost bank lending.[13] As Figure 6.2 shows, bank lending in the euro area as a percentage of GDP peaked in Q2 2010 at 106.3 percent and then fell steadily to 89.6 percent in Q1 2018. 

Heider et al. (2018) note that the NIRP is especially detrimental to banks that rely more on deposit funding. They find that the introduction of negative policy rates by the ECB in mid-2014 led euro- area banks with greater reliance on deposit funding to take more risk and lend less because they were reluctant to pass on negative rates to depositors. Moreover, because NIRP raised the relative costs of deposit funding, banks substituted for it less safe types of funding. 

Similarly, Nucera et al. (2017) examine 111 eurozone banks and find that smaller banks that followed a more traditional business model and relied predominantly on deposit funding were perceived by markets as riskier relative to large banks after the introduction of the ECB’s NIRP. 

Arce et al. (2018) also analyze the impact of negative policy rates in the euro area. Their findings suggest that negative rates do not necessarily contract the supply of credit. However, they note that banks affected by the negative interest rates are less capitalized. This suggests that the NIRP aggravates the problem of undercapitalized banks in Europe, hitting the most vulnerable institutions. This strengthens the market power of stronger banks, which reduces competition and the amount of lending. 

**==> picture [322 x 164] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 6.2_ Bank credit as percentage of GDP in the euro area from Q1 2000 to Q1 2018. Source: Bank of International Settlements (2019), stats.bis.org, retrieved September 17, 2019. 

_Is there life below zero?_ 123 

Similarly, Urbschat (2018) uses a large microlevel dataset on German banks to examine how banks behave in an environment of negative interest rates. He finds that while some banks may benefit in the short run via, for instance, reduced refinancing costs or lower loan- loss provisions, many banks with high deposit ratios face lower net interest income and lower credit growth rates. So although the average credit growth rate has increased, the increase was caused by the reshuffling of credit growth from banks with high deposit ratios to those with low ratios, though this is unobservable from the macroeconomic view. 

Albertazzi et al. (2016) also find that in response to unconventional measures, less capitalized banks expanded loan supply less. Moreover, they find that because of the maturity transformation and the duration gap typical of banks’ business activity, unconventional measures aiming at flattening the slope of the yield curve may be expected to erode future profitability via reducing net interest income. 

Kerbl and Sigmund (2016) use an ARIMA forecasting approach and show that negative rates in Austria substantially reduce the profitability of banks, especially smaller, regional, deposit- financed banks. 

Danmarks Nationalbank (2015) finds comparable effects in Denmark. At the end of 2014, deposits made up 69 percent of the small, nonsystemic Danish banks’ total equity and liabilities and only 38 percent of the large, systemic banks. So, all else equal, the NIRP squeezes the profit margins of smaller banks more. 

Amzallag et al. (2019) examine the behavior of Italian mortgage lenders following the introduction of the NIRP, using a novel loan- level dataset. Although they argue that the aggregate economic implications of the NIRP for households are small, they find that when policy rates turn negative, banks with higher ratios of overnight retail deposits to total assets charge more on new fixed- rate mortgages. 

Molyneux et al. (2017) employ a bank- level dataset comprising 16,675 banks from thirty- three OECD member countries over the 2012–16 period and find that following the introduction of negative interest rates, bank lending was weaker in NIRP countries than in countries that did not adopt the policy. 

Not all researchers find negative effects of the NIRP on banks’ profitability. Basten and Mariathasan (2018) study the Swiss experience and find that retail banks witnessed no net change in profitability as a result of the NIRP, but only because banks increased their risk- taking. Higher fees and noninterest income successfully compensated for squeezed liability margins in the aftermath of the NIRP, but credit risk and interest rate risk increased. 

Scheiber et al. (2016) investigate the development of bank profitability in three European countries that have had more than one year of negative interest rates— Denmark, Sweden, and Switzerland— and conclude that negative interest rates have not significantly reduced bank profitability and net interest income. 

124 _Is there life below zero?_ 

Eisenschmidt and Smets (2018) show that negative rates in the euro area have been expansionary while the negative effects on the interest rate margin and profitability were generally offset by the positive impact of lower market rates on asset values and loan- loss provisions. 

Lopez et al. (2018) use a cross- country panel of over 5,100 banks in twenty- seven countries over the period from 2010 to 2016, finding little overall impact of negative nominal rates on bank profitability, as a decline in net interest income was largely offset by increases in noninterest income stemming from, for example, increased fees and other income, such as capital gains on securities. 

However, all these results lead to a question about the sustainability of banks’ profitability under negative rates as one- off gains fade away. For instance, the effects of higher asset prices weaken over time relative to the negative impact of a narrowed interest margin, especially for banks in the eurozone, as “the aggregate balance sheet of euro area banks suggests that lending is about 6–7 times more important than investments for profitability” (Jobst and Lin 2016, 15). There is also a question of the long- term sustainability of higher asset prices triggered by the unconventional monetary policy. Rossi (2019) shows that the SNB’s NIRP prompted an increasing number of people to allocate their savings to real estate, whose prices rose to elevated levels, threatening the outbreak of a housing crisis. 

More generally, the fact that commercial banks were able to counteract to a large extent the negative effect of the NIRP does not mean that the NIRP did not impair banks’ lending margins, creating downward pressure on total profitability. Some mitigation measures, such as increasing fees, have clear limits (the fees are generally imposed as a flat rate, not in proportion to the amount of deposits), while more risk- taking, although an intended consequence of monetary- policy easing, threatens financial instability.[14] 

What is crucial here is that the negative interest rates may indeed boost lending, but that could result in higher loan- impairment charges in the future when many of the newly granted loans turn out to be nonperforming. As recognized by Noizet (2016), what really matters is not the net interest income, but the risk- adjusted net interest income, which is a more proper indicator of banks’ profitability. Unfortunately, mainstream economists focus on the former indicator, though banks can easily increase lending to higherrisk borrowers more than to lower- risk borrowers. If this happens, margin compression seems to not occur, but this is just an accounting illusion, resulting from neglecting the risk profile of the new loans. More generally, when banks extend new loans— for example, induced by low or even negative interest rates— their profitability should increase temporarily, simply because potential losses materialize only a few years later when loans become nonperforming. 

The usefulness of data analyzed above in the assessment of negative interest rates is limited. Negative interest rates were not fully implemented; the rates were only modestly negative for a limited time.[15] And they were not 

_Is there life below zero?_ 125 

set below the effective lower bound. Moreover, in many cases, the negative rates were applied to only a small fraction of deposits, while central banks tried to minimize or counterbalance the effects of a negative interest rate. For example, they set the threshold at which reserves are charged the negative deposit rate relatively high, or they imposed negative rates on the “marginal” deposits.[16] If the negative rates were more powerfully implemented, their effects could be more significant and probably more detrimental to banks’ profitability. 

It might be useful to analyze Japan after the burst of its asset- price bubble in 1991. The following cuts in interest rates by the BoJ down to zero in 1999 compressed the lending rates and squeezed commercial banks’ profit margins. With compressed lending rates and the zero lower bound on deposit rates, and hence low profit margins on new commercial lending, banks were unable to cover losses and write off bad loans out of current earnings. And they had motivation to curb their commercial lending (Goyal and McKinnon 2003). Indeed, although monetary policy was accommodative, bank credit to the private sector in absolute terms shrank in the late 1990s, and, as a share of GDP, it plunged in the early 2000s, following the introduction of the ZIRP, as Figure 6.3 shows. Instead, banks increased their portfolio allocation to government bonds, which supported government borrowing, thereby crowding out private investments and weakening the incentives for structural reforms. The figure also reveals that in the euro area, neither the ZIRP nor the NIRP managed to revive banks’ credit to the private sector. 

More generally, the problem with using as a macroeconomic tool interest rate policy in general, and NIRP in particular, is that it focuses on the

## 126 _Is there life below zero?_ 

demand side of bank lending, abstracting from the supply side (Michail 2018). Although lower policy interest rates, including negative rates, are supposed to reduce borrowing costs and thus support spending, they do not have to support bank lending, as they might not impact the sector’s supply- side factors. According to Goodhart (2013), the main factors influencing the supply of bank loans to the private sector are the targeted return on equity, the marginal bank- funding cost spread over the policy rate, and the perceived probability of borrowers’ default. He points out that a reduction in the policy rate does not guarantee an equivalent fall in bank funding costs,[17] and he argues that the NIRP might not be enough to generate new lending (Goodhart 2013). And, as we have already seen, the NIRP may actually narrow interest rate margins. 

## **Other problems with negative interest rates** 

In this section, I focus on several more issues with negative interest rates. Although the debate in the mainstream literature focuses on the impact on banks’ profitability, this is not the only and maybe even not the most important problem. 

First of all, negative interest rates risk significantly disrupting the financial system. Sufficiently subzero nominal rates can drive people to accumulate cash, shrinking banks’ deposit balances. When faced with fees or negative interest rates on deposits, people can always choose cash, which bears a zero nominal interest rate. As using cash entails some storage, insurance, and transportation costs, the effective lower bound is below zero. However, it cannot be very far below zero.[18] Hence, lowering nominal interest rates below the effective lower bound increases demand for cash, which reduces the money multiplier and limits the credit and money supply in the economy, a result opposite to the aim of the NIRP. 

All these factors point to the key limits of the NIRP. If negative policy rates do not transmit to deposit rates, they narrow banks’ profits. But if they do, then deposits suffer. If depositors withdraw their money, banks’ deposit base shrinks, which could destabilize the banking system. As Borio and Zabai (2016, 20) put it, 

If negative rates do _not_ transmit to lending rates, they cannot boost the demand for loans. If they do transmit to lower lending rates but _not_ to deposit rates, they squeeze banks’ profits, over time possibly undermining their willingness and ability to lend. And if they transmit to both lending _and_ deposit rates, they risk unsettling the deposit base, making it harder for banks to attract funds. 

Bech and Malkhozov (2016) point out the same fundamental problem with the NIRP. To boost lending and aggregate demand, negative rates have to feed into lending rates for households and companies. But if they do not 

_Is there life below zero?_ 127 

transmit to deposit rates also, banks’ profit margins will narrow, impairing their capital. And if negative rates are also imposed on deposits, the retail deposit base may be destabilized. “In either case,” Bech and Malkhozov (pp. 40–41) write, “the viability of banks’ business model as financial intermediaries may be brought into question.”[19] 

According to Waller (2016), this fundamental problem of the NIRP results from the fact that negative policy interest rates are just a tax on banks’ reserves. Like all taxes, it has to be borne by someone. He distinguishes three scenarios. In the first one, the banks do not pass on the tax; they accept lower after- tax profits. Their share prices and balance sheets suffer. In the second scenario, the banks pass the tax on to depositors by paying a lower interest rate on deposits or charging them higher fees. In that case, the disposable income of depositors is reduced. Finally, the banks can pass the tax on to borrowers by charging them a higher interest rate (or higher fees) on loans. Borrowing costs are higher, which is the opposite of the outcome desired by the central banks.[20] As Waller (2016) concludes, just as taxes do not stimulate the economy, in none of the above scenarios does the NIRP stimulate spending on goods and services.[21] 

Another fundamental problem related to the NIRP is that the negative interest rates cannot induce commercial banks to lend reserves out, as banks simply do not lend reserves to the nonfinancial private sector (which has no accounts at the central bank) but only to other banks. The reduction of excess reserves as a result of granting a loan by one bank will mean an increase in the reserves of another bank (on the “hot potato” principle). In other words, the banking system cannot reduce the aggregate amount of central bank reserves no matter how many loans banks grant, because the funds lent by one bank are eventually redeposited at another bank. Therefore, the negative policy rates are not able to reduce the reserves of the banking sector as a whole through an increase in loans (Anderson and Liu 2013). Surely, in response to the NIRP, some banks may try to grant more loans, but they will make loans they would not otherwise grant (if that were not the case, the NIRP would be unnecessary), which means loans of worse credit quality whose returns do not fully compensate for the risk of default. 

The fact that lowering the deposit rate into negative territory is not effective at encouraging greater borrowing within the euro area is not surprising. Taxing reserves may instead lead banks to reduce them by paying off loans from the central bank and borrowing less from the central bank. This puts upward pressure on interbank interest rates, which is contractionary rather than accommodative (Coeuré 2014). 

What is striking is that the ECB, and other central banks, imposed a tax on central bank reserves, even though it determines the aggregate amount of such reserves. This suggests that the motivation behind the ECB’s NIRP differed from the official aim of supporting lending to the real economy.[22] 

Negative interest rates also entail some important frictions, which negatively affect the financial sector. In particular, money market funds might be 

## 128 _Is there life below zero?_ 

unable to maintain the value of a share in the fund at par or very close to par or even just deliver attractive returns while remaining safe and liquid, which would prompt large outflows and closures and reduce liquidity in a key segment of the financial system. The negative interest rates also pose challenges for insurance companies and pension funds, which often promise to reach fixed rate of returns; the negative rates prompt them to search for yields. Many financial institutions abandon traditional business models and adopt new ones more appropriate in an environment of low interest rates but also riskier. For example, banks and insurers may become asset managers active in international markets. As Fukuda (2017) points out, after the BoJ’s announcement of NIRP, Japanese life insurance companies that lost investment opportunities in domestic markets expanded their investment in emerging markets in Asia, risky but potentially highly profitable investment destinations. Similarly, Bräuning and Wu (2017) analyze monetary- policy transmission in the euro area from 2009 to 2016, finding that in the NIRP period, banks and other market participants invested more in longer- maturity and riskier assets, consistent with reaching- for- yield behavior. 

And, as Jackson (2015) points out, risk management may be affected in a world of negative interest rates since most option- pricing models either do not work or do not work well with negative interest rates. Finally, Garbade and McAndrews (2013) and McAndrews (2015) note that negative interest rates could trigger a variety of interest- avoidance strategies that, even if individually beneficial, are socially unproductive. For example, negative interest rates could induce people to buy safes or to excessively prepay debts or tax obligations to earn a zero return during the prepayment period.[23] 

Additionally, the NIRP may negatively affect market participants’ sentiment. People may interpret the introduction of the negative policy rates as reflecting the central bank’s gloomy outlook or even desperation. They may then start to worry about the state of the economy and the impact of the NIRP.[24] 

Negative interest rates also reduce the incentive for fiscal consolidation and structural reforms. As I discuss in detail in Chapter 7, with interest rates at ultralow levels, governments do not feel pressure to reduce their debt and introduce fiscal discipline. Hence, the NIRP continues and actively encourages the unsustainable model of economic growth based on debt and asset price inflation that caused the Great Recession (Palley 2016). 

Last but not least, the NIRP also has adverse distributional effects. First, as noted above, the negative interest rates harm smaller banks more than larger banks. This distorts the structure of the banking sector relative to what would exist without negative policy interest rates. Second, given that central bank reserves are not held equally across the members of the euro area, negative interest rates on excess reserves affect banks from countries with large surplus positions within Target 2, such as Germany, more than banks from countries with large deficit positions within Target 2, such as Italy (Angrick and Nemoto 2017). Third, those who allocate their savings into assets will 

_Is there life below zero?_ 129 

benefit from higher asset prices at the expense of those who save in bank accounts with very low, or even negative, interest rates. Fourth, people’s abilities to avoid negative interest rates differ. So when banks decide to pass the costs on to account holders, wealthier individuals can avoid negative interest rates more easily (by, for instance, shifting funds into gold and foreign currencies or by paying bills and taxes well in advance). 

## **Negative market rates** 

We have so far focused on negative policy rates. But the NIRP, together with other unconventional monetary policies and other factors, has created an unprecedented situation in which nominal interest rates are negative in several European countries across a range of maturities in the benchmark yield curve, from overnight to five- or even ten- year maturities (see, for example, Figure 6.4, which displays yields on ten- year German bonds that turned negative in 2016 and then again in 2019). The pool of negative- yielding government bonds around the world is already above $15 trillion, and about one- quarter of the world’s GDP is produced in countries with negative interest rates (as of September 17, 2019) (Mourmouras 2016). Does it make any sense? Do the negative bond yields deny the laws of economics? 

Not necessarily. Generally, each case of negative market rates has its own special story and should be analyzed separately. However, we can provide a few reasonable explanations for why market participants do sometimes accept negative nominal interest rates. Let us separate negative deposit rates and 

**==> picture [322 x 205] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure 6.4_ Yields on ten- year German bonds from 2009 to 2019. Source: Stooq (2019), stooq.com, retrieved September 17, 2019. 

## 130 _Is there life below zero?_ 

negative lending rates, or bond yields, and start with the former. In some countries, commercial banks simply have to maintain excessive reserves in a negative- interest- bearing account. Keeping money in bank deposits is not obligatory, but banks offer productive services related to storing money, making transfers, and so on, so negative deposit rates are fully justified. The fact that it is possible to collect fees for keeping deposits is not surprising to Austrian economists, who propose separating deposit banking and loan banking. 

Negative bond yields are a bit more problematic, but they also do not violate economic laws. Sometimes the negative yields result from specific government regulations. For example, during the Great Depression, US Treasury bonds and notes had negative nominal yields as they approached maturity, but this was because “in addition to making coupon payments, Treasury securities were options that gave the owner the right to buy a new security on a future date” (Cecchetti 1987, abstract).[25] 

Another example is the negative yields of five- and ten- year inflationindexed US Treasuries in 2011–12 (see Figure 6.5). As investors anticipated high inflation during the life of the bonds, they were willing to pay high prices, resulting in negative yields, because these securities offered protection against inflation. In other words, investors demanded these securities because at maturity, they would receive a nominal value plus the accumulated inflation at maturity (Anderson and Liu 2013). 

However, it seems that the main reason for accepting negative yields is a flight to safety. If some market participants have to keep somewhere large sums of money above the level subject to deposit guarantees, they can choose some safe government bonds, for example German bunds because of the very low odds of Germany defaulting on its debt obligations. 

Moreover, investors may accept negative yields when they expect the currency to appreciate. After all, the yield in foreign currency is only one of
