_Figure 6.5_ Yields on ten- year inflation- indexed Treasuries and on five- year inflationindexed Treasuries from January 2003 to September 2019. Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019c, 2019p), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved September 17, 2019. 

_Is there life below zero?_ 131 

the components of the rate of return when investing in foreign securities. Changes in the exchange rate are also very important. If investors expect, for example, the breakup of the euro area and the reintroduction of a strong German mark, paying for the opportunity to invest in German government bonds does not seem senseless. Similarly, from the point of view of, for example, a Russian investor, the need to pay a small fee for holding German bonds does not seem unreasonable in light of the risk of the ruble’s depreciation. If, for example, the ruble drops against the euro by 50 percent, then from a Russian perspective it is worth having bunds with slightly negative yields. 

Therefore, one can look at this type of investment as a purchase of a call option for a given currency. Investors have to pay a certain previously known fee (option premium), but if a given event occurs (e.g., the breakup of the eurozone or the depreciation of the ruble) it can bring significant profits. Thus the negative market interest rates reflect an insurance premium rather than negative time preference (Block 1978). 

Of course, there are other possible reasons for negative yields: loss of trust in the banking system (government bonds may be treated as substitutes for deposits guaranteed only up to a certain level), the need to acquire a given security in order to close a specific transaction such as a repurchase agreement (lacking the security would be more expensive than bearing the negative interest rate) (Fleming and Garbade 2004), or regulations that oblige insurance companies and pension funds to allocate a certain amount of funds to government bonds, regardless of their yield. Last but not least, investors can buy bonds with a negative yield to maturity if they expect they could sell them at higher prices to other investors or to central banks, which increased their presence in bond markets in the aftermath of the Great Recession. 

What is important is that reported negative yields do not necessarily mean that issuers of such bonds may pay back less than they borrowed. Negative yield to maturity does not mean negative coupon payments. Negative yields only mean that bonds are traded at such prices that investors who buy them at these prices and hold them until maturity will incur a nominal loss on the investment.[26] 

Hence, economic laws have not been reversed. Investors purchasing securities with negative yields respond rationally to many risks and uncertainties present in the current monetary system (e.g., the risk of the breakup of the euro area). In an environment of slow economic growth and high public debt, the demand for safe havens is high. In an environment of low interest rates, the speculation on exchange rates becomes more and more important. In this sense, negative yields are an unintended consequence of ultralow interest rates in a world of floating exchange rates. In an environment of central banks’ asset purchases and easy liquidity, investors can reasonably expect a further increase in bond prices. In this sense, negative yields are an unintended consequence of the accommodative stance of the central banks. 

Finally, commercial banks may prefer to buy bonds with relatively small negative yields rather than holding reserves at the central bank’s account 

## 132 _Is there life below zero?_ 

bearing a more negative deposit rate. Doing so means that the NIRP has been successfully transmitted to market interest rates. 

However, it is worth bearing in mind that in a free market without central banks, yields would generally not be negative.[27] In an unhampered economy, interest rates are determined by the social rate of time preference, which is a universal, praxeological preference for present satisfaction over future satisfaction. Importantly, time preference must always be positive; otherwise people would prefer doing everything later than earlier, but such postponing would quickly lead to death by thirst.[28] As Mises (1998, 481) states, 

Time preference is a categorial requisite of human action. No mode of action can be thought of in which satisfaction within a nearer period of the future is not— other things being equal— preferred to that in a later period. The very act of gratifying a desire implies that gratification at the present instant is preferred to that at a later instant. He who consumes a nonperishable good instead of postponing consumption for an indefinite later moment thereby reveals a higher valuation of present satisfaction as compared with later satisfaction. If he were not to prefer satisfaction in a nearer period of the future to that in a remoter period, he would never consume and so satisfy wants. He would always accumulate, he would never consume and enjoy. He would not consume today, but he would not consume tomorrow either, as the morrow would confront him with the same alternative. 

Hence, negative lending rates set at a very low level for a long time would punish lenders, which would freeze private investment and lead to capital consumption. As Mises (1998, 529) puts it, 

There cannot be any question of abolishing interest by any institutions, laws, and devices of bank manipulation. He who wants to ‘abolish’ interest will have to induce people to value an apple available in a hundred years no less than a present apple. What can be abolished by laws and decrees is merely the right of the capitalists to receive interest. But such laws would bring about capital consumption and would very soon throw mankind back into the original state of natural poverty. 

## **Conclusions** 

Given the low level of policy interest rates, central banks will likely resort to unconventional monetary policy when the next crisis hits. One of the much- discussed alternatives among academics is negative interest rates. Thus, understanding them is high on the research agenda, and this chapter adds its two cents. 

I analyzed the rationale behind the NIRP, showing that it is based on the traditional interest rate channel of monetary transmission and also on the 

_Is there life below zero?_ 133 

credit channel. Lowering interest rates is believed to stimulate the economy, so moving them into negative territory— although it looks revolutionary on the surface— was a logical step for central banks given the weak recovery after the Great Recession, the estimated decline in the neutral interest rate, and the observation that the effective lower bound is below zero because of the opportunity costs of holding cash. The consensus is that when the aggregate demand for investment and consumption is extremely weak, it is optimal for central banks to implement negative interest rates (Abo- Zaid and Garin 2016; Rognlie 2016; Dong and Wen 2017).[29] 

However, there is a lack of satisfactory theory explaining how charging for the excess reserves of commercial banks held at central banks— some economists even call it a “tax on reserves”—is supposed to revive bank lending and then the overall economy. The banking system itself cannot decrease the amount of reserves through granting loans. 

Additionally, the rationale behind introducing the NIRP was a very weak economy; but, in such an environment, identifying creditworthy borrowers to whom banks can grant loans without deteriorating the quality of the portfolio of loan clients is exceptionally challenging.[30] The NIRP may lead to less bank lending, not more, because of the negative impact on banks’ profitability or increased demand for cash. Thus, the negative policy interest rates could be counter- productive regarding their impact on the aggregate demand. 

Indeed, there is no convincing evidence that the NIRP has helped stimulate bank lending, at least not in the euro area. Stiglitz (2016) even argues that 

in none of the economies attempting the unorthodox experiment of negative interest rates has there been a return to growth and full employment. In some cases, the outcome has been unexpected: Some lending rates have actually increased. […] Negative interest rates hurt banks’ balance sheets, with the ‘wealth effect’ on banks overwhelming the small increase in incentives to lend. 

Moreover, mainstream economists seem to underestimate the long- term risks related to the NIRP. The operation of this policy does not appear to have caused significant volatility or impaired market functioning, but its scale was limited. If prolonged, the negative effects may get stronger. So far, commercial banks have been able to offset to a large extent the decline in the net interest income caused by the NIRP, but only thanks to higher asset prices and lower loan provisions (which might turn out to be not sustainable, especially at higher interest rates), charging depositors higher fees (which could hit the depositor base), and taking more risks (which threatens their profitability and overall financial stability in the future).[31] 

The literature recommends that central banks should cut interest rates aggressively in response to a negative demand shock to avoid the risk of falling to the ZLB. But when it falls to the ZLB anyway, monetary policy should 

## 134 _Is there life below zero?_ 

remain more accommodative than implied by standard policy prescriptions, and the policy rates should remain lower for longer in order to lower future expected interest rates, thereby stimulating today’s spending (Williams 2014). 

However, much as ultralow but positive interest rates spur risk- taking, negative interest rates trigger it even more. Much as ultralow but positive interest rates reduce the debt- service burden, negative interest rates limit it even more, delaying the exit of zombie firms and the efficient reallocation of resources. Much as targeting neutral interest rates, which requires low policy rates and lowers market interest rates below the natural level, may lead to macroeconomic imbalances, negative policy rates may lead to evenlarger macroeconomic imbalances. Negative interest rates also aggravate the challenges faced by savers by presenting them with ultralow interest rates and limiting their ability to save for retirement. 

This chapter goes beyond that, pointing out the specific problems of negative interest rates. Although the debate about the effectiveness of the NIRP in the mainstream literature focuses on its impact on banks’ profitability, the main issue is not the limited transmission of the negative policy rates to deposit rates. 

The true problem with the NIRP is that it is based on an erroneous understanding of interest rates. According to the Keynesian view, which dominates among the supporters of the NIRP, the interest rate is a purely monetary phenomenon. In _The General Theory of Employment, Interest, and Money_ , Keynes (1936, 147) writes that the rate of interest is “the ‘price’ which equilibrates the desire to hold wealth in the form of cash with the available quantity of cash.” Similarly, Keynes (1937, 216) states that “the rate of interest obviously measures […] the premium which has to be offered to induce people to hold their wealth in some form other than hoarded money.” 

The implications of such a view are substantial. The interest rate becomes merely a “hurdle rate” to investment for business, or an opportunity cost of hoarding.[32] The interest rate is simply the cost of borrowing money, “the reward for parting with liquidity for a specified period” (Keynes 1936, 146), and “nothing more than the inverse proportion between a sum of money and what can be obtained for parting with control over the money in exchange for a debt for a stated period of time” (Keynes 1936, 146–47). 

Hence, lower interest rates decrease hoarding while increasing borrowing, investment, and aggregate demand. If low but positive interest rates or zero interest rates are insufficient to stimulate the economy, central banks can lower rates below zero. 

In this simplistic view,[33] the interest rate is considered a reward for overcoming the individual’s liquidity preference. But the same should apply, then, to any other price. If sellers of any good want to sell it, they have to offer sufficiently low prices to “reward” buyers for parting with cash. But this is clearly absurd, so the whole idea of the interest rate as a purely monetary phenomenon should be rejected. 

Of course, monetary factors can affect the rate of interest, but this does not mean that real factors can be ignored. The borrower pays interest not 

_Is there life below zero?_ 135 

for money but for the assets she can obtain with money. It is true that some classical economists, who believed in money neutrality, disregarded monetary influences on interest rates, but this does not justify the conclusion that interest rates are purely monetary (Hazlitt 1959). 

For Gesell (1958), who came up with the idea of stamped money to lower interest rates, the rate of interest is a purely monetary phenomenon due to money’s negligible carrying costs relative to holding goods (the suppliers of goods are thus willing to pay them a rate of interest to induce them to dishoard and spend money). Gesell (1958) believed that interest rates are too high because of the low storage cost of money, so he proposes stamped money. Although Keynes did not fully embrace Gesell’s theory, he shared Gesell’s view that the interest rate is a purely monetary phenomenon, and he believed that “the idea behind stamped money is sound” (Keynes 1936, 318).[34] 

For the classical economists, real long- term interest rates are determined by thrift and productivity, so central banks cannot control them. For the Austrian economists, interest rates are driven by time preference, so central banks’ manipulations of interest rates necessarily distort the economy. They argue that if the interest rate is set artificially low by central banks and through monetary expansion, the intertemporal allocation of resources will be inconsistent with actual social time preferences and available resources, leading to an unsustainable boom. 

Meanwhile, in a Keynesian view, the interest rate is not determined by the supply of and demand for loanable funds. It is the main monetarypolicy tool, or the central lever by which the central banks can exert their influence over the cost of borrowing and the overall economy. Interest rates can be thus manipulated at will through monetary policy. For central banks, unless there is high inflation, interest rates are not too low. In other words, the only significant downside to low interest rates is the possibility of inflation, as if interest rates affect only the price level. In this view, interest rates are set by the central bank (economists acknowledge that the central banks directly control only short- term yields, but they assume that central banks influence the whole structure of interest rates through market participants’ expectations), while in the Austrian view the driving force of interest rate determination is not the central bank but time preferences. 

But, contrary to Keynes’s view that there is no link between savings and investment, the latter is financed by the former. When savings increase, the interest rate declines, but this only reflects the greater pool of resources available for investment. A lower interest rate as such does not cause more investment. The mere decline in interest rates implies only redistribution from creditors to borrowers (Shostak 2018). 

What mainstream economists seem to neglect is that the interest rate is not just the price of borrowing. It is the rate of exchange of present goods for future goods, the key price in a market economy. As I wrote elsewhere, 

## 136 _Is there life below zero?_ 

While other prices affect the allocation of resources in the horizontal dimension (between sectors or production processes), the interest rate determines the intertemporal allocation of resources (in the vertical dimension). Since all production processes take place over time, the interest rate not only affects the profitability of the investment, but also the volume and especially the structure of production. 

(Sieroń 2019a) 

Hence, setting interest rates at an ultralow level or even pushing them into negative territory may lower the opportunity costs of holding cash but only at the expense of significant distortions in the structure of production. 

The view that the interest rate is the reward for not hoarding and is the main monetary- policy tool led to the formulation of the ZLB. Interest rates were believed to be constrained by the opportunity costs of holding cash, so monetary policy would become impotent at the ZLB. However, the whole problem of the ZLB is misplaced. First of all, the effective lower bound is actually below zero. The concept of the ZLB has been falsified by the fact that a number of central banks have set their policy rates below zero. 

Second, there is no single effective lower bound at which all economic agents suddenly rush to hold cash, just “as there is no positive nominal interest rate which causes economic agents to collectively abandon cash altogether” (Berentsen and Schär 2016, 6). 

Third, the ZLB literature adds the nominal interest rate to the list of rigidities that prevent the economy from reaching equilibrium. The problem emerges from the fact that central banks cannot set policy rates in line with the neutral interest rate. However, as I showed in Chapter 5, the concept of a neutral interest rate is dubious, especially the idea that it can be negative. 

Moreover, the ZLB literature concerns the constraints on policy rates that stem from technological or institutional factors such as the storage costs of money. But such factors should not be treated analytically as components of the interest rate (Garrison 1979). It is true that storing money is costly and risky, but that is also true of other goods. The focus on the storage costs of money suggests that the physical characteristics of reality rather than the interaction of market participants and their subjective valuations determine interest rates.[35] Similarly, interest rates cannot be simply explained by opportunity costs, as that would mean that the cause of the rates is outside the transactions themselves.[36] 

But the most important issue is that, even if nominal interest rates cannot fall below a certain level, the increase in money supply will still affect economic activity. This is why Brunner and Meltzer (1968) reject the idea of a liquidity trap. According to them and other monetarists, even if the policy rates reaches zero or some other lower bound, there need not be an infinite demand for money. Other assets than central bank reserves may have yields above the lower bound by more than the amount of the risk premia (Meltzer 1963). The ZLB problems result from focusing on the interest rate channel as the main monetary transmission channel. 

_Is there life below zero?_ 137 

Hence, the problem with New Keynesian macroeconomics is the mistaken premise that a central bank cannot fight recessions except by lowering nominal interest rates. Given such a premise, New Keynesians ended up logically with the idea of punishing the public for holding currency. However, although cutting interest rates is seen as expansionary because central banks tend to lower their target interest rate when they adopt an expansionary monetary policy, it is not the only expansionary tool. Central banks have other monetary- policy tools available to them (Swanson 2018).[37] 

Actually, cutting interest rates is not necessarily expansionary. Generally, Meltzer (2003) points out that measuring monetary conditions by the nominal interest rate is not appropriate. Sumner (2016) goes even further, saying that “the reduction in interest rates reduces the opportunity cost of holding base money, and hence increases base money demand and reduces velocity.”[38] 

Support for the NIRP results also from an erroneous view of the nature of money. According to Keynes (1936), demand for money is only the unwillingness to invest in interest- bearing assets, but, in fact, it is “the unwillingness to buy or rent nonmoney, including interest- bearing assets (i.e., land, labor, and/or capital goods, or future goods) _and_ noninterest- bearing assets” (Hoppe 1992, 212, italics in original). 

As Ilgmann and Menner (2011) note, economists reach conclusions about the benefits of negative interest rates based on DSGE (dynamic stochastic general equilibrium) models in which money is neutral and not necessary for the efficient allocation of resources. However, in reality, because of the Cantillon effect and other factors (Sieroń 2019a), money is not neutral. And money is not just a very liquid nonyielding asset, nor is it an obstacle to introducing negative interest rates, nor something we can easily tax to reduce the opportunity costs of holding cash. Money does not become a substitute at a zero interest rate for safe financial assets that are not media of exchange. Although there exists a certain degree of substitutability, money serves the key and unique function of medium of exchange. 

Hence, introducing a tax on money would erode its functionality, making it less attractive than alternatives, including foreign currencies (Brunner 1971). It would not only impair the effectiveness of monetary policy but also bring harmful results for social cooperation and economic calculation, including even the risk of the breakdown of the monetary system. 

McAndrews (2015) points out that positive nominal interest rates are important social conventions, so it is costly to change them. The same applies to money. But money is something more than a mere convention that can be changed by the government or society through the democratic process. People choose and accept money in the market process. 

Therefore, this chapter is a voice against proposals to reduce further the effective lower bound and the level of policy interest rates. At present, the level of negative rates is constrained by the opportunity costs of holding cash. Logically, some economists, such as Gesell (1958), Goodfriend (2000), Buiter and Panigirtzoglou (2003), Rogoff (2014, 2017a, 2017b), and Grasselli and Lipton 

## 138 _Is there life below zero?_ 

(2019), argue that taxing cash or even phasing out or eliminating cash could solve the problem. Mankiw (2009) discusses the idea that each year the Fed would pick randomly a number between zero and nine, and all bills with a serial number ending in that number would cease to be legal tender. Agarwal and Kimball (2015) argue that central banks could use an electronic money as a unit of account that could yield negative interest rates with paper currency that diverge from par.[39] However, all these proposals try to solve a nonexistent problem.[40] They are technocratic and assume that people will meekly accept the lower attractiveness of cash, that the proposals will not bring about any unintended consequences. They all try to create Gesell’s _Freigeld_ and to provide very cheap or even free credit. The contention is that there ought to be no interest at all, but it is impossible to eliminate interest rates. 

Central banks can manipulate the interest rate to whatever level they desire, even to negative levels. However, as Shostak (2018) puts it, 

they cannot exercise control over the underlying interest rates as dictated by people’s time preferences. When the central bank engages in a persistent lowering of interest rates this policy sets in motion an increase in the underlying interest rates as dictated by people’s time preferences. 

Hence, even if the central banks could lower rates substantially below zero, that would not help the economy. On the contrary, it could add to the existing macroeconomic imbalances. 

Some economists propose that central banks should adopt a specific interest rate rule. However, this chapter shows that how exactly the central banks set their interest rates is of secondary significance. A rule might help sometimes, but it might also add to the imbalances, especially if it allows for negative neutral interest rates. What really matters is that central banks target interest rates at all. Interest rates are critical for the allocation of credit and, consequently, economic resources. It is more likely than not that the central banks’ target will deviate from the natural interest rate. When the central banks keep their interest rate targets far below reasonable estimates of the natural rate for too long, they impair the allocation function of interest rates, which separates between high- and low- return investment— when the interest rates are ultralow, or even negative, even unviable projects appear to be profitable[41] —and thus distort the allocation of resources. 

In order to refute the counterarguments, the proponents of the NIRP sometimes refer to the fact that early mathematicians thought the idea of a negative number was absurd (Mankiw 2009). However, the problem does not lie in the negative numbers per se but in their economic interpretation, their consequences, and the motivations behind them. Prices balance the supply and demand. If something is abundant like air (in normal circumstances), it has no price. But it is unclear what would be the economic interpretation of negative prices. Seemingly, the negative prices would mean that the sellers pay the customers to take their products, which would be clearly an absurd. 

_Is there life below zero?_ 139 

Should not we view the negative interest rate in a similar vein? According to the cliché, insanity is doing the same thing over and over and expecting a different result. After several years of a ZIRP, with expectations of acceleration in economic growth, some central banks have decided to lower the interest rate into negative territory. It remains an open question why they think a NIRP will work if a zero rate has not worked.

## **7 Are we falling into a debt trap? Monetary policy and debt** 

## **Introduction** 

Global debt has risen dramatically in the postwar era. It has increased from around 100 percent in 1950 to almost 200 percent of world’s GDP in 2007, when the financial crisis came (Mbaye and Badia 2019). What is disturbing, the global debt has continued to rise since the Great Recession, reaching 225 percent in 2017. Contrary to what one would expect after the longest and deepest global recession since the Great Depression, a recession that was caused by the bursting of the global credit bubble, the world’s economies have not deleveraged. Instead, debt continues to grow in nearly all countries, in both absolute terms and relative to GDP (Dobbs et al. 2015). 

According to the estimates of Mbaye and Badia (2019), the world’s debt now exceeds, on average, $86,000 per capita, more than two and half times average income per capita. The rise in the global debt has been driven largely by advanced countries,[1] although since the financial crisis emerging markets have added to global debt while advanced countries have deleveraged somewhat. 

Global public debt, on which public’s attention is focused, after a steady decline up to the mid-1970s, has gone up since then. However, the driving force behind global debt has been the private sector’s debt, which has tripled since 1950. In particular, household debt has risen, especially in the form of borrowing for housing. 

In most developed countries, the growth in household debt over the past two decades has exceeded that of income. For example, in advanced economies, during the five years preceding 2007, the ratio of household debt to income rose by an average of 39 percentage points, to 138 percent. In the United States, the ratio of total household debt to aggregate personal income has risen from an average of 0.6 in the 1980s to 1.0 in the period 2000–6 (Dynan and Kohn 2007), while between 1987 and 2006 household debt and residential house prices in the United Kingdom quadrupled absolutely and grew by more than 50 percent as a percentage of income (Waldron and Zampolli 2010). 

Figure 7.1 presents the components of the US total debt as a percentage of GDP. Household debt has increased from 25 percent in 1950 to 100 percent

in 2007, when house prices started to decline and the financial crisis started. Since then, the household sector managed to deleverage, reducing its debt to 80 percent of GDP. As I explained in Chapter 1, although the deleveraging was necessary, it reduced the effectiveness of expansionary monetary policy adopted in response to the Great Recession. 

Nonfinancial corporate debt has increased from 50 percent in 1950 to 110 percent in 2007 and to above 120 percent in 2017. Last but not least, general government debt has declined from 83 percent in 1950 to 40 percent in 1981. However, it has increased to above 100 percent in 2017, an unprecedented level during peacetime. But why does the increase in debt matter? 

## **Does debt matter?** 

Credit creation has been the basis of the Austrian business cycle theory since Mises’s ([1912] 1953) _Theory of Money and Credit_ . Fisher (1933) formulates a debt- deflation theory of the Great Depression. Minsky (1992) develops a financial- instability hypothesis according to which endogenously rising leverage in good times paves the way for crisis. Koo (2013) argues that a balance sheet recession and debt overhang were the reasons behind the sluggish recovery in Japan after the bursting of its asset price bubble in the early 1990s and in the United States after both the Great Depression and Great Recession. 

Despite these theoretical developments, debt played a limited role in standard macroeconomic models before the Great Recession. It was considered 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 145 

as just enabling economic agents to smooth out payments. The assumption was that the overall level of debt does not affect aggregate net wealth since one person’s liability is another person’s asset and adverse income effects on borrowers will be offset by beneficial income effects on lenders.[2] This view is, for example, held by Krugman (2011), who says that “the debt we create is basically money we owe to ourselves, and the burden it imposes does not involve a real transfer of resources,” although “we” and “ourselves” are in reality different groups of people. 

Moreover, as long as the debt does not increase spending on nondurable consumption goods but on durables, such as houses, or financial assets, the net wealth of the borrower is believed to be also unchanged or even increased if the asset prices appreciate fast enough. It follows that the level of debt matters only if the distribution of that debt matters. 

However, there are a few problems with such a view. First of all, in the contemporary monetary system, commercial banks increase the money supply through credit creation. Werner (2014, 73) describes the process as follows: 

While the balance sheet total is not affected by the granting and disbursement of the loan in the case of firms other than banks, the picture looks very different in the case of a bank. While the loan contract shows up as an increase in assets with all types of corporations, in the case of a bank the disbursement of the loan takes a different form from that of the other firms: it appears as a positive entry on the liability side of the balance sheet, as opposed to being a negative entry on the asset side, as in the case of non- banks. As a result, it does not counter- balance the increased gross assets. Instead, both assets and liabilities expand. The bank’s balance sheet lengthens on both sides by the amount of the loan. 

This means that although borrowers’ liabilities are matched by banks’ assets, these assets are not counterbalanced by a negative entry on the banks’ asset side— that is, they are not funded by cash, but created out of thin air. From the accounting point of view, everything looks fine, but this is because of the tautology that assets are always equal to liabilities plus capital. From the economic point of view, however, commercial banks create money in the form of credit not backed by savings or any assets. Hence, in the contemporary monetary system, an increase in indebtedness is not sustainable in the long run. Moreover, what are problems for debtors are not necessarily good for lenders. Actually, defaults on debts, declines in collateral values, and nonperforming loans create serious problems for lenders and commercial banks. Finally, credit creation implies a redistribution of income, in particular through the Cantillon effect (Sieroń 2019a), so changes in the overall level of debt do matter. 

Second, it is true that as long as asset prices appreciate, the net wealth of borrowers who incur debt to buy assets is unchanged or even improved. But if the increase in asset prices results not from fundamental factors but from 

## 146 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

credit creation, it will not be sustainable.3 The rise in net wealth will thus turn out to be illusionary. In other words, justifying increased debt by referring to increased wealth can be a circular argument. Increased availability of credit increases the demand for housing. As houses are used as collaterals, increases in house prices and secured debt tend to reinforce each other. This means that the increased debt might not be rational even if the asset values and net worth seem to justify it (Large 2004). 

The key here is that central banks can affect asset prices. After all, this is the channel through which quantitative easing was supposed to work, as I show in Chapter 2. It follows that the higher asset prices do not necessarily justify a higher debt level. Actually, when people consider the rise in asset prices caused by monetary policy easing as a real gain, they make incorrect (too optimistic) assessment of their real wealth and debt servicing capacity, contributing to overconsumption and excessive indebtedness. 

One should distinguish here between realized capital gains and paper capital gains. When house prices fall, the paper capital gains diminish and the measure of household net wealth or the ratio of debt to assets deteriorates quickly. This is why the increase in debt- to- income ratios makes at least some households more vulnerable to negative shocks to prices, incomes, and interest rates. 

Another important distinction is between capital gains and income. The real estate, when its price goes up, can create capital gains for its owners but not income for the overall economy: 

All funds from which the possessor derives an income, which income he can use without sinking and dissipating the fund itself, are to him equivalent to capital. But to transfer hastily and inconsiderately to the general point of view, propositions which are true of the individual, has been a source of innumerable errors in political economy. In the present instance, that which is virtually capital to the individual, is or is not capital to the nation, according as the fund which by the supposition he has not dissipated, has or has not been dissipated by somebody else. 

(John Stuart Mill, 1848, chap. 5, par. 3) 

The crucial question here is how we define wealth. A rising price of an owned asset does not constitute an increase in wealth. Wealth is “the capacity to have a higher future standard of living” (White 2012, 12), so higher house prices constitute wealth only when they are justified by higher expected productivity and higher future earnings, not when they result from policy- induced lower interest rates. But it should be clear that houses purchased not for rental but for residential purposes cannot increase productivity, earnings, or disposable real income over time, an increase that Samuelson (1945) considers the only reasonable criterion of whether a person or institution is better off. What the higher house prices can do is to increase the collateral values against which loans can be taken out to support spending. However, as I pointed out 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 147 

in Chapter 2, loans must be repaid at the cost of future consumption, so there is no wealth created here, but a mere intertemporal shift in consumption. 

In other words, the commonly understood wealth is not a sufficient measure of standard of living. As Merton (2006, 62) points out, 

What matters to people, particularly in the context of the retirement part of the life cycle, is not how much wealth they have but the standard of living they can enjoy. The standard of living is much better represented as a lifetime flow or a perpetuity than as a stock of wealth. What we see is another dimension of risk in addition to wealth— changes in what you can earn with that wealth, described by changes in the investment opportunity set of risk and return, including the interest rate. 

From this perspective, it should be clear that ultralow interest rates, which lower the income that wealth can generate, cannot do anything but lower the standard of living. 

Third, the positive effect of the increase in the house prices for homeowners may be offset by the rise in housing costs— both for the renters and for the homeowners themselves, as they will have to pay more to live in a new home, if they intend to move, or pay more, albeit implicitly in the form of higher opportunity costs, to live in their current house (Benito et al., 2006). Moreover, the have- nots will have to pay more to purchase a new house. Again, the increase in the debt does matter for the distribution of income and wealth and for the macroeconomy. 

## **Causes and consequences of high private debt[4]** 

There are several drivers of debt accumulation. The main driver of the rise in private debt in the last few decades was the increase in the money supply through credit expansion and a decline in interest rates, which lowered the cost of borrowing and the real cost of housing (Waldron and Zampolli 2010). The decrease in interest rates discouraged savings and encouraged consumption, especially financed by now- cheaper debt. Figure 7.2 shows the decline in US mortgage rates and the increase in the household debt- to- income ratio since 1980. 

The decrease in interest rates and the increase in mortgages boosted house prices, which increased housing debt by increasing perceived wealth, raising the value of collateral, and generating expectations of further price appreciation (Dynan and Kohn 2007). There was a feedback loop: when commercial banks extended more mortgages or extended them at lower interest rates and with eased conditions, the demand for houses increased, so the house prices rose. Higher house prices meant higher value of collateral, which strengthened the borrowers’ balance sheets, which encouraged them to amass even more debt (Large 2004). Figure 7.3 shows the comovement in US house prices and the household debt- to- income ratio from 1987 to the aftermath of the Great Recession.

Source: Ahn et al. (2018); Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019e), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved September 17, 2019. 

Moreover, as I showed in Chapter 3, in an environment of ultralow interest rates, when one cannot get much of a return on a bank deposit or other safe assets, real estate looks more attractive as an investment, which increases 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 149 

housing debt and the demand and price of housing. In other words, persistently ultralow interest rates support debt- financed investment in relatively risky and highly leveraged long- lived assets, such as housing. 

Ultralow interest rates also affect the supply side of mortgages. In such an environment, banks take more risk by granting more mortgage loans. Den Haan et al. (2007) find that after monetary tightening, banks substitute real estate and consumer loans with commercial and industrial loans. Conversely, after monetary easing, banks substitute commercial and industrial loans, which are usually safer and granted for shorter periods, with mortgages, which are generally granted for longer periods and are riskier. Monetary policy thus affects not only the level but also the composition of debt. 

What are the consequences of high private debt? First of all, the accumulation of debt increases financial fragility. As debt levels increase, borrowers’ ability to repay becomes progressively more sensitive to drops in their income and increases in the interest rates. High debt increases debt- servicing risks, rollover risk, and credit risk.[5] For a given negative shock, the higher the debt and the higher the leverage, the higher the exposure to such shocks and the higher the probability of defaulting. If the downturn is bad enough, borrowers will default and aggregate spending will fall. The high level of debt makes even relatively mild negative shocks and tightening of financial conditions potentially dangerous for the economy. The higher the level of debt, the lower the pain threshold and the bigger the crisis for a given size of the shock (Balke 2000; Cecchetti et al. 2011). 

Moreover, the accumulation of debt will encourage credit quality to deteriorate as, over time, especially when interest rates are too low for too long, lenders grant credit to less creditworthy borrowers. In other words, the credit expansion draws in marginal borrowers or borrowers with relatively high- risk profiles, which might cause systemic imbalances in the financial system (Schmidt 2018). Indeed, the global average quality of bond issuers has declined in recent years. For example, in 2017, in the United States, almost 40 percent of all nonfinancial corporate bonds were rated BBB, up from 31 percent in 2000 (Lund et al. 2018).[6] 

Second, the increases in the supply of credit and the high level of debt can lead to price bubbles, financial fragility, higher uncertainty, and macroeconomic instability (Taylor 2007). In particular, as Sieroń (2019a) shows, granting mortgages can lead to a housing bubble whose bursting generally results in slower economic growth than the bursting of bubbles in other asset markets (Detken and Smets 2004). This is because granting mortgages leads to significant debt overhang among households, which reduces financial stability and usually leads to deeper recessions and slower economic recovery. 

Indeed, Schularick and Taylor (2012) and Jordà et al. (2012, 2014) demonstrate that high debt levels are not only a good predictor of financial crisis but also a key determinant of the intensity of the ensuing recession. International Monetary Fund (2012) reaches similar conclusions for the United States: when households accumulate more debt during a boom, the subsequent contraction in economic activity is more severe. Similarly, Mian et al. 

## 150 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

(2013) argue that the Great Recession was aggravated by the high marginal propensity to consume of heavily indebted US households that cut spending rapidly following the negative house- price shock.[7] These findings are in line with the literature on balance sheet recessions (Koo 2013) and my analysis presented in Chapter 1 suggesting that the interest rate channel does not work efficiently in such an environment. 

Third, because real estate is essentially immobile and relatively specific, the financial crisis resulting from the bursting of a real estate bubble is associated with particularly high costs for society. This is because the capital structure that results from the expansion of loans to households for purchasing real estate is costly to adjust and typically cannot be rapidly restructured (Rzońca 2014). 

Fourth, greater household indebtedness also reduces labor mobility because the need to sell a house to repay the mortgage increases the cost of searching for employment in distant labor markets. It reduces the efficiency of the labor market and the whole economy, as the less flexible the labor market is, the longer is the necessary process of reallocating labor during a recession (Debelle 2004a; Ferreira et al. 2012). 

Fifth, lending to households lowers the liquidity barrier, reducing the savings rate and lowering the pace of economic growth (Jappelli and Pagano 1994; Rzońca 2014). Figure 7.4 presents the negative relationship between the US personal saving rate and its household debt- to- income ratio. 

Ultralow interest rates make saving much more problematic, putting all savers, including those who save for retirement, in a difficult situation. For example, a person who wants to have $1 million for retirement in forty years needs to save $843 each month when the deposit rate is 4 percent (I assume

_Figure 7.4_ US personal saving rate (left axis, in percentage) and household debt- toincome ratio (right axis) from Q1 1980 to Q3 2017. Source: Ahn et al. (2018 and Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2019r), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved September 17, 2019. 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 151 

here and in subsequent examples monthly compounding and no taxes), $1,077 at 3 percent, $1,359 at 2 percent, $1,694 at 1 percent, and $2,083 at 0 percent. This means a reduction in interest rates by 3 percentage points, from 4 to 1 percent, will force savers to save twice as much to achieve their retirement goal. 

Viewed another way, if a person saves $1,000 each month at 4 percent, then she will have $1.2 million in forty years, but only $928,000 at 3 percent, $736,000 at 2 percent, $590,000 at 1 percent and $480,000 at 0 percent. This means a reduction in interest rates by 3 percentage points, from 4 to 1 percent, will make a saver about half poorer in forty years. 

Finally, let’s consider a person who has $1 million in a bank account bearing 4 percent. She will have $4.9 million in forty years, but only $3.3 million at 3 percent, $2.2 million at 2 percent, $1.5 million at 1 percent, and, of course, $1 million at 0 percent. This means that a reduction in interest rates by 3 percentage points, from 4 to 1 percent, will make a saver more than three times poorer in forty years. This effect reduces savings- driven growth, increases less sustainable debt- driven growth, and redistributes income and wealth from savers to borrowers.[8] 

It is true that, on the other hand, the decline in interest rates today supports borrowers. But borrowers’ future demand will be diminished, as they will have to pay back the debt plus interest. Moreover, as most borrowers have mortgages with equal installments over time, they will benefit less from the decline in the interest rate that the savers will suffer.[9] Additionally, in some countries, many borrowers have mortgages with fixed rates, so the decline in the interest rate does not affect them directly. Last but not least, all people are savers to a certain extent, but not all people are borrowers. All this means that the overall effect of the ultralow interest rates on the level and path of future aggregate demand will be negative.[10] 

Sixth, the increase in debt enables borrowers to increase spending. They can buy more goods and assets that would be unavailable to them otherwise. However, the increase in spending leads over time to an increase in prices, which neutralizes the initial, democratizing effect of debt. Actually, the price inflation reduces the availability of a particular asset for the future generations of buyers who are yet to purchase a house or another asset: they will have to work and save more or amass even higher debt to allow themselves to buy the asset. In other words, the increase in debt entails important distribution effects. The rise in prices of real estate is beneficial for homeowners and the construction sector, but it hurts the have- nots, future buyers, and owners renting out apartments and houses (higher property prices mean lower profitability at unchanged rental rates). There is thus a transfer of wealth from current renters and future homeowners to existing homeowners. 

Seventh, credit booms— particularly those in the construction sector, which is one of the least productive sectors— and asset price bubbles result in misallocation of resources and a slowdown in productivity growth, with long- lasting adverse effects on the real economy (Borio et al. 2016). The debt 

## 152 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

financing is based on collaterals, so the credit expansion disproportionately benefits high- collateral/low- productivity projects and thus misallocates resources into activities whose output is easy to pledge as collateral, reducing growth in total factor productivity (Cecchetti and Kharroubi 2015). 

For all these reasons, high household debt reduces economic growth. Logically, as debt must be repaid someday, its accumulation may stimulate the economy today but only at the expense of slower economic growth tomorrow. Additionally, the debt must be repaid with interest, and it can lead to instability along the way. As Shirakawa (2015) notes, “Monetary policy can bring forward future demand to today by engineering lower real interest rates. But, when tomorrow becomes today, the economy is faced with lower demand, which necessitates bringing forward demand from the day after tomorrow.” 

The debt can accelerate economic growth only if it enables entrepreneurs to engage in productive investment in physical capital with a greater rate of return than the costs of borrowing. But household debt is amassed to smooth consumption over the life cycle or for speculative purposes associated with debtors’ expectation of an increase in asset prices (Turner 2010). In other words, if the credit flows into the entrepreneurs supporting production of goods and services, it can have positive impact on GDP growth. But if credit finances transactions in assets, such as houses, it may just inflate asset prices rather than leading to growth in economic activity (Bezemer et al. (2015). 

Indeed, research shows that an increase in private credit, particularly mortgage bank lending to households, is associated in advanced economies where credit provision is already high with lower long- term growth, rising inequality, and misallocation of capital that would otherwise be channeled toward corporate investment (Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development 2017a; Arcand et al. 2015; Cournède and Denk 2015). Cecchetti et al. (2011), using a dataset on debt levels in nineteen OECD countries from 1980 to 2010, find that beyond a certain level, debt is bad for economic growth. The threshold is 85, 90, and 85 percent for government, corporate, and household debt, respectively. Similarly, using data on fifty- four economies over the period 1990‒2015, Lombardi et al. (2017) show that although household debt boosts consumption and GDP growth in the short run (mostly within one year), it reduces them in the long run. A 1-percentage- point increase in the household debt- to- GDP ratio tends to lower growth in the long run by 0.1 percentage points. The research suggests that the negative long- run effects on consumption and GDP growth tend to intensify as the household debt- toGDP ratio exceeds 60 and 80 percent, respectively. 

Similarly, the International Monetary Fund (2017b) finds a trade- off between the short- term benefits of rising household debt to growth and its medium- term costs to macroeconomic and financial stability, in particular a greater probability of banking crises. Although an increase in the household debt- to- GDP ratio is associated with faster economic growth in the short run, the effects are reversed in three to five years. 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 153 

Further, the distribution of debt can amplify the negative effects of high indebtedness on financial and macroeconomic stability, especially if high debt is concentrated among households with lower income and limited access to credit or less scope for self- insurance (Zabai 2017). Very often this is exactly what the data show. For example, Debelle (2004b) reports that detailed data on the distribution of household debt available in a number of countries (including Australia, Netherlands, Norway, Sweden, and the United States) show that among the population of indebted households, the highest debt- toincome ratios are found at the lower end of the income distribution. 

## **Monetary policy and public debt** 

In response to the Great Recession, most countries, especially the most developed ones, increased their budget deficits and public debt. Figure 7.5 shows the increase in general- government gross debt in the United States, the euro area, and Japan in the aftermath of the great financial crisis.[11] The unprecedented peacetime substantial rise in public debt was mainly caused by the governments’ bailouts of banks (and other companies), and the low interest rates that made the bailouts possible (or at least facilitated them). 

Indeed, ultralow interest rates have effects on public debt and fiscal policy. First of all, lower interest rates increase the discounted value of future income

## 154 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

and thereby augment the market value of Treasury securities, among other assets. As the bond prices rise, the yield declines, reducing government’s borrowing costs. Incurring debt becomes more attractive, which encourages running or even enlarging budget deficits (Mauro et al. 2013). In this environment, the increasing public debt does not necessarily cause higher debtservicing costs. The case of Japan is illustrative, as, despite its enormous public debt, borrowing costs are similar to yields on German bonds and lower than US yields, as Figure 7.6 illustrates. 

Another example is the euro area in recent years. The decline in policy interest rates has reduced the implicit interest rate paid on euro- area aggregate public debt from a high of almost 8.5 percent in 1995 to a low of 2.5 percent in 2016. As van Riet (2018, 292) notes, 

This has cut the annual interest bill for euro area governments from nearly 5.5 percent of GDP to just 2.3 percent over the same period, with a further decline still in the pipeline. These savings occurred despite the fact that euro area government debt was broadly stable at around 70 percent of GDP from 1995 to 2007, subsequently saw a sharp rise during the euro area crisis to a peak of 94 percent in 2014, and only then entered on a modestly declining path. 

The decline in borrowing costs before the global financial crisis was used not for reducing general- government deficits and debts but largely for higher government expenditures (European Central Bank 2016). 

Hence, although governments pay lower interest rates, ultralow interest rates are not the solution to the debt problem. They rather paint a misleading picture of debt sustainability: when interest rates are ultralow or even below

_Monetary policy and debt_ 155 

zero, even irresponsible fiscal policy can look rational. They thus support the debt- driven growth, which is unsustainable in the long run. 

The central banks’ purchases of government bonds also, of course, increase their prices while decreasing their yield. Quantitative- easing programs thus “constitute massive central banks’ intervention on secondary treasury bond markets which lead to an increase in their prices and a fall in long- term interest rates, effectively making public debt less costly to service” (Sławiński 2016, 13). What is more, as central banks effectively return any interest payments on the Treasuries to the government as seigniorage and return principal payments by reinvesting in government bonds, the Treasuries held by central banks become “ _de facto_ zero- coupon perpetuities” (Sławiński 2016, 13), creating space for more fiscal easing. 

Hence, ultra- easy monetary policy increases fiscal deficits on the margin, decreasing governments’ motivation to conduct unpopular but necessary fiscal and structural reforms. As a result, the economy does not grow as quickly as it could. Moreover, larger budget deficits mean more redistribution from the private to the public sector, which disturbs the structure of relative prices and reduces productivity growth. The fiscal stimulus may also support private zombie companies, which also lowers the pace of economic growth, which constitutes then the justification for even more fiscal accommodation, creating a vicious cycle. 

Additionally, higher public indebtedness makes a return to a more normal monetary policy with higher interest rates much more difficult, as it would raise the costs of debt service for governments. The increased share of the interest rate burden in the budget would force the government to increase taxes or cut spending— unpopular actions among citizens and policy makers.[12] This pressure on monetary policy leads to the hysteresis of low interest rates and high government debt levels (Schnabl 2013). 

Second, monetary policy affects the fiscal outlook also through zombifying the economy. In Chapter 4, I showed that ultralow interest rates tend to increase the share of zombie firms, which hampers productivity growth. The sluggish economic growth increases the pressure on the government to maintain budget deficits to stimulate the economy, putting the economy into a trap of low growth and chronic high budget deficits. 

Moreover, as some government outlays are rigid, a productivity slowdown lowers revenues more than it lowers GDP and increases fiscal deficits. Sheiner (2018) finds that a reduction in average annual labor- productivity growth in the United States of 0.6 percentage points (from 1.6 to 1.0 percent) over the next 25 years would increase primary deficits relative to GDP, pushing the federal debt to between 146 and 173 percent of GDP by 2042, compared with the baseline estimate of 130 percent of GDP. 

Third, monetary policy affects public debt by distorting the price of risk as well. In Chapter 3, I showed that ultralow interest rates tend to increase risk- taking and lower the price of risk. When interest rates are kept at ultralow levels, their allocation and signaling functions are impaired, making it 

## 156 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

difficult to adequately assess the risk premium. Riskier assets look sounder than they really are. The lowering of short- term, risk- free rates makes the riskier assets more attractive. This applies also to government bonds. Their yields should reflect the soundness of the public debt level and the default risk. The rising ratio of public debt to GDP should be reflected in increased default risk and the risk premium. However, easy monetary policy can compress the risk premium, increase bond prices, and boost tax revenue in the short term, making public debt levels look sounder than they are in reality.[13] 

For example, the introduction of the euro lowered interest rates and compressed the risk premium in several peripheral countries of the euro area, leading to substantial fiscal imbalances (Bagus 2010). As Figure 7.7 shows, the long- term Treasury yields in Greece, Spain, and Italy started to decline in the run- up to the introduction of the euro, reaching the level of yields on German bunds, despite all the differences between the fiscal policies in these countries. Meanwhile, real estate booms in Ireland and Spain inflated tax revenues and made public budgets look sound, although the rising imbalances set the stage for immense burdens on public finances (Schnabl 2013). 

Fourth, the ultralow interest rates also increase deficits in defined- benefit pension schemes, which necessitates higher public spending (or lower revenues), adding to the public debt (Rzońca 2014). According to Guo (2019), the shortfall in pension funding for the global retired population is forecast to grow by 5 percent annually, rising from $70 trillion in 2015 to $400 trillion, or 108 percent of GDP, by 2050. 

The fact that ultralow interest rates increase the public debt is very important, as high public debt generates important economic costs, some of which increase downward pressure on the ultralow interest rates, putting the economy in a trap of high public debt and low interest rates.[14] First, as high

_Monetary policy and debt_ 157 

public debt increases the odds that a fiscal crisis will follow a financial crisis, it limits the room for countercyclical fiscal policy, including tax cuts during recessions. 

Second, if high government debt is to remain stable, it requires running primary surpluses, which can be difficult to maintain. Mauro et al. (2013) analyze a panel of fifty- five countries for up to 200 years and find that when a country has a public debt stock above an estimated threshold of 65 percent of GDP, unexpected declines in long- term growth lead to less of an increase in the primary fiscal balance as debt rises. Governments may be thus inclined to increase taxes to cover the debt- servicing costs. Higher taxes are not only contractionary but also weaken incentives for productive activities. 

Third, high government debt may crowd out private investment, and finance unproductive expenses. It reduces investment and slows the growth of the capital stock, which hinders labor productivity growth and GDP growth (Kumar and Woo 2010; Reinhart and Rogoff 2010; Cecchetti et al. 2011). According to Reinhart and Rogoff (2010), when gross external debt reaches 60 percent of GDP, annual growth declines by about 2 percent; for levels of external debt in excess of 90 percent of GDP, growth rates are roughly cut in half. 

Fourth, the rapid increase in the public debt may negatively affect agents’ expectations, increasing uncertainty and lowering spending. FernándezVillaverde et al. (2011) find that the increased uncertainty about the mix and timing of fiscal consolidation needed after the increase in the public debt hampers business investment. 

Moreover, concerns about the sustainability of the high budget deficits and rising public debt may boost the risk premium, which could radically increase borrowing costs, making the debt problem more severe. This adverse feedback loop can lead eventually to fiscal crisis (Greenlaw et al. 2013).[15] Corsetti et al. (2011) point out that rising sovereign indebtedness may increase sovereign default risk, which could spill over to the rest of the economy, raising funding costs in the private sector as well. As Figure 7.7 shows, the disclosure of Greece’s true fiscal position led to a downgrading in its credit rating and an increase in yields on its bonds, which aggravated the fiscal situation of the country, leading to the outbreak of the European sovereign- debt crisis. 

Fifth, fiscal accommodation may support zombie companies, which hampers productivity growth. Higher public debt and a larger public sector may increase demands for fiscal redistribution by powerful groups, reducing long- term economic growth (Tornell and Lane 1999). The sluggish pace of economic growth reduces the pressure on the central bank to normalize monetary policy and raise interest rates. 

Sixth, large issuance of government bonds ensures the commercial banks an income stream without need to restructure their balance sheets. And when commercial banks buy Treasuries, they crowd out lending to the private sector and increase the sovereign- bank interconnection.[16] The fact that commercial banks have large volumes of Treasuries on their balance sheets adds to 

158 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

the pressure on the central bank to not normalize interest rates as the increase in bond yields would lead to mark- to- market losses. 

Seventh, the need to refinance a high amount of public debt could lead governments and central banks to monetize the debt or apply financialrepression techniques to secure low borrowing costs. There is also a risk that central banks will conduct quasi- fiscal policies or that politics will dominate monetary policy (van Riet 2018). 

Hence, the International Monetary Fund (2010) argues that although fiscal consolidation, especially when it relies primarily on tax hikes, lowers output in the short term, reducing government debt is likely to raise output in over the long term. 

In his 2019 American Economic Association presidential address, Blanchard (2019) argued that if the interest rate paid by the government is lower than the growth rate of GDP, which was the case at the time in the United States and many other countries, then the public debt may have no fiscal cost. He acknowledged that even in the absence of fiscal costs, public debt reduces capital accumulation and may therefore have welfare costs, but he believed that these welfare costs may be smaller than typically assumed, as the safe rate, which is the risk- adjusted rate of return to capital, is lower than the growth rate; hence, the United States and many other countries can roll over high debts without significant costs. 

However, there are some problems with Blanchard’s stance. First of all, Treasury yields can increase in the future. Although interest rates on government debt fell after the Great Recession, there is no guarantee that they will drop again in the next global crisis (Rogoff 2019). 

Second, the safe rate has been clearly suppressed by monetary policy, so it is not appropriate to compare it to the growth rate. Third, there is still crowding out. Even if the fiscal stimulus financed by debt does not increase interest rates, the additional government spending still leads to distortions in the structure of production and to changes in the composition of GDP (Ravier and Cachanosky 2015). 

Fourth, GDP growth is not the rate that can be compared to bond yields. Abstracting from the fact that growth in tax revenue should be used instead of growth of GDP, the problem is that the accumulation of public debt will still entail some negative effects, no matter what the pace of economic growth will be. It increases the scale of the public sector’s control of economic resources, whether it is fiscally sustainable or not. This is why the current low differentials between the interest rate on government debt and the growth rate in many advanced countries “should not be taken as an incentive for higher debt levels” (Checherita- Westphal 2019). 

Last but not least, low differentials between the average effective interest rate on government debt and the growth rate do not imply lower odds of sovereign defaults, as it is the interest rate on new government borrowing that matters. As Mauro and Zhou (2019, 3) find, “history teaches us that many crises have occurred after years of low differentials, and that market 

_Monetary policy and debt_ 159 

expectations can turn quickly and abruptly, shutting countries out of financial markets in a matter of a few months”. 

## **Conclusions** 

This chapter is related to the wider debate about the role of debt in the economy. Used wisely and in moderation, it can improve welfare, but when used imprudently and in excess, the result can be disastrous. I showed that although an increase in household debt can reflect financial deepening, in an environment of ultralow interest rates it may rather indicate a build- up of financial imbalances. 

Low interest rates and the resulting accumulation of debt can stimulate the economy in the short term, but they hamper long- term economic growth. As debt must be repaid, its accumulation may spur some growth today, but only at the expense of lower growth tomorrow. However, the accumulation of debt does not only shift economic growth in time, by borrowing from the future, but also lowers the whole path of economic growth. This is because the high level of debt spurred by low interest rates increases the probability of large economic crashes, while reducing the economy’s resilience to them, and misallocates resources, even in good times. 

What is important here is that there is also a reverse relationship: high levels of debt make an increase in interest rates more difficult. This is because of political pressure and also because “in a high- debt economy, interest rate hikes could be more contractionary than cuts are expansionary” (Zabai 2017). In other words, the prolonged period of low interest rates can result in a debt trap: the cheap credit makes debt financing more affordable, but the accumulation of debt would require more spending on servicing the debt. In such an environment, any increase in interest rates could be detrimental for borrowers. Hence, although El- Erian (2016) might be right in claiming that central banks become the only game in town, this is because of the high level of debt, which was caused partially by themselves in the first place. For example, the European Central Bank (2016) estimates that under an assumption of no policy change, aggregate euro- area public debt as a percentage of GDP would decline from around 94 percent in 2015 to around 84 percent in the coming decade, while an interest rate 0.5 percentage points higher would put debt on an increasing path toward the end of the scenario horizon. 

So fiscal and monetary policies are not really independent from each other. Asymmetric monetary policy leads to asymmetric fiscal policy: during bad times, government debt accumulates quickly, but it is reduced only partially (if at all) during good times, in obvious contradiction to the Keynesian postulate of symmetric countercyclical fiscal policy. 

The effects of ultralow interest rates on the public debt and fiscal policy are further evidence that monetary policy is not neutral. In mainstream models, fiscal policy is considered an exogenous factor to which monetary policy should adapt. But in reality, easy monetary policy does not only passively 

## 160 _Monetary policy and debt_ 

react to fiscal policy but actively shape it through keeping interest rates at low levels, which encourages higher levels of public debt. 

The same applies to private debt. Its level and composition do matter. Central banks do not only passively react to agents’ intertemporal decisions about their consumption spending over their life cycle or to the exogenous buildup of private debt but actively affect asset prices and the attractiveness of debt financing, which encourages higher levels of private debt. 

The policy recommendation stemming from this chapter’s findings is thus that although it is possible to stimulate the economy through even- larger debt levels, the positive effect will be short- lived while the overall, long- term impact will be negative. Monetary authorities should thus drop their asymmetric approach and lean against the wind, trying to prevent the buildup of excessive debt. Similarly, fiscal authorities should not add debt during the boom, but consolidate public finances. During busts, the main problem is balance sheet repair, so addressing debt overhang should have a priority. Ultralow interest rates can only delay solving this problem or even make it more difficult. If the government has to intervene, it should perhaps, aside from implementing structural reforms, consolidate public finances and engage in private debt restructuring rather than providing fiscal stimulus (Borio 2012).[17] 

Contrary to the many mainstream economists who believe that accommodative fiscal policy is the method to overcome the zero lower bound and pull the economy out of such an unfortunate condition (e.g., Eggertsson and Woodford 2003; DeLong and Summers 2012; Eggertsson and Krugman 2012), this chapter shows that easy fiscal policy can actually prolong the period of ultralow interest rates (Rzońca 2014). 

Central bankers can remind politicians about the importance of fiscal and structural reforms.[18] But as long as interest rates remain low, policy makers simply have no motivation to make fiscal adjustments. Hence, moral suasion— and fiscal- policy rules, especially those that cannot be enforced— is not able to substitute for the signaling function of interest rates and the information value of national sovereign spreads. Central banks can buy time for governments, but they risk wasting time. The compression of Treasury yields weakens market- based incentives for governments to conduct structural reforms and pursue sound public finances. As monetary policy operates by encouraging borrowing, it is not an effective solution to the private and government debt problems; instead, it is a part of the problem.

> 2 But Koo (2013) maintains that debt deleveraging leads to stagnation that must be counteracted by fiscal stimulus.

   - domestic sovereign bonds in response to expansionary unconventional monetary shocks, promoting the sovereign- bank nexus. 

- 17 Policy makers should also reduce or even eliminate the debt bias in corporate taxation and eliminate tax incentives for home loans and homeownership. Efficient insolvency regimes could also help by allowing for quick bankruptcy and reallocation of resources.
