## **Synopsis and final conclusions** 

## **The contents of this book can be summarized as follows:** 

- Monetary policy does not work the same way all the time; its effectiveness depends on the macroeconomic context, and it is particularly ineffective during balance sheet recessions. 

- The ultralow interest rates may hamper the recovery through weakening people’s incentives to repair their balance sheets, which leads to the zombification of the economy. 

- The ultralow interest rates also can support excessive risk- taking, endangering financial stability. 

- The ultralow interest rates transfer wealth from savers to the indebted governments. 

- Negative interest rates can strengthen the above effects. They can especially strongly hit banks’ profitability. And NIRP is based on an erroneous understanding of interest rates and money. 

- Economists pay too much attention to the interest rate channel. Monetary impulses operate also through other channels, such as the portfoliobalance channel. 

- The zero- lower-b ound problem is thus exaggerated since the interest rate is not the only monetary- policy tool available to central banks. The sluggish recovery from the Great Recession was caused by debt overhang and the slow rate of churning, not by the zero lower bound. 

- The monetarist description of the monetary transmission mechanism is better than the Keynesian one, but it still suffers from important shortcomings, such as not including the Cantillon effect and relying on the dubious wealth effect. 

- Whether monetary policy operates via the interest rate or asset prices, it distorts the structure of prices and production. 

- The interest rate is an important factor affecting investment but not the only one. You can lead a horse to water, but you can’t make it drink. 

- • The interest rate is, however, not just a hurdle rate or the cost of borrowing money; it plays a much more important role in the economy, as it coordinates intertemporal resource allocation and prices time and risk. 

164 _Synopsis and final conclusions_ 

- When interest rates are at an ultralow level, their signaling and allocation functions are impaired. Ultralow interest rates thus work like subsidies for unproductive firms, or soft budget constraints (Kornai 1993), hampering the market’s selection mechanism and supporting zombie companies. 

- The secular stagnation hypothesis confuses a delayed recovery with chronically weak aggregate demand and cannot withstand critical examination. 

- The financial- drag or monetary- policy- drag hypothesis— according to which not only real factors but also monetary factors are responsible for the postcrisis slowdown— offers a better explanation of the recent decline in neutral rates (or long- term real market rates). In other words, monetary- policy shocks have significant and long- lasting effects on real interest rates. The “secular” decrease of long- term interest rates might be partly caused by (asymmetric) monetary policy. 

- There is no such thing as neutral interest rate policy, or neutral monetary policy, even in the long run. Ultralow interest rates, by lengthening the economic recovery and zombifying the economy, can drag down productivity growth. The effect might not be temporary, since at least some of the monetary- policy- induced resource reallocations might be irreversible and lead to hysteresis. That is, it’s easy to zombify the economy, but it’s not so simple to de- zombify it. 

- The subdued consumer price inflation does not mean that neutral interest rate must have fallen and that the monetary policy is not accommodative enough. 

- The neutral interest rate is not independent from monetary policy. Central banks do not merely passively adjust policy interest rates to an exogenous decline in the neutral interest rate. 

- The ultralow interest rates incentivize agents to accumulate more debt, which entails important negative consequences that in turn prolong the period of ultralow interest rates. 

- Monetary policy is not independent from fiscal policy. Ultralow interest rates do not motivate policy makers to lower budget deficits and reduce public debt; rather, they encourage fiscal easing. 

- Monetary policy cannot solve supply- side problems. The monetary stimulus can rather reinforce the misallocation of resources and perpetuate the disequilibrium. 

The above points do not, of course, constitute the entirety of my contributions for the theory of economics. However, they clearly show that my analysis enriches the debate on the monetary transmission mechanism. My analysis points out the shortcomings of the traditional interest rate and portfoliobalance channels while advancing our understanding of the risk- taking and zombification channels. In contrast to what many standard models of monetary economics claim to do, my book is really based on microfoundations and assigns the financial sector an important role in the transmission of monetary 

_Synopsis and final conclusions_ 165 

policy, as it shows how monetary policy operates via the balance sheet management of companies, investors, and financial intermediaries. The book has the great advantage over many mainstream models, as it shows how monetary policy affects not only the quantity of bank credit but also its quality. The book also goes beyond the representative firm and focuses on the link between monetary policy and the misallocation of resources across firms. Thus, it does not analyze large aggregates, but builds on the microfoundations of economic growth. 

This book advances a theory of the interest rate, its nature, its functions in the economy, and its role in the investment process. It presents evidence that when interest rates are kept at ultralow levels, their allocation and signaling functions are impaired. Practically all borrowers and all projects look sound with interest rates close to zero, which masks truly unsound projects and bad debts and makes the effective allocation of resources more difficult, if not impossible. It also explains how monetary policy affects not only the term structure but also the risk structure of interest rates, increasing our understanding of the relationship between monetary policy and risk. 

Austrian economists have long pointed to the negative effects of ultralow interest rates. However, I have greatly enriched the achievements of the Austrian school by applying its insights to the topics of zombie firms, negative interest rates, and neutral interest rates and by pointing out the importance of the risk structure of interest rates. I have also compared the Keynesian interest rate channel with the Austrian interest rate channel and shown that the latter is more advanced. 

My work has also proposed solutions to three important postcrisis puzzles. The first one is the dissonance between sluggishness in investment and exuberance in financial markets. The book has suggested that the explanation might lie in excessive risk- taking— or, to be more precise, in the wrong forms of risk- taking. The second is the insolvency puzzle: the low level of insolvencies in the aftermath of one of the greatest recessions in history. The third is the productivity puzzle: the productivity slowdown in advanced countries since the 2000s. This book has suggested that zombification— the increase in the share of zombie firms and the resulting misallocation of resources— might explain all of these puzzles. 

What are the policy implications of this book? First of all, it has presented arguments, both theoretical and empirical, against the use of ultralow interest rate policy. The central bank should not only avoid taking rates into negative territory but should cease to manipulate them at all or at least set a floor at a level high enough above zero. If central banks should exist (which we cannot assume), they should limit themselves to providing liquidity in times of crises. But they definitely should not suppress market interest rates, thereby impairing their allocation and signaling functions. The interest rate is not a mere tool of monetary policy. It is a key price in the economy. Setting it too low for too long can bring important negative consequences. Hence, both the scale and scope of monetary policy should be limited. 

## 166 _Synopsis and final conclusions_ 

Second, monetary policy should be more symmetric. Policy makers not only should lean more aggressively into financial booms and ease less aggressively in busts but also should not encourage risk- taking and increase asset prices and thereby increase the potential for future instability. They should not focus solely on the output gap and the price level, but also on the composition of output, asset prices, the leverage and risk taken by different agents, and more. In other words, we don’t need simply a boost to demand but a boost to “sustainable” demand. The debt- driven growth, which means borrowing growth from the future, is clearly not sustainable, and the diminished demand in the future reduces incentives to invest. The financial crisis was the result of excessive leverage, so increasing leverage should not be very helpful. Weak aggregate demand is just a symptom; we should investigate its sources and respond to them. We cannot assume that all economic problems, including the sluggish growth, result from the shortage of aggregate demand. As King (2016, 316) notes, “policies to boost demand in the short term may appear to help but do not alter the need for a correction of the disequilibrium.” 

Third, policy makers should not target neutral interest rates as it does not guarantee price stability but does lead to macroeconomic instability because it means market interest rates diverge from the natural interest rate. The neutral interest rate is not independent from monetary policy, so it should not be used as the policy benchmark. 

Fourth, neither estimates of neutral interest rates nor the zero- lowerbound problem constitutes a sound justification for further lowering interest rates or for taxing or eliminating cash. Central banks should not endorse such policies. After all, ZIRP and moderate NIRP have failed to accelerate economic growth. So we should not do more of the same; instead, central banks should keep policy rates from falling below a certain level substantially higher than zero, as failing to do so impairs the allocation and signaling functions of interest rates, which delays the needed restructuring of the economy and leads to all the negative consequences I have described. After all, economic recoveries occurred historically without ZIRP or NIRP— and they were faster and stronger. 

Interest rates are too important to be manipulated by central bankers. They determine the intertemporal allocation of resources and the allocation of risk, so all changes in the structure of interest rates affect the whole economy in several key dimensions. This is why interest rates are, in a sense, tools too blunt for monetary policy. The global financial crisis boosted the risk premium demanded by certain market participants. To counteract that increase, the major central banks slashed policy rates, affecting the whole global economy. However, it remains unclear how a decline in risk- free interest rates could solve the problems that caused the increase in the risk premium. Instead, it causes a new economic disorder, which can increase uncertainty and thus aggravate the original problem.[1] More generally, the Great Recession resulted from the bursting of the housing bubble caused partially by easy monetary policy and interest rates kept too low for too long. Hence, it is far 

_Synopsis and final conclusions_ 167 

from clear how even- lower interest rates could help. In other words, when central banks lower interest rates, they can decrease borrowing costs, but they do not address the underlying reasons for the crisis and the following recession. If anything, it aggravates the situation and leads to several unintended consequences as the impact of interest rates is not limited to a particular market segment but extends to the whole economy. During the crisis, it might be understandable to aid certain systematically important institutions, but it is certainly not appropriate to manipulate interest rates. 

Fifth, central banks should acknowledge that monetary policy is less effective during recessions, especially balance sheet recessions. Thus, when a crisis is over, the policy focus should shift toward promoting the necessary postcrisis adjustments in balance sheets and quick reallocation of resources to avoid zombifying the economy. We need more balance sheet repair and less reliance on traditional aggregate- demand management through monetary policy. We have to drop the illusion that central banks are omnipotent. They can supply liquidity, but they cannot solve structural problems. Neither ultralow interest rates nor quantitative easing can substitute for restructuring the economy. 

Sixth, it does not follow from the above point that, for example, the global economy needs easier fiscal policy. Rather, this book calls for policies that accelerate balance sheet repair and the reallocation of resources, such as normalizing interest rates, writing down debt, deregulating and liberalizing the economy, designing insolvency regimes that would make firm entry and exit less costly, opening the economy to increased competition, making the economy more flexible, and enacting reforms strengthening commercial banks’ capital position. 

Feldstein (2003, 1) notes once that “The history of monetary policy also contains a long list of fundamental but wrong views that were held with certainty by policy officials and widely accepted among academic specialists.” The book does not examine all “fundamental but wrong views”, nor does it exhaust all issues related to the monetary transmission mechanism in general and the effects of unconventional monetary policy in particular, because those topics are extremely extensive and nuanced fields of economics.[2] 

For a better understanding of the monetary transmission mechanism, a discussion of the theory of banking and of the debate on the exogeneity versus endogeneity of money would be particularly fruitful (Sieroń 2019b). It would also be worth examining the theory of interest and its link with monetary policy. Another promising field of research seems to be a detailed, microeconomic analysis of how ultralow interest rates affect NPV analysis, savingsinvestment decisions, and personal finance. The importance of the market’s selection mechanism and the efficient bankruptcy mechanism, especially in the context of monetary policy, also is worth exploring. Last but not least, a thorough investigation of various proposals for changes in monetary policies should be more than welcome. In the Appendix 1, I will briefly assess such proposals as a higher inflation target, price- level targeting, NGDP-targeting, and helicopter money, but a more thorough study would be useful. 

168 _Synopsis and final conclusions_ 

## **Notes** 

## **Appendix 1** 

## **What else will central banks try to do?[*]** 

In this book, I presented several strong arguments that the central bank should not set policy interest rates at ultralow levels (for too long). However, given the asymmetric stance of central banks and high level of indebtedness, ultralow interest rates are here to stay. Instead of normalizing monetary policy, central banks are more likely to continue to use unconventional tools or to invent new policies to overcome the zero-l ower-b ound problem. What exactly can we expect from central banks when the next crises occur? 

## **Making unconventional tools more conventional** 

First of all, central banks could use unconventional tools, such as quantitative easing and forward guidance, more widely, making them in effect standard tools. In Chapter 2, I showed that the theoretical justifications behind quantitative easing are dubious. It operates on the portfolio-b alance channel, which can lead to excessive risk-t aking (see Chapter 3), and the wealth effect, which boils down to redistributive policy. Redistribution cannot increase society’s wealth, while boosting asset prices leads to capital gains concentrated among relatively few households, a policy that may fail to gain public support. Another issue with quantitative easing is that unwinding it may be problematic.[1] 

The problem with forward guidance is that it does not constitute a fully autonomous monetary-p olicy tool. To remain credible, forward guidance must be backed ultimately by real actions. Another issue with forward guidance is that central banks face time-i nconsistency problems as decision makers (e.g., during a recession, they can promise to overshoot the inflation target, but when the recession ends, overshooting has only costs and no benefits), which lowers the credibility of this policy tool (Haberis et al. 2017). As Krugman (1998, 139) notes, “Monetary policy will in fact be effective if the central bank can credibly promise to be irresponsible, to seek a higher future price level.” Last but not least, to the extent that forward guidance concerns promises to keep rates low for a long time, it lowers long-t erm interest rates, which decreases banks’ profitability and can lead to excessive risk- taking or the zombification of the economy. 

* This appendix was written in 2019 well before the COVID-19 crisis. 

170 _Appendix 1_ 

## **Lowering the effective lower bound** 

Central banks could also implement negative interest rate policy. Some already have, as I discussed in Chapter 6. However, they could try to lower or even eliminate the lower bound to extend NIRP. As the level of negative rates is constrained by the opportunity costs of holding cash, central banks could follow the suggestions of some economists and adopt one of the following four approaches (Rogoff 2017a): (1) move to a cashless society; (2) find a technological approach to paying negative interest rates on currency; (3) dispense with the one- to- one exchange rate between electronic bank reserves and paper currency, which would enable central banks to discount cash in a way that mimics paying negative interest; and (4) take steps to make large- scale hoarding of cash much more costly (e.g., by phasing out large- denomination notes). 

As I showed in Chapter 6, all these proposals stem from the mistaken New Keynesian view that the interest rate channel is the only or the key channel of monetary policy. But even if this was true, the desire to restore the efficiency of the monetary transmission mechanism would not necessarily justify punishing the public for holding currency. While the benefits would be limited— after all, the proposals boil down to more of the same, and ZIRP has failed to accelerate economic growth— these proposals would not only decrease social welfare but also risk disrupting the monetary system. 

## **Raising the inflation target** 

Lowering nominal interest rates is just one way to reduce real interest rates. The second option, suggested by several prominent economists (Krugman 1998; Blanchard et al. 2010; Ball 2013), is for central banks to lift the inflation target, as higher inflation would also decrease real interest rates, closing the output gap. 

The proposal has one important advantage over lowering nominal interest rates. It does not entail directly attacking cash or radically changing the monetary system. And it does not involve directly intervening in the structure of interest rates. 

However, the suggestion to increase the inflation target has important drawbacks. First and foremost, it introduces all of the costs of higher inflation, such as reducing the purchasing power of money, lowering savings, increasing shoe leather costs, indebtedness, and menu costs, redistributing income, impairing the price mechanism, disrupting the structure of relative prices, causing capital to be consumed, making inflation more volatile, and causing bracket creep (Sieroń 2019a). The proposal is at odds with New Keynesian models. They focus on the costs arising from sticky prices, but higher inflation would clearly increase the need for price adjustments and the accompanying costs in an economy with price rigidities. 

Moreover, given that the major central banks failed to achieve their 2 percent target for years, why should they be able to reach a 4 percent target? Such 

_Appendix 1_ 171 

a target might thus fail at increasing inflation expectations and lowering real interest rates. 

Or it might succeed, which could have negative consequences. After all, central banks have invested a lot in convincing people they are committed to a 2 percent target, so changing the target could erode their credibility and destabilize inflation expectations. People might reasonably fear that the increased target would require much more monetary accommodation and that the inflation target might be raised again in the future. If that happens, nominal interest rates will go up, which could mean real interest rates would remain unchanged. 

## **Adopting price- level targeting** 

Another idea for how to increase inflation expectations and thus reduce real interest rates is to switch to price- level targeting (Eggertsson and Woodford 2003). In contrast to an inflation- targeting regime, under price- level targeting undershooting the price- level target in one period leads to higher inflationary expectations as central banks have to overshoot in the next period to compensate for the previous undershooting. 

The proposal faces similar problems to raising the inflation target. Moreover, the public would not like the fact that below- average inflation would be followed by above- average inflation,[2] while the central banks would dislike the fact that above- average inflation would have to be followed by belowaverage inflation or even deflation to return to the target. It would mean that the central banks could not neglect the supply shocks that temporarily drive up inflation, but they would have to tighten monetary policy to reverse their effect. 

## **Adopting NGDP-targeting** 

Although it seems to be less discussed among central bankers, some economists suggest switching to nominal- GDP targeting (Meade 1978; Bean 1983; Hall and Mankiw 1993; Frankel 2012; Sumner 2012; Garín et al. 2016). An NGDP- targeting regime is said to respond to demand shocks in exactly the same way as an inflation- targeting regime but better handle supply shocks. In the face of negative supply shocks, the central bank, operating under inflation- targeting regime, should tighten its monetary policy because the shock would increase prices, while under NGDP-targeting regime it would also take the fall in the output into the account. Thanks to this feature, NGDP-targeting would be less restrictive when the economy is faced with negative supply shocks and real output falls. 

Although NGDP-targeting could improve central bank performance, which currently focuses too much on price- level stability, it is not a panacea, and it faces several important problems. First of all, it implies that the money supply should offset changes in money demand. However, it obscures the 

## 172 _Appendix 1_ 

fact that the effects of growth in the money supply do not necessarily neutralize the effects of increased demand for money but, on the contrary, can compound them if the money does not specifically reach those people whose demand for money has increased. NGDP-targeting would thus still generate the Cantillon effect and the resulting macroeconomic instability (Sieroń 2019a). With a target set particularly high, the resulting instability could be especially significant. 

Second, NGDP-targeting wrongly assumes that GDP is a good measure of productive economy. It also adopts the aggregate supply- aggregate demand framework, leaving the inter-t emporal capital structure out of the analysis (Ritenour 2019). 

Moreover, NGDP-targeting implies that the central bank would commit to keeping the nominal- spending level growing even if real economic growth slows. It would mean accepting more inflation in times of sluggish growth, implying stagflation— that is, people would face not only sluggish growth but also increasing prices. Last but not least, the technical problem associated with nominal- GDP targeting is that measures of GDP are subject to large and frequent revisions, making such a policy difficult to undertake and communicate. 

## **Helicopter money** 

The last proposal I would like to analyze is so- called helicopter money, which has recently gained some popularity (Buiter 2004; Turner 2015). Although it does not seem it will soon be implemented, the arrival of the next crisis could cause panicked central bankers to get into helicopters. That is why it is worth knowing now what the tool is and what the results could be. 

Although the term was taken from Friedman (1969), who undertook a thought experiment in which a helicopter flies over a certain society and spreads money in such a way that the cash balances of all persons double at the same time, most researchers define helicopter money as the central bank’s financing of expansionary fiscal policy (through directly crediting the Treasury account or purchasing perpetual Treasuries). It is thus the monetization of government deficits in an attempt to stimulate aggregate demand. Thanks to the funds from the central banks, governments could then either transfer money directly to citizens,[3] or finance tax cuts, or increase expenditures on goods or services. Despite important differences between these options, in all three cases the money supply would increase, just as in quantitative easing. 

However, contrary to QE, the newly created money would go directly (or indirectly through government spending) to households, which would most likely spend it on consumer goods, which could lead to relatively serious inflation. Although spurring higher inflation would be the main goal of helicopter money, the inflation might get out of control.[4] 

Moreover, helicopter money would mean overtly funding government deficits, providing virtually free financing of government deficits and 

_Appendix 1_ 173 

allowing governments to continue their unsound fiscal policies and postpone necessary structural reforms. As monetary policy would become quasi- fiscal policy, the independence of central banks would be endangered. 

## **Summary** 

There are, of course, many more proposals to reform central banking and support the economy.[5] Almost all economists have their own idea for how best to manage monetary policy. I have focused on the most important ideas. The probability of central banks implementing them might seem low, but one should remember that quantitative easing and NIRP were perceived as too unorthodox or impossible to introduce just several years ago.[6] 

The chorus of voices demanding new measures or changes in the current framework is growing.[7] This is why we cannot ignore them when the next crisis hits. What is, however, striking is that all proposals boil down to further reducing real interest rates either through lowering nominal interest rates or through generating higher inflation. Unfortunately, none of them are free of problems. All these ideas call for increasing the money supply or suppressing market interest rates, but it is unreasonable to expect that these monetary actions will not interfere with the structure of prices and production. Although the history of central banking is a story of one failure after another, researchers are still searching for a robust monetary policy. But looking for the Holy Grail of monetary policy is illusory.

When it comes to the former, note that the major central banks target 2 percent inflation over the medium term, which is similar in practice to price- level targeting. And they have recently started to more strongly emphasize that the current 2 percent inflation target is symmetric, indicating that they may be more ready to accept

## **Appendix 2** 

## **Should central banks cut interest rates in response to the COVID-19 crisis?[*]** 

## **Introduction** 

Pandemics are one of the biggest global risks, especially in the modern, highly globalized world of intensified travel and integration between countries. They can cause high morbidity and mortality, and can have negative socioeconomic effects. Historical examples include the Black Death that killed 30–50 percent of the European population (Routt 2008; Ziegler 2009; DeWitte 2014) and the Great Influenza epidemic, which caused about fifty million deaths worldwide (Taubenberger and Morens 2006; Barro et al. 2020). 

In March 2020, the novel coronavirus disease COVID-19, caused by the SARS- CoV-2 virus, reached pandemic status (Ghebreyesus 2020). The pandemic is primarily a health crisis, but it early on began to hurt the global economy. The pandemic triggered a stock market crash (Ramelli and Wagner 2020) (see Figure A2.1) and a soaring corporate- bond spreads (see Figure A2.2). It also severely hit the real economy, triggering a global recession that could be larger than the Great Recession (Gourinchas 2020).

In response, central banks all over the world have stepped in, cutting interest rates, reintroducing quantitative easing, and introducing other measures to support the economy. In particular, on March 12, 2020, the European Central Bank announced additional Longer- Term Refinancing Operation and introduced considerably more favorable terms applied to Targeted Longer- Term Refinancing Operations, and additional net asset purchases of 120 billion euros (ECB 2020a), followed by 750 billion in purchases through the Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme announced on March 18 (ECB 2020b). The Bank of Japan (2020) and the Bank of England (2020a, 2020b) also have expanded their asset- purchase programs, while the latter additionally reduced interest rates from 0.75 percent to 0.1 percent in two moves and lowered capital requirements for commercial banks. Last but not least, the Federal Reserve (2020a, 2020b, 2020c) cut in two meetings the target range of the federal funds rate from 1.50–1.75 to 0–0.25 percent, reintroduced quantitative easing (initially up to $700 billion, only to make it unlimited several days later), and introduced several other emergency measures. 

The aim of this appendix is not to analyze thoroughly the consequences of central banks’ response to the economic crisis triggered by the pandemic, as it is too early for such a project; the goal is, rather, to focus on the interest rate cuts and to analyze their adequacy in response to the crisis triggered by the global epidemic. Section titled “The nature of the COVID-19 crisis” analyzes the nature of the COVID-19 shock. Section titled “Can interest rate cuts address the cause of the COVID-19 crisis?” examines whether the interest rate cuts can mitigate the crisis. The last section concludes. 

_Appendix 2_ 177 

## **The nature of the COVID-19 crisis** 

The standard justification for lowering interest rates is to support aggregate demand by making investment, consumption, and exports more attractive. However, the crisis resulting from the COVID-19 pandemic is not only a negative demand shock but also a negative supply shock.[1] When the latter type occurs, central banks face a trade- off between curbing inflation and reducing output variability. But some economists argue that when inflation expectations are well anchored or when they fall, as they did in the aftermath of the initial COVID-19 shock (see Figure A2.3), a central bank can reduce the policy rate to stabilize output without sacrificing price stability (Tachibana 2013). 

So the mere fact that aggregate demand or supply declined is not sufficient information on the basis of which to develop an adequate policy response. The declines are only a symptom of underlying economic change. For example, and to simplify the matter, during the Great Depression, aggregate demand declined because commercial banks went bankrupt and the money supply shrank (Friedman and Schwartz 2008). During the Great Recession, aggregate demand decreased because housing prices fell, which reduced household wealth and spending and impaired the assets of the banking system, which limited the supply of loanable funds for investment (Koo 2013; King 2016). 

The COVID-19 crisis is different, so it requires a distinct policy response. COVID-19 is a health crisis. To prevent the spread of the coronavirus, people are staying at home, which is significantly reducing the labor supply and consumer spending. Hence, the COVID-19 shock is neither a normal negative supply shock nor a standard negative demand shock. It differs from the former as it is caused neither by a natural disaster (natural disasters are short- lived) nor by an increase in commodity prices (oil prices plunged in the aftermath of 

**==> picture [323 x 125] intentionally omitted <==**

_Figure A2.3_ Five- year breakeven inflation rate derived from five- year treasury constant maturity securities from March 24, 2019 to March 24, 2020. Source: Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis (2020e), fred.stlouisfed.org, retrieved March 25, 2020. 

178 _Appendix 2_ 

the crisis). This supply shock has been caused by disruptions in supply chains as some factories are not functioning as they lack employees because of the involuntary and voluntary measures introduced to prevent the spread of the disease. 

The COVID-19 crisis differs also from previous negative demand shocks, as it was not caused by decreased money supply nor by a decline in asset prices (although the stock market crash may have amplified the negative demand shock); rather, it was caused by the quarantine and self- quarantine and the subsequent reduced spending. Hence, although there is a risk that the COVID-19 crisis will morph into a financial crisis, so far (as of March 26, 2020) it has been a crisis in the real economy caused by an unprecedented reduction in the labor supply, as workers were put into quarantine or put themselves into self- quarantine. 

## **Can interest rate cuts address the cause of the COVID-19 crisis?** 

The nature of the COVID-19 crisis raises significant doubts about interest rate cuts as a proper tool to address it. The source of the problem is the global epidemic and the resulting higher morbidity and mortality, overburdened health care system, higher employee absenteeism, disturbances in international trade and supply chains, social distancing, and people under quarantine. Interest rate cuts cannot address the source of the problem. 

As Figure A2.1 shows, neither the first nor the second Federal Reserve interest cut (nor the later announcement of additional measures to support the economy) stopped declines in the stock market. Actually, larger- than- usual interest rate cuts decided at unscheduled meetings could fuel investors’ anxiety. They also did not prevent the rise in corporate- bond spreads, as Figure A2.2 shows. 

As the proverb goes, “One can lead a horse to water, but one can’t make it drink.” Indeed, a central bank can make it easy for commercial banks to increase their credit creation and for borrowers to incur new debts, but it cannot compel them to borrow and spend. Hence, interest rate cuts do not help anyone but the government (through lower Treasury yields) in times of pandemic. In such circumstances, consumers do not buy new houses, cars, or durable goods. They do not take out new mortgages. In times of high uncertainty, they hoard cash or stockpile food and personal- hygiene products. 

How can interest rate cuts help today if people are actually encouraged by the authorities to stay at home? They cannot. Nor will investors be encouraged by a lower interest rate to invest more in a time of disrupted supply chains, increased stock market volatility, significant uncertainty about the future economic structure in general and demand for their products in particular, and transformation of production lines for the production of medical equipment. This is a health crisis, not a credit crunch resulting in a collapse in investment demand. Nor will exporters benefit from lower interest rates when supply chains and international trade are disrupted.[2] 

_Appendix 2_ 179 

According to Jonas (2013), the distribution of the economic impact of a flu pandemic is as follows: 12 percent is due to higher mortality, 28 percent is due to lost production due to high worker absenteeism, and 60 percent is due to people’s changes in behavior and the resulting shifts in demand and supply. The interest rate cuts cannot affect any of these sources of pandemic- related economic costs. They can neither reduce mortality nor limit absenteeism nor change people’s behavior. It is actually debatable whether central banks should change people’s behavior at all, as it could put people at a greater risk of infection. 

Some central banks are aware that monetary policy has a limited impact during the pandemic. For example, the Management Board of the Polish Central Bank wrote in a statement on March 16, 2020, that 

changes in interest rates will not prevent supply- side disruptions, nor will they make demand in the economy grow in the short term, but they will reduce the burden resulting from existing obligations, which will provide support for the budgets of firms and households and also reduce the costs of the necessary fiscal measures. 

(NBP Management Board 2020) 

It is true that the interest rate cuts will improve the situation of debtors whose debts are serviced at variable interest rates, but they will do so at the expense of creditors. The mere redistribution is unlikely to support the economy. Moreover, indebted households and companies could be helped by introducing a grace period, postponing credit payments till the period after the epidemic, or even writing off some obligations. There would then be no need to lower the interest rate, which plays a key role in the entire economy, coordinating intertemporal resource allocation and the pricing of time and risk. 

To be clear, I am not downplaying the potential economic consequences of the COVID-19 pandemic and I am not claiming that central banks cannot help at all. I am arguing that lowering interest rates will not help to limit the impact of the pandemic. And if they have already been cut, central banks should not keep them too low for too long, as it could increase macroeconomic imbalances in the future (Taylor 2007). Policy makers should identify and address the fundamental cause of the economic fallout. The economy does not need an interest rate policy, as high borrowing costs and low asset prices are not the root problems. Nor do we need a monetary stimulus to increase aggregate demand, as the output gap is not the main issue. Rather, social distancing and trade disruptions have reduced potential output. Many consumers and investors are unable to spend money because of quarantine or disrupted supply chains. Instead, the main threat to the economy is that companies and households will be unable to meet their payment obligations, which could lead to mass bankruptcies, a high unemployment rate, and a financial crisis. This is why we need to lay the foundation for a quick and sustainable recovery by making the economy as flexible as possible, and to support liquidity, rather than a standard macroeconomic response. 

180 _Appendix 2_ 

## **Conclusion** 

The COVID-19 pandemic has resulted in both demand and supply shocks. However, policy makers should not merely focus on those shocks but should investigate their underlying causes. The current economic crisis stems from a health crisis and the reasonable actions of individuals responding to it. It is not a crisis that has resulted from weakening of animal spirits, banks’ bankruptcy, a credit crunch, or lack of confidence in financial markets. Interest rate cuts thus cannot mitigate the economic crisis as that crisis will not end as long as the health crisis continues. Since ultralow interest rates did not help to revive unusually weak economic growth after the Great Recession, as I have shown in this book, they will not help much now. Central banks should not respond automatically as in previous crises, with tools that are inadequate to solve the health crisis and can only lead to some unintended negative consequences in the long run that I have analyzed thoroughly in this book.
